0,2026-11-18,"Your browser does not support the video element.
Your browser does not support the video element.
Amanda McDormand's cover letter opened with a bold pitch.
""This isn't a job application—it's an idea, and I'll get straight to it. I'm a corporate fixer. If you've watched Scandal, think Olivia Pope. If you're more of a Ray Donovan person, that works too. And if you're into music, I like to consider myself the Taylor Swift of business.""
It's one of several Hail Mary strategies 43-year-old McDormand tried when entering an unfamiliar landscape after being laid off from Comcast in December. In 2019, the New-Hampshire based senior leader had been turning down offers while exploring new job opportunities. This time, her LinkedIn feed was inundated with posts about job seekers who'd sent hundreds of applications that went unanswered over years-long searches. Still, she wasn't prepared for her inbox to be flooded with auto rejections.
With a few months of severance left, McDormand decided she had some time to experiment — and not much to lose.
She tried the direct route with an ""I am not AI"" bit in her cover letter. In another, she wove in golf references for a hiring manager who liked the sport. Her experiments, indeed, led to more callbacks, but her first big win — which resulted in a fractional role before her current full-time job — was a cover letter laced with Taylor Swift lyrics. She described ""identifying the blank space"" within an organization, told the employer they've ""seen this film before, and it's time to change the ending,"" and closed by saying, ""This could be our era, just say 'yes'.
As AI slop crowds the job market, applicants are taking drastic measures to show hiring managers they’re human — and nothing is off the table. Beyond cover-letter riddles, recruiters say they’ve seen a resurgence of résumé interest sections with odd details and more candid personal statements. Some include fun-fact infographics. Others may be purposefully including punctuation or spelling errors to signal they wrote it themselves.
Carly Martinetti, cofounder of PR and AI visibility firm Notably, said that one job seeker mentioned in her LinkedIn reach-out that she's ""been an in-house girly my whole career."" That's ""totally not something AI would say,"" Martinetti said, adding that she found the message down-to-Earth.
Among the entries recruiters and candidates shared with me: ""buffalo chicken wraps,"" the legal drama ""Suits,"" ""vampire and Robert Pattinson movies,"" ""clowning,"" ""mermaiding,"" ""lucid dreaming,"" ""love of french fries,"" ""superhero,"" and ""movie theme dinner party thrower.""
The effort comes at a time when over 84% of job seekers use AI to write or edit their résumés, according to recent survey data from Ladders. The report also found that 76% of 237 surveyed job seekers have taken at least one deliberate step to sound less like AI.
Before AI, Ladders' founder and CEO Marc Cenedella says he ""rarely saw people adding personal anecdotes to their résumés. It was almost a faux pas.""
Nowadays, Cenedella says, candidates are left wondering: ""How do I signal effectively to people that I'm not a machine?""
Job seekers have long found a way to flex on their résumés by including marathon training, freelance work, or volunteering under a section labeled ""hobbies,"" ""skills,"" or ""extracurriculars."" Cover letters similarly gave applicants space to expand on accomplishments, explain a career pivot, or reveal a bit more about themselves.
In a crowded job market, those extra details are showing up in stranger places — and sometimes taking on a more unconventional, and cringey form. Applicants who have spent months sending out résumés with little response said they eventually adopted a ""screw it"" mentality. Some have written sob-story cover letters and posted them on social media to share their frustration.
One Reddit user shared a cover letter in a post titled ""The cover letter I finally had the balls to send, because I have nothing to lose,"" which opened with recounting their various unanswered applications to the company. If a niche confession catches a hiring manager's eye, some applicants figure it may be worth a little embarrassment.

The move is also a response to the sheer volume of applications recruiters now have to sort through. A 2026 Greenhouse analysis found that companies receive an average of 194 applications per hire. AI has made the process of sifting through hundreds of résumés even more monotonous. Many recruiters are growing tired of reading perfectly tailored résumés that pull exact language from the job description, and 300-word AI-polished notes that all sound vaguely similar.
""When you've been looking at résumés for an hour, you start to get really picky,"" said Kristin Raiford, an executive recruiter who previously led executive search for Amazon ads and finance. ""You're like, what's different about this person from the last person? Why do I pick them and not them?""
The same Greenhouse report said that 3.2% of applications reach a first interview. Listing college football as a hobby may not be enough to set you apart from the masses these days. However, claiming to know the first 100 digits of pi may be just enough to make a hiring manager pause. Kelli Hrivnak, a tech and marketing recruiter, still remembers a résumé submitted years ago from a candidate who claimed just that. The person wasn't hired, and the fact didn't change her view of their qualifications — but it stuck with her.
McDormand recently received 220 applications for a role she posted just days earlier. The candidates getting her attention are the ones who give a recruiter something to remember, weird or not.
""After you've seen it like a dozen times, you're like, 'I don't know who this person is. This person is not giving me anything,'"" McDormand said, adding that she appreciates the unconventional approach.
Sharing a quirky interest or hobby offers a two-for-one advantage for some applicants. It can jolt a hiring manager out of résumé fatigue — and also give the candidate an easy transition into talking about their skill set in an interview.
Kelsey Anderson, a graduate student now applying for jobs, lists ""Julia Child"" in her résumé's interest section. It can lead to a straightforward conversation about cooking — or help her transition to a deeper topic. For example, she could discuss American food dynamics, Child's love of travel, her late-career pivot, or her feminist advocacy.
For others, hobbies reflect their skill set. Paige Maly, who works in marketing, said she got more callbacks than ever after adding visual scale of five of her wildest adventures to her résumé.
She said that section became a conversation starter in every interview. For Maly, it was more than a stunt. It was proof of the qualities she believes marketing leaders want: independence, decisiveness, and a willingness to take risks.
""I'm OK with making moves,"" Maly said.
All of these tactics are ultimately a gamble, even if they pay off. While a bold move could result in a job offer, it could also turn off the recruiter. Some hiring managers may appreciate the added flair, but others prefer résumés to remain strictly professional.
Erica Keswin, a former executive recruiter and bestselling workplace strategy and human leadership author, said that including wild details could ""really backfire"" with the wrong crowd. Creative industries might be more receptive to this approach — but old-school law firms or consulting giants might not be as keen on eccentric confessions. Keswin said that including a unique hobby can be a smart move to humanize your application, but she wouldn't recommend tacking on an especially odd confession just because it worked out for someone else.
""I wouldn't always say, 'plug and play,'"" Keswin said. ""Because if it doesn't feel like you, and someone asks you about it, it's going to have the opposite effect.""
For McDormand, a self-described ""Swiftie for life,"" it was genuine, although she said she researched the companies before sending applications to decide whether to go the traditional or creative route. McDormand said weaving song lyrics into her application gave her an opening in an interview to spend 10 minutes discussing Swift's marketing playbook and how she used similar tactics to set herself apart. She said the ability to connect pop culture into meaningful strategy was both effective and memorable.
Those conversations are what made the biggest impact in actually landing a job, she said.
""It's a technique to get in the door,"" McDormand says, ""But once you get in the door, you have to have the wherewithal to be able to back up the conversation.""

1,2026-11-18,"Brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei are chief executive and president of Anthropic
In the space of just five years, Anthropic has gone from unknown start-up to tech giant, worth more than a trillion dollars.
The company behind the AI platform Claude was formed by seven people who left rival OpenAI in 2021 and, in a matter of hours, will become multi-billionaires - on paper at least.
Anthropic is floating the business on the US stock exchange and will sell its shares at $83 each, it said on Wednesday. 
At that price, the founders' shareholdings will be worth at least $19.3bn (Â£14.7bn) each.
So, who are these people, all in their thirties and forties, who are about to become incredibly wealthy?
In 2020, Dario Amodei was working for OpenAI, the firm that brought generative artificial intelligence to a mainstream audience, when it launched Chat-GPT. 
He decided he wanted to do things his own way, and left, taking his sister Daniela, and five colleagues with him.
Amodei grew up in San Francisco, impressing school friends not just with his academic prowess but also his speed on the running track, and his preoccupation with philosophical and technological questions including the potential of machines to gain consciousness and human longevity.
Initially he studied physics at Princeton and Stanford. But following his father's death from hepatitis C, he switched to biology and biomedical sciences.
Amodei did stints at the Chinese tech giant Baidu and Google before joining OpenAI.
But he began to worry that some people within the company were putting profit before safety, prompting the decision to jump ship.
Now 43, he is Anthropic's chief executive, and the person who, in his sister's words, makes the company ""ambitious"".
Listen: Dario Amodei BBC Sounds profile
Dario and Daniela Amodei, were close growing up and later shared a flat together in San Francisco with other friends. She followed him into OpenAI and joined him when he left to found Anthropic.
She and her brother have always genuinely liked each other, she says, but the only thing she ever did first, was when he followed her in becoming vegetarian.
Amodei studied English Literature at the University of California Santa Cruz, going on to work for Democrat representative Matt Cartwright.
She has also worked at financial services platform Stripe, where she was head of risk operations.
The 39-year-old is president of Anthropic, manages the executive team, and leads the firm's research, engineering, product, governance and commercial work.
Tom Brown, now 39, followed the Amodei siblings when they left to found their new venture.
He led GPT-3 engineering at OpenAI, and prior to that was at Google on its DeepMind project.
Brown established his entrepreneurial credentials co-founding an app called ""Grouper"" that helps set up drinks between groups of friends.
He has supported US President Donald Trump's stance on datacentres, arguing that they are ""an enormous source of prosperity"".
Now as chief compute officer at Anthropic he is responsible for how the company secures and uses its computing resources.
Jack Clark, is another arts graduate among the original start-up's founding members and the only one who is not from North America.
Originally from Brighton in the UK, he studied English Literature and Creative Writing at the University of East Anglia and became a technology journalist, writing for publications including ZDNet UK, The Register, and Bloomberg.
He was caught up in the excitement of artificial intelligence and joined OpenAI, where he worked initially in communications, later becoming its policy director.
Now, 37, Clark is head of public benefit at Anthropic. He also leads the Anthropic Institute, an AI think tank.
Jared Kaplan, 42, is chief science officer at Anthropic, and heads its technical research. He is also an associate professor at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore.
He holds a PhD in physics from Harvard University.
His work on scaling laws - which describe mathematical patterns that show that bigger AI models trained on more data tend to be more consistent - has helped provide AI firms with a framework for predicting AI behaviour, according to Anthropic.
Chris Olah is described as ""interpretability research lead"" primarily focused on how AI models work with each other.
But according to the New York Times, Olah has spent much of the last year convening meetings with faith leaders from across the religious spectrum. 
Described by the newspaper as ""boyish"" but with ""the zeal of an evangelist"" the 34-year-old is reportedly exploring the relationship between morality and AI.
He previously led interoperability research at OpenAI, and prior to that was a research scientist at Google Brain. 
The Canadian left the University of Toronto early, but later secured a Thiel Fellowship of $100,000, which is a scholarship to help gifted people move into research without a degree.
Sam McCandlish, 36, takes less of a public-facing role than the other founders, but like them will own around 1.6% of the company after its shares go on sale. As a result he too will see his stake crystallise into a hugely valuable asset.
McCandlish is Anthropic's chief architect, and helps shape and implement the company's policy designed to mitigate the risks of AI. Prior to this he worked on safety at OpenAI.
He has a physics PhD from Stanford, and maths and physics degree from Brandeis University outside Boston.

2,2026-11-18,"Thursday is supposed to be a celebration of AI.Anthropic (ANT) is heading toward its long-awaited IPO at the Nasdaq, potentially giving Wall Street one of the biggest new pure-play AI investment stories in years. Before you hit me up on X, Elon Musk-led Space…"
3,2026-11-18,"Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has quickly become a big name in the tech industry
Anthropic has seen its value jump to as much as $1.5tn (Â£1.14tn) as its shares are set to start trading on the stock market on Thursday.
The artificial intelligence (AI) company behind the popular chatbot Claude said its shares will be priced at $83 each when it lists on the Nasdaq, a tech-focused stock exchange based in New York.
That will make it one of the most valuable companies in the world.
Anthropic's stock market debut will make it the first US firm focused exclusively on AI to go public. It will also mark a crucial test for the fast-growing industry, which faces questions over whether the huge amounts of money being poured into the technology will be profitable. 
The firm said earlier this month it would offer its shares for between $70 and $78 each, which valued the company at up to $1.3tn.
But demand for Anthropic's stock debut - commonly referred to as its initial public offering, or IPO - is very high, according to one early investor in the company who spoke with the BBC on condition of anonymity.
The person said shares were more than 10 times oversubscribed ahead of the public listing, meaning demand for Anthropic shares exceeded the number of shares available.
If that turns out to be the case, Anthropic's valuation could quickly blow past its target on Thursday when trading begins.
Depending on how strong that demand is, Anthropic could even exceed the early market valuation of Elon Musk's SpaceX, which was the world's most highly valued stock debut when it went public with a value of $2.2tn in June.
However, in the weeks that followed, SpaceX shares fell significantly in price.
As it stands, Anthropic's listing will see the firm raise more than $68bn from its initial investors. It is also expecting another $26bn from private placements. So the total amount the company will raise is expected to be $94bn.
The amount of cash Anthropic has raised from its listing is already more than SpaceX raised in June.
OpenAI, the maker of ChatGPT and Anthropic's biggest AI rival, is expected to go public next year.
Until then, Anthropic will be the only public company in the US that is entirely focused on the technology, or one that was founded and built around AI tools and products. Its scope is different from tech giants like Google and Microsoft that also develop AI but are involved in other tech services.
The seven Anthropic founders who are about to become multi-billionaires
There is excitement around Anthropic's listing, but also some concern from investors around its ability to generate revenue and profits going forward.
Yang Wang, a principle financial analyst with Counterpoint Research, said the central issue for Anthropic is whether its business ""can keep pace"" as hundreds of billions of dollars in financial obligations come due in the next couple of years. 
""Nevertheless, it is not an everyday occurrence for public investors to gain direct exposure to a frontier-model developer with the potential to shape the next stage of productivity,"" Wang said.
While nearly every other major tech company is pouring huge amounts of money into the technology and infrastructure required for their own AI tools, they are built around social media or online retail or online search. AI tools and applications are secondary.
""Anthropic going public gives us a whole new window into what AI demand looks like,"" Marta Norton, who heads strategy at investment firm Empower, told the BBC.
Anthropic was started by a group of co-founders, led by siblings Dario Amodei and Daniela Amodei, all of whom left their work at OpenAI to start a new AI firm.
The company has carved itself out in the ongoing AI race as more focused on building the technology with safety top of mind.
It refused to comply with a demand by the US Department of Defense that Anthropic's tools be used in an essentially unrestricted way. 
CEO Dario Amodei has been outspoken in his belief that AI tools may soon be so powerful that they present a threat to human existence.
The company has urged developers to slow the development of AI, arguing that advanced systems could pose ""catastrophic"" risks to humanity if they are not handled with appropriate safeguards.
Rival firm OpenAI has also voiced similar concerns about the technology's risks and delayed its plans to list on the stock market to address AI safety concerns. 
Nonetheless, Anthropic is looking to grow quickly and gain more access to financing through the public markets in order to do so.
Claude is Anthropic's widely-used AI chatbot
Norton said Anthropic going public now, in a time of market volatility comes down to the simple fact that the company needs access to more money.
""AI is financially demanding,"" Norton said. ""The public markets open up the financial spigot a little bit more, and so the time has come.""
But broader anxieties have started to grow about the AI industry's business model, which is reliant on expensive computer chips that are in short supply. The technology is also dependent on controversial data centres and funding such operations require deals with other tech companies.
The Federal Reserve Bank has expressed concern about the rapid growth of AI-related debt being financed through ""complex and opaque"" structures, according to minutes from its October meeting to discuss a potential rate rise.
A few of the meeting's participants also noted that ""AI-related exposures were becoming increasingly concentrated among a small number of firms"".

4,2026-11-18,"This summer, OpenAI paused its largest planned frontier training run after its AI agents accidentally hacked Hugging Face and early evidence suggested that its next model could independently find and exploit security flaws. In September, Anthropic CEO Dario A…"
5,2026-11-18,"Author of the AI Insider newsletter
Nvidia's board and interim CEO did just about enough late Tuesday to keep the growing army of Jensen Huang supporters at bay. At least for a few hours.
""A clean quarter and guide strengthen the board's hand but do not close the question,"" Dan Ives, a tech analyst at Yorkville Ives & Co., wrote in a note to investors after the AI chip giant reported results.
I listened to Nvidia's earnings conference call, and I thought interim CEO Colette Kress and board chair Harvey Jones performed well under extraordinary circumstances.
Jones came out swinging. He said the board had no intention of reinstating Huang and made it clear that questions about the ousted CEO were off-limits.
Nvidia shares fell after those comments.
Then Kress quickly delivered some welcome news. Nvidia raised its revenue growth forecast for fiscal 2028. She also pushed back against concerns that the company might have to cut prices to compete, and said profit margins would recover after dipping in the current quarter.
The stock rebounded in after-hours trading. But on Wednesday, Nvidia shares continued to fall.
That's not good for Nvidia board and the interim leadership.
""I believe the @nvidia board is not acting in the best interest of shareholders nor in a manner consistent with their fiduciary duties,"" billionaire activist investor Bill Ackman said on X on Wednesday.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Jones, the board chair, said Nvidia would consider internal and external candidates for its next CEO.
That brings me to the most intriguing moment of the call.
As the question-and-answer session began, Nvidia announced that Ian Buck, VP of hyperscale and high-performance computing, would join Kress to answer analysts' questions.
This was a big deal.
Buck is a serious technologist who helped invent CUDA, the software that makes Nvidia's chips so useful for AI. He's also spent more than two decades at the company and has close relationships with its biggest customers.
Business Insider has a great profile of Buck, who has emerged as a potential successor to Huang.
""His hat is in the ring,"" Ives said when asked about Buck's appearance on the call.
Now Nvidia appeared to have an almost perfect setup. The board had ruled out Huang's return, Kress had reassured Wall Street about the business, and a respected internal technical leader was being introduced to investors.
Awesome.
Except Buck proceeded to say absolutely nothing.
Not a word.
I kept waiting for an analyst to ask him a question. Nobody did. And Buck never volunteered anything.
That was soooo weird.
If you're being floated as a possible CEO of one of the world's most important companies, and you get a chance to address Wall Street during a leadership crisis, surely you say something?
Maybe Nvidia wants the strong, silent type as its next CEO. (Huang, as we've learned recently, hasn't exactly been the silent type).
Joking aside, if I were a serious Nvidia investor, I would have wanted to hear from Buck.
""It was odd, but then again this situation is all odd,"" Ives told me after the call.
Meanwhile, Huang still has powerful, wealthy supporters who aren't going away.
With continued declines on Wednesday, Nvidia shares are well below their pre-Huang level — not a great look for the board.
""His return to Nvidia is in all of our best interest,"" Shopify CEO Tobi Lutke wrote on X after Tuesday's results. He added that there could never be a true replacement for a founder like Huang.
Ives agrees. ""The 15% special meeting threshold, activist pressure, and the Commerce Department's position on the export investigation remain the next steps in the saga,"" he wrote.
Ackman has already explained how shareholders could pressure Nvidia's board to bring Huang back.
Under Nvidia's bylaws, shareholders representing at least 15% of the company's voting power can demand a special shareholder meeting, provided they've held their shares for at least a year.
They could then seek to replace directors with people who support Huang's reinstatement. A new board could then reappoint him CEO.
That's a formidable challenge. Removing directors without cause requires support from two-thirds of Nvidia's outstanding voting shares, according to the company's bylaws.
And 15% of Nvidia, at current valuations, represents more than $500 billion in stock. Huang owns roughly 3.5%, leaving supporters needing another 11.5 percentage points.
Who has that much money?
Only some of the world's largest investment funds and sovereign wealth funds.
Oh, and Elon Musk.

9,2026-11-18,"Alan Milburn's proposals to help young people who are neither learning nor earning would reduce benefits for some young people but aim to get them jobs in the long run
The prime minister describes Alan Milburn's report on jobs, training and benefits for young people as a ""watershed moment"".
In so doing, he is accepting Milburn's case for change and taking on the task of delivering it. Were ministers to actually manage that, it would amount to one of the most significant shake-ups of the welfare state in its history.
But the proposed timeframe for this stretches to a decade, which is another geological era in political terms, at least two general elections away.
Milburn is a deft operator. From a round of morning television and radio interviews, to addressing the cabinet, to releasing his report into the wild, he was still out and about on Tuesday evening in Westminster persuading, charming and cajoling those possibly in need of all three.
And this former MP, still every inch the politician, has an ongoing role - appointed the chairman of the government's new Youth Opportunities Unit.
The new job, which will report directly to Andy Burnham, is about tying together the myriad different aspects of change he is suggesting, involving multiple government departments.
And yes, it does mean a 68-year-old bloke becomes an early beneficiary of new employment prospects for the young.
But that glib observation apart, there is a hope that Milburn has gifted Labour MPs a moral argument for welfare reforms they can endorse. Or, to put it more bluntly, one not grounded in a specific objective to make benefits cuts to make the sums add up elsewhere.
Plus, him hanging around perhaps means he can be a useful lightning conductor for any forthcoming angst on Labour's benches â as having persuaded them of the big picture mission, the gritty reality of implementing it begins to take shape.
Burnham promises to 'move quickly' on Neets report
Watch: What's in the Milburn review?
At a glance: Key proposals from Milburn's Neets report
The young people caught up in Britain's Neets crisis
As the prime minister puts it in an interview in the Times, external, the government wants to offer ""a new social contract"" for ""the let-down generation"".
And he tells the newspaper that the initial upfront cost of around Â£500m, for employment support in 2027-28, will come from existing government budgets, which will mean cuts to other things elsewhere in government. 
""I've asked the cabinet to reprioritise,"" he said.
Crucially, if you are in Scotland, Wales or Northern Ireland, many of the decisions around whether or not to press ahead with these ideas will be up to the devolved governments in Edinburgh, Cardiff and Belfast.
From the government at Westminster's perspective, the focus now will be drawing up the specific plans for implementing Milburn's ideas, having accepted the broad thrust of his argument.
A white paper will follow in the new year, and then legislation.
What we don't know yet and will be crucial to the next stage of this debate, is precisely who will be the perceived winners and losers, at least in the short term, of the proposed changes.
Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden told me that ""those with the most severe and lifelong disabilities where work is not a realistic prospect"" should, as Milburn suggests, ""get more support than they receive at the moment"". But how will those in this category be defined?
McFadden also told me that for some of those facing ""extra costs"" associated with their circumstances ""there will be an extra cost benefit that will make at least a contribution towards that"". 
Again, how will those in this category be defined?
There is too an emerging political argument, following remarks from Alan Milburn already seized upon by the Conservatives, that decisions taken by Labour in its first few years in power have made a difficult situation harder still for young people looking for work.
On page 66 of his report, external, Milburn says the Employment Rights Act 2025 ""could have major implications at a time when the youth labour market is already weak"".
Interestingly, on this point, the prime minister is willing to push back, telling BBC Breakfast that ""there is a balance to be struck"". 
""It is important to consider on the other side of this that the more you strengthen employment security, that can create more durable jobs that young people are more likely to stay in,"" he says. ""We're going to consider all of this in the round.""
The big decisions, then, now sit on the prime minister and the work and pensions secretary's desks.
In accepting the magnitude of Milburn's diagnosis and proposed treatment, they are tacitly accepting too that it is their responsibility to deliver â or at least begin to deliver â on much of what he suggests.
Their success or otherwise will matter to this generation of young people and those that will follow â and to the sustainability of the benefits system in the years ahead.

10,2026-11-18,"Author of the CMO Insider newsletter
Chris Koehler, CMO of cloud communications platform Twilio, is redesigning his marketing team for the AI era.
Koehler is shifting from a model built around a team of ""95%"" narrow specialists toward broader, more cross-functional marketers who oversee projects from start to finish, with AI taking on more tasks such as research, localization, and content creation.
The goal: fewer handoffs and higher output, while keeping head count flat.
""AI has simply been a game changer,"" Koehler told me.
Koehler joins a growing group of CMOs restructuring their teams as AI takes on repetitive, data-driven tasks. Some CMOs are reshaping around ""full-stack marketers"" or ""marketing engineers."" For Twilio, it's ""orchestrators.""
Koehler said it's enabled the company to stay nimble and condense time-intensive work, while demonstrating a return on investment, such as increasing the number of campaigns it produces and reducing the hours spent on projects.
In the global campaigns team, for example, Twilio has created ""pods"" led by a single campaign strategist focused on a priority, such as delivering a campaign targeting a key customer persona. That strategist works with a small pod of cross-functional marketers to execute against the priority. Koehler estimated the pods will make up no more than 20% of the total marketing organization.
""Our intent is for these pods to remain laser-focused on our important bets, while the team remains focused on other, critical 'business as usual' activities,"" Koehler said. ""This enables us to experiment and adjust without risking what has been working for us.""
Koehler said the campaign team's use of AI tools has condensed weeks of account research and personalized content creation into hours, and increased output to 15 account-based campaigns per quarter, up from three previously. Koehler said these campaigns performed ""just as well as our usual campaigns.""

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Elsewhere, the go-to-market communications team has been deploying AI agents to build campaigns in 10 minutes that used to take human-only teams two hours to prepare and send. Koehler said customers who engaged with its AI agent, ISA, were three times more likely to convert than those who did not, which helped contribute to the over-30% growth in self-serve revenue it reported in its third-quarter earnings.
Koehler said the creative team has become more efficient by using built-in AI features in platforms such as Adobe, CapCut, and Canva. It's also testing newer AI startups, such as video platform Capsule, which can generate videos from a single text prompt.
These experiments haven't always gone to plan. Koehler said when asked to localize a campaign for the French market, one AI tool attempted to put berets on all the photos.
""There's still a human in the loop in all this stuff,"" Koehler said. ""The reality is, it's getting better and better, and the more you reinforce it, train it, and put the effort in, you get better output.""
Koehler said he's currently figuring out how the marketing team's reorganization will affect job descriptions, job codes, and the management hierarchy. Asked whether it could lead to a reduction in roles, Koehler said the plan is to keep its overall head count ""about the same"" next year.
Twilio's companywide workforce has also remained broadly flat. Its reported head count rose about 1.5% year over year through the quarter ended September 30, while revenue and gross profit each increased by 20%.
Twilio joins a raft of companies reconfiguring their marketing departments as AI tools take on an increasing number of tasks. Wix CMO Omer Shai told CMO Insider earlier this year he's deploying AI and ""super agents"" and replacing specific roles with ""full-stack marketers."" Klarna CMO David Sandström said he's looking to hire ""marketing engineers"" as his department goes all in on AI. Publicis Sapient CMO Teresa Barreira said earlier this year that 80% to 90% of the daily tasks her team performs can be accelerated or done entirely by AI, and created new job descriptions focused on the functions that matter.
A recent survey of 194 business-to-business marketers by the B2B marketer network CMO Huddles and the research firm Benchmarkit found a gap between respondents who use AI for individual functions and those who integrate it into how their teams operate. While 98% of marketers surveyed said they used AI in at least one marketing function, only 3% said they had coordinated it across their go-to-market teams and processes.
Drew Neisser, CEO of CMO Huddles, said it's important for marketers to demonstrate how AI can drive business growth, rather than simply pointing to productivity gains. Otherwise, he said, AI risks reinforcing the perception that marketing is a commodity that can be outsourced or cut as companies' AI costs rise.
""The thing you can't lose sight of is, 'How is AI reinforcing differentiation?'"" and, ""'How is it increasing the exposure of the organization?'"" Neisser said.

Writer
Writer
November 18, 2026, 2:31 pm ET
The Disney Plus and Hulu (with Ads) bundle, which is regularly priced at $13 a month, is now available at no additional cost to subscribers of Fubo’s Pro and Elite plans – priced at $89 and $100 a month, respectively. The news was announced during the company’s earnings call on Tuesday. It’s the latest subscription option to launch on the platform since the companies completed the merger of Hulu’s live TV streaming service and Fubo.
Moving forward, Fubo customers will be able to watch original programming from Disney Plus and Hulu, blockbuster movies and have access to the deep library of TV shows and films available on both platforms. The timing is right if you want to catch up on Marvel ahead of Avengers: Doomsday, or watch shows like Percy Jackson and the Olympians or ABC’s Abbott Elementary. Sports content available on Fubo is extensive, and eligible subscribers already have access to ESPN Unlimited and Fox One for free.
During the earnings call, Alisa Bowen, the new CEO of Fubo (who used to run Disney Plus), discussed the strategy to meld the two offerings into one cohesive fan experience.
More from CNET
“Our strategy review reinforced that our core value proposition remains serving sports fans on Fubo and entertainment fans on Hulu Plus Live TV,” she said. “With profitable growth as our North Star, we are sharpening how we market the full breadth of both services, offering choice across price points, and investing in product innovation, particularly mobile, to deepen engagement.”
Fubo and Disney-owned Hulu plus Live TV first shared news of a new agreement to combine its businesses in January 2025, and the deal was approved by the Department of Justice and finalized in October 2025.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator, at Semafor's ""Silicon Valley & The World"" event on Tuesday, hinted that he wasn't overly concerned about how Huang's departure from Nvidia would affect CoreWeave's business.
He said that Nvidia has been an ""extraordinary company,"" but it also employs ""thousands and thousands of amazing engineers and employees"" who would continue their partnership.
""Those thousands of people integrate with CoreWeave every day to work on how you deploy the equipment, how you build the data centers, how you network the infrastructure, all of these things, and you know, that continues,"" Intrator added.
The moderator asked if Huang's absence — even if just emotionally — would be felt when it came to accessing credit for data center infrastructure. Huang was a huge backer of neocloud companies like CoreWeave.
Intrator said that Huang was a visionary who supported not just Nvidia but the data center and AI ecosystem. But the reason Huang propped the ecosystem up was because these elements of the supply chain ""were needed and they will continue to be needed, and they will continue to be supported by Nvidia and by the balance sheet that they represent,"" he said. 
Intrator's comments came after a tumultuous week for Nvidia, where Huang resigned after multiple allegations emerged over his extramarital affairs. 
But the pessimism didn't extend to Nvidia's earnings call on Tuesday, where the chipmaker reported revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year earlier. Nvidia's stock price was up more than 4% in after-hours trading, and Nvidia's leadership said it wasn't considering bringing Huang back.
Neoclouds — cloud computing services specifically for AI customers — made headlines in October for their high debt levels to build AI capacity, but the returns on that investment remain unclear. Investors were spooked by the uncertainty, and their stocks took a major hit.

13,2026-11-18,"Anthropic is set for a valuation well above $1 trillion.
Anthropic announced on Wednesday it will sell stock at a price of $83 per share.
The company behind popular chatbot Claude is expected to go public on Thursday at a valuation north of $1 trillion, which would immediately vault it into rarefied air among some of the largest public companies in the world.
Anthropic will trade under the ticker symbol ""ANT.""
The price per share is above a previously expected range of $70 to $78 and means the company will raise $81 billion in the IPO with a valuation of about $1.5 trillion.
The IPO will pull in fresh funds for the San Francisco-based firm. It aims to compete in a potentially lucrative but cost-intensive section of the AI industry centered on advanced models and the tools they empower.
The company significantly expanded its revenue at the outset of 2026, but it continued to suffer losses.
Anthropic earned $16.3 billion in revenue over the first six months of this year, growing nearly 14-fold from the same period a year earlier, according to a government filing.
Still, the company reported a net loss of $80.7 billion over the first six months of this year, indicating the scale of its spending over that period.
Anthropic will all but certainly become a part of people's 401(k) accounts soon. Those accounts often hold index funds, which are pegged to major indexes like the Nasdaq.
Until recently, newly listed companies were barred from major indexes until after an extended waiting period. But the Nasdaq issued a rule change in May permitting ""fast entry"" to the Nasdaq-100 for some major IPOs. Over the ensuing weeks, some other top exchanges also tweaked their rules, though their eligibility requirements vary.
In the run-up to its IPO, Anthropic touted AI as a transformational and lucrative technology, while warning about possible catastrophic risk that could bring financial fallout for shareholders.
A financial filing last month presents AI as ""one of the most consequential technologies in human history. If managed well, it could rapidly accelerate scientific innovation, greatly increase economic growth rates, bring cures for major diseases within reach, and solve problems that have resisted generations of human effort.""
In the same document, Anthropic warned of the possibility its AI models could pose a ""catastrophic or existential risk to humanity.""
Investing in the company's stock ""involves a high degree of risk,"" Anthropic added.

An audiophile's best friend

About four years ago, I was anointed with a pair of what were the best wireless headphones at the time, and all this time later, my Sony WH-1000 XM5s are still going strong, and I've never looked back.
Now that we're creeping even closer to Black Friday season, I cannot stress enough how perfect an opportunity you have to get one at a discounted rate. As it stands, the best Black Friday 2026 deal I've come across is at Best Buy, where Sony's WH-1000 XM5 headphones are now $299.99 down from $398.
And yes, Black Friday is still a week away, but stock is much higher during early sales, and if you're not one to wait for the possibility that a better deal will arise, it's best to act sooner rather than later.

If you're an audio novice and the price tag still scares you, allow me to reassure you.
As a beginner, you may think that a cheaper pair of headphones with standard audio features will be enough to get you by. And yes, there are plenty of budget-friendly alternatives with above-average performance, but I was also a newbie, and I can confidently say that introducing yourself to audio tech through a brand like Sony will go such a long way.
Sure, $229.99 is still a lot of money even when it's a deal, but once you take the plunge, you won't have to spend this money for a good few years before the headphones start to show signs of wear and tear. I'm four years in, and the audio output is still as punchy and bold as it was when I first purchased them.
Speaking of audio, the Sony WH-1000 XM5 headphones pack a versatile listening experience, bringing out the very best of lossless and hi-res music streaming. Its Active Noise Cancellation succeeds at drowning out pretty much everything around you, making them perfect for noisy commutes. At the same time, the Ambient Sound setting is useful for letting in outside noise while you listen to music, which you can easily enable and disable via the button on the left ear pad.
If there's anything to note, it would be the lack of collapsible ear pads, which are a fan-favorite feature from models past (it just makes it a lot easier for travelling). The Platinum Silver version is also prone to discoloring, so Black is probably the better shade to opt for, but other than that, they've presented zero disappointments.

While Nvidia and Anthropic are under the spotlight, OpenAI and Microsoft dropped several new models this week.
Reporter 2
Reporter 2

				Edited by Jon Reed 
November 18, 2026, 11:28 am ET
It has been a wild week for AI, and it’s only Wednesday. Aside from outages, cheating scandals and upcoming IPOs, there are several new models available now. Microsoft dropped seven new versions of its MAI models at its Ignite conference, and OpenAI released GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol, the next step forward for its flagship models.
First, Microsoft: At its Ignite developer conference on Tuesday, Microsoft introduced seven new AI models. They include MAI Cyber 2 Flash, MAI Code 2, MAI Image 2.7 and Dragon Copilot Transcribe — models meant to help with tasks in cybersecurity, coding, image generation and transcription. 
It’s clear from Microsoft’s marketing that it is aiming to position its new tech as a cheaper alternative to OpenAI’s and Anthropic’s leading models, with many of these significantly cheaper to run at the enterprise level. That, plus its new licensed weight program, which lets companies customize the new AI models for their purposes, highlights how Microsoft is continuing to pitch its AI tech to the workplace.
More from CNET
Next, OpenAI: The ChatGPT maker dropped two new model variants of its flagship model, GPT-6.2 Astra and GPT-6.2 Sol. Astra is the big model — it costs the most and uses the most firepower to handle complex tasks — and Sol is the “everyday” version. Both models beat their sibling models in OpenAI’s benchmarking. These are available to paying subscribers in ChatGPT and Codex globally now. (Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)
While these new models might be useful for AI power users, they are far from most of the AI industry’s mind this week. During Tuesday’s earnings call, new interim Nvidia CEO Colette Kress tried to assure investors that everything was “business as usual” after weeks of turmoil inside the company rocked the global economy. Nvidia cofounder and now-former CEO Jensen Huang’s multiple extramarital affairs, including one with a woman suspected of ties to Chinese intelligence, threw the company into chaos and have attracted the attention of the Securities and Exchange Commission and both chambers of Congress, where Huang has agreed to testify.
And let’s not forget about Anthropic, the Claude maker plans to become a public company this week, with the possible biggest-ever IPO slated for Thursday. Anthropic’s reportedly seeking an opening price of $78 per share, for a total valuation of $1.4 trillion — narrowly beating the other largest-ever IPO earlier this year, Elon’s Musk mega-conglomerate, SpaceX (including now SpaceXSI, because President Trump said so).
The Nvidia scandal caused a drastic 14% dip in its stock price during its first week and wiped out $1 trillion in the company’s value, but Anthropic investors don’t seem to be worried. At least, not publicly.
If nothing else, Anthropic’s IPO and the new models from OpenAI and Microsoft are stark reminders that the tech industry is nowhere near ready to slow down on AI. Even as financial, environmental and legal concerns mount — and arguably hit a fever pitch during the midterm elections — Big Tech is plowing ahead with generative AI.

18,2026-11-18,"The PlayStation 5 Pro is a great current-gen console — one that's been in high demand for playing Rockstar's ""Grand Theft Auto 6."" Unfortunately, demand is so high that supply has become limited, making the console currently cost much more than its regular price on the used market. As such, GameStop raised the price of a used PS5 Pro with a disc drive to $1,499.99 in October ($1,399.99 for the digital edition), which is quite expensive. If you manage to buy one, you'll definitely want to take full advantage of it. That means you need a TV or monitor equipped with 120 Hz and variable refresh rate (VRR) support.
A major reason consumers buy a PS5 Pro is the visual upgrade in games through its AI-driven upscaling technology. As such, Sony offers enhancements for specific titles compatible with its premium console. That often means you can get 4K visuals and steady FPS, or high FPS with decent graphics, since different games let you choose what to prioritize — all of which looks even better with true HDR. Sadly, not all TVs and monitors can handle all these features, so you'd miss out on everything offered by this gaming system.
If you're unsure whether your chosen screen supports a PS5 Pro, you can verify by looking up the TV model online for its native resolution and VRR support. For VRR specifically, you can also check your TV's settings; for example, on a Sony TV, you'll find it under Channels & Inputs or External Inputs > HDMI Signal Format. From there, look for Enhanced Format (VRR) or Game Mode. Keep in mind that Sony's VRR (for the PS5 Pro) is only supported on specific HDMI ports (HDMI 2.1 ports).
When someone mentions 120 Hz, think of it as how often the screen refreshes its image. So the higher the number, the more often it refreshes things — which is why, when you're pushing 120 FPS, you want your chosen TV or monitor to keep up — any delay means you aren't taking advantage of those extra frames supported by the console and title.
Generally, it's 1:1, so 120 FPS is compatible with a 120 Hz screen. Some standard budget entry level TVs and monitors may support only up to 60 Hz; TV models like LG's lower-tier UQ or UR series, as well as Samsung's base Crystal UHD models, have 60 Hz panels. That means the hardware limits you, and you'll only perceive 60 FPS.
VRR is a little different. Its job is to synchronize your display with your console's FPS in real time by adjusting its refresh rate, eliminating screen tearing, stuttering, and noticeable input lag. As such, you can think of it as the performance factor. While it's nice to have hardware capability on both ends between the TV/monitor and your PlayStation 5 Pro console, it doesn't matter if they can't consistently match each other. The last factor is response time. Response time refers to how quickly pixels wake up — you'll want a screen with fast response times to avoid ghosting and blurring.
If you're in the market to upgrade your TV for gaming, consider a Hisense U7SG or an LG C6 OLED 2026. While both TVs are well-rated by experts, their MSRPs are a bit high ($1,299.99 to $1,999.99 for a 55-inch), even though they're usually mid-range — steep discounts make them much more affordable. If you're really strapped for cash, you can go for a TCL QM7K if you can find one in stock; it offers great value for money.
However, if none of those picks work, consider using a small checklist while shopping, since you'll want to make sure the box or published specs mention a few crucial features. One is that the TV has native 120Hz and supports clean motion handling. Two, it has HDMI 2.1 ports, since you'll need that for VRR, plus support for auto low latency mode. Third, ideally, you'll want an OLED panel for the best visual experience and fast response times.
OLED TVs are still pretty expensive, so you might need to compromise. Alternatively, if on a tighter budget, mini-LED delivers high brightness and avoids the risk of screen burn, which can happen on OLED TVs."
19,2026-11-18,"Nvidia and its co-founder Jensen Huang have been at the centre of the artificial intelligence (AI) spending boom.
No company or chief executive has arguably been more consequential to the development of AI in recent years, with the US microchip manufacturer under Huang positioning itself as a critical cog in the development of the world-changing technology. 
That partnership has now ended following Huang's resignation over revelations of extramarital affairs, one of which is alleged to have been with a Chinese spy.
The company has been rocked by the scandal, with hundreds of billions of dollars wiped off its stock market value and questions swirling about the extent of the impact of Huang's departure.
While Nvidia is not as much of a household name as other Big Tech players such as Apple, Amazon and Microsoft, the company has been just as integral to the booming US stock market of recent years.
The billions of dollars being funnelled into AI and the construction of data centres has bolstered the American economy, with Nvidia being one of the biggest spenders.
Originally focusing on chips which process graphics for computer games, it pivoted to making the chips used in AI systems and now dominates the market. 
It is now one the most valuable firms, worth $3.8 trillion (Â£2.9 trillion), and on Tuesday said its rapid growth is poised to continue as the firm's quarterly sales doubled from a year earlier.
But there's now an air of uncertainty over Nvidia's future direction. Huang played a major role in the company, leading its shift towards AI, and maintained a firm grip on the company, with up to 200 people directly reporting to him. 
Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at investment firm AJ Bell, says because Huang was also a key figure in ""flying the flag"" to drum up enthusiasm and investment for the wider AI industry, his departure posed a ""real risk"" that Nvidia could now decide to change its current strategy and rein in spending.
Such a move could spell a wider slowdown in AI investment, which would have an economic impact. How big? That's ""unknown"", Coatsworth says.
""If they decide to go with the much more cautious route... there could be a knock-on effect in that you have got various companies in the AI infrastructure space suddenly finding they don't have a key source of finance,"" he says.
Kunjan Sobhani, senior technology industry analyst at Bloomberg Intelligence, expects the impact of Huang leaving to be ""limited"" for now as Nvidia has strong commitments and a deep order book. But he said this picture could change in the long run.
That is because Nvidia, on its journey to the top of the AI industry, has created a spider's web of deals through what is known as ""circular financing"".
Not only does it sell microchips to its customers, which include the likes of Meta, SpaceX and OpenAI, it has also provided funds for them and other firms in the supply chain by either handing out loans or investing in them directly.
The practice has prompted concerns among some investors, who worry what might happen if Nvidia were to turn off the spending taps. If investment dried up, would other firms in the supply chain cut spending or jobs themselves? Would that spread to the wider US economy?
That risk is perhaps why tech bosses including Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, the CEO of Fortnite maker Epic Games, have called for Huang's reinstatement. 
Musk claimed that ""critical deals"" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation, which has also prompted a campaign by shareholders and supporters to ""Bring Jensen Back"". 
Huang himself has said he is open to a return, just days after resigning.
Coatsworth says he has never seen circular financing used at this scale before, but admits there are competitive rivals around, which could insulate the wider AI industry from any potential slowdown at the firm.
When Nvidia's share price plunged in the wake of the affair revelations, the stock of its rival semiconductor makers including AMD, Broadcom and Marvell Technology jumped.
""I don't think all AI activity grinds to a halt if Nvidia slows down what it's doing and if it pulls some financing, but it does change how people would look at the whole situation,"" Coatsworth adds.
The risk of a slowdown has already rattled stock markets. Investors took fright at Democrat gains in the midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms. 
Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will actually pay off. 
Investment in AI is expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, who said the technology would remain the ""dominant driver of business investment growth"" next year.
Nvidia's interim boss Colette Kress said it was ""business as usual"" without Huang at the helm as the company's rapid growth remains on track.
The value of the shares in Big Tech companies have cushioned the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs, buoying investments and pensions for people worldwide.
Despite Huang saying he would be ""honoured"" to return, Nvidia's board has said it is not considering bringing him back.
And while Coatsworth thinks ""no CEO is irreplaceable"", he admits the pressure is on for whoever succeeds him.

NEW YORK, Nov 18 (Reuters) - Wall Street, expecting that wealthy investors and retail ⁠buyers willbattle for shares of Anthropic, is rolling out products tied to the stock, hosting client events and giving away merchandise ahead of the AI company's potentially record launch.
Despite the steep losses among the hyperscalers and ⁠other stocks linked to the AI trade in recent weeks, there is building anticipation from these investors for the Anthropic  initial public offering, which is expected on Thursday.
The IPO is expected to value the ‌company, which created the Claude chatbot, at up to $1.4 trillion. But it has a hard act to follow after the SpaceX launch, earlier this year, created a frenzy of excitement and events.
""Judging by the number of people interacting with us and asking us about this, and filling out forms, this could be even bigger than SpaceX,"" said Will Rhind, founder of GraniteShares, who plans to launch a 2x bull Anthropic exchange-traded fund along with a 2x bear product next week.
A successful IPO could boost confidence in other AI-related stocks, including many of the largest technology companies that have powered the stock ​market rally to a series of record highs, even as shares have recently swung sharply amid questions over the industry's ability to generate measurable ⁠profits.
According to industry estimates based on regulatory filings, the market was preparing in coming days ⁠for at least11 leveraged single-stock ETFs from seven issuers, withretail investors accounting for much of the interest.
One advertisement, on the StockTwits investing social media website,read: ""Artificial intelligence meets human conviction with the 2X Long Anthropic Daily ETF from Tradr. Coming ⁠soon!""
""It’s ‌going to hinge on the bull/bear case for AI,"" said Rhind. ""The believers are not going to be put off by this volatility in the broader AI trade.""
Vanda Research, which tracks retail trading activity, said it expects Anthropic itselfto attract about $250 million in net retail buying, based on its analysis of activity on listing days and the volume of the company's pre-IPO perpetual futures traded on Hyperliquid.
Such a level would double the record $118 million of retail buying for the SpaceX ⁠IPO.
Karl Cheong, head of ETFs at Ninepoint Partners in Toronto, points to the selloff in bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies as a ​sign that retail investors want to raise cash to buy Anthropic. ""This will be ‌the first frontier model that retail investors can access easily on a public market,"" he said.
Ninepointplans to launch an ETF for Canadian investors that would combine leveraged exposure toAnthropic, with a covered call strategy generating ⁠income.
WEALTH CLIENTS, LIVESTREAMS, MERCH
Banks have also planned ​select events.
Goldman Sachs organized a launch event on Tuesday for its wealth clients that was attended by the bank's president, John Waldron, as well as Anthropic Chief Financial Officer Krishna Rao and its co-founder and president, Daniela Amodei, according to a person familiar with the matter.
JPMorgan plans to livestream the IPO for clients and has prepared merchandise such as t-shirts and laptop covers, according to another person familiar with the matter. Anthropic Chief Executive Dario Amodei and his team were at the bank's offices all day on Friday meeting investors, the person said.
Anthropic declined ⁠to comment on the two events.
Still, not everyone is jumping on the bandwagon.
London-based Ben Preston, responsible for the Orbis Global Equity ​Strategy, a long-only stock-picking fund which also has a retail investor base, said the fund will not be buying Anthropic at the IPO.
“If it comes in at an IPO value of whatever it is – one and a half trillion dollars – that is a very expensive valuation to pay for a company which is unproven,"" said Preston.
Although StockTwits users give the anticipated IPO an ""extremely bullish"" score of 85 out of 100, based on a poll on its site, 67% of the more than 18,000 members who ⁠responded said they did not plan to buy Anthropic during the initial offering.Only 20% said they will buy, while another 13% said they would wait to see the price.
Some on social media wereskeptical about the potential price of the listing. Others saidit has been tougher for ordinary investors to get their hands on pre-IPO shares of Anthropic than for SpaceX shares before the latter's IPO in June.
SpaceX has since had a mixed record, with shares priced at $135 in the IPO, jumping to $150 then briefly as high as $225, before dropping as low as $104. They now trade around $142.
Still, ""as it has gotten closer to(Anthropic's) launch date, I've seen the excitement and the clamor to get a piece of whatever is allocated to retail grow and grow,"" said Mitchell ​Caplan, chairman and CEO of Willow Wealth and former CEO of E-Trade Financial.
OVERSEAS INTEREST
Interest has also been building abroad, with many British platforms inviting retail investors to send orders for ⁠shares.
“Historically, retail investors have been on the sidelines when it comes to public listings,"" said Jordan Sinclair, president of Robinhood UK.
Yet Susannah Streeter, chief investment strategist at Wealth Club, a British investment platform serving high-net-worth investors, said the IPO highlights the divide ​between connected buyers who can get in pre-IPO and retail investors who end up pleading with their brokers for part of an allocation.
""It's a bit like ‌arriving at a glamorous launch party – institutional backers on the VIP guest list gain early entry, enjoying the vintage ​champagne and finest canapés behind closed doors,"" Streeter said in an email.
""By the time the doors finally open to the general public, the risk is that the best of the festivities are well underway and latecomers are left paying a steep entry fee to pick over the flat prosecco and leftover crisps.""
(Reporting by Suzanne McGee; additional reporting by Nupur Anand, Saeed Azhar,  Vidya Ranganathan and Amy-Jo Crowley; editing by Megan Davies and Jonathan Spicer )

21,2026-11-18,"
A lot of the lamps that we see in the market announce themselves as lamps just by its silhouette and design. A glowing pebble tied up like a tiny parcel is a pretty appealing alternative to another bulky bedside lamp. Entoro Design’s sekimori-ishi lamp addresses a familiar little problem: wanting a pool of evening light without necessarily surrendering your nightstand to a stem, shade, and trailing power cord. The handmade lamp has a rechargeable battery, so its placement doesn’t have to revolve around an outlet.
Its reference is more specific than a general appreciation for Japanese gardens. A sekimori-ishi is a stone bound with cord and placed along a garden path to communicate that visitors shouldn’t proceed farther. No words are necessary but only if you understand the convention. I like the restraint of that idea. A boundary can be communicated through an object that belongs in the landscape, rather than a sign competing with it.
Designer: Entoro Design






The project began after the designer encountered one of these stones at a Kyoto temple. Back home in Hokkaido, he searched a riverbed in neighboring Shiraoi for stones, then worked through the handmade process over several months. The aim was unusually tactile for a lighting project. He wanted it to be a surface that would transmit light like frosted glass, paired with a weight that would feel convincingly stone-like.
The finished shade is cast epoxy resin, not an illuminated rock or a piece of frosted glass. Vegetable-tanned leather cord replaces the traditional rope which adds to the character of the lamp. That distinction matters because the appeal depends on a little visual uncertainty. Unlit, the pale body in the supplied photographs looks cool and cloudy. Switched on, it becomes a warm, luminous volume, with the crossing cords remaining sharply defined against it.

Those cords do a surprising amount of visual work. They trace the rounded body, gather at the top, and form an upright loop with a tightly wrapped texture. Without them, the shade might read as another pebble-shaped accent light. With them, it has a recognizable construction and a slightly endearing awkwardness. I’m particularly fond of how the loop gives the squat shape some height without introducing a separate stand.
The lighting controls are less conspicuous. Entoro specifies three brightness settings, selected through a touch sensor underneath, and a warm-white color temperature of 2700K. Putting the control below preserves the uninterrupted body, although I’d want to try that arrangement before praising its convenience at bedtime. A hidden switch can make an object look wonderfully simple while asking a little more of the hand using it.






Cordless operation also comes with a practical limit. Entoro lists approximately 6.7 hours of battery life on low, dropping to 1.6 hours on high, with USB-C charging. For me, that positions it more naturally as an evening companion than a light to leave running all night. Moving it between a bedside table and a living room shelf makes sense. But also, forgetting to recharge it remains entirely possible, especially for a forgetful person like me.
The photographs encourage that intimate reading. In one, the lamp sits beside a reclining cat, its glow illuminating a small patch of wooden floor. Another places it on a moss-covered rock. The outdoor setting is lovely, but I wouldn’t mistake a photograph for confirmation of weather resistance. Indoors, the low profile and visible wrapping are enough to give it character without requiring an elaborate arrangement around it.


I’d choose this for a room that already has adequate functional lighting and needs something smaller for the hours afterward. The design’s strongest quality is how carefully its parts relate: the broad, flattened body, the narrow crossing lines, the generous loop. Even before knowing the garden reference, I can read those proportions. Learning the story adds another layer rather than doing all the work.
That’s a balance I appreciate in an object with a cultural reference. You can understand where the form came from without treating the lamp like an exhibit. On a nightstand, it still has an ordinary job to do: provide some light, occupy a little space, and look worth keeping when it’s switched off.

Every Black Friday I tell myself I’m shopping for other people, and every Black Friday a knife somehow ends up in my own pocket. This…
Every craft that works in color figured this out long ago. A painter doesn’t rinse one brush between every color; there’s a brush for each…
This lamp, the second part of the Completing Absence series by designer Petra Schmidt, is more concerned with shedding light on the issue of energy…
Described as the lamp that leans but never topples over, the Spintop lamp is a strangely satisfying amalgamation of a top and a lamp. Designed…
NOVA is more than just an air purifier. This “nomadic” product can be placed anywhere and everywhere to not only clean the surrounding air of…

22,2026-11-18,"Robert Pattinson is officially the next James Bond.
Now, as we prepare to herald Bond's next era, I set out to visit as many of the most iconic and lesser-known London filming locations from Daniel Craig's movies as I could.
I've always loved the James Bond movies, especially the Daniel Craig ones. As a Londoner, I found this tour a fun way to immerse myself in the 007 universe and see my city in a whole new way.
Although the Bond films are known for their stunning locations around the world, from Lake Como in Italy to the Atacama Desert in Chile, the home of 007 is, after all, London.
""Casino Royale"" (2006), Craig's first Bond movie, was not filmed in London, so the locations below are from his four subsequent movies.
Some are iconic tourist landmarks, while others are lesser-known back streets and restaurants, often in fancy parts of the city. Although filming also took place in areas such as Camden and Canary Wharf, I primarily stuck to central London locations.
If you're planning a trip to London and want to do your own Bond tour, it's doable in a day, although you'll need comfortable shoes. This route took me around five hours with two pit stops and a short visit inside the National Gallery.
Here are some of the locations from Craig's Bond movies in London.
This is where M and Tanner discussed Bond's mission.
The brutalist estate is one of my all-time favorite spots in London, and I always recommend it to people visiting the city. It's a residential complex that also has a center with restaurants, bars, a library, theater, and a cinema.
I found the parking lot not exactly scenic, but it's located right across from Smithfield Market, London's famous and historic wholesale meat market.
I love this part of London — the City of London it's called — precisely because of how old parts of it are.
The fictional apartment is located in London's beautiful Notting Hill neighborhood. Nothing short of the best, of course, for one of MI6's most elite agents.
It's one of my favorite areas to stroll around, though it's mostly residential, so it's important to be respectful of the residents.
I feel like spy films set in London always feature a chase scene on the London Underground, which makes me half-expect to see some sort of espionage whenever I'm on an escalator.
Some of the ""Skyfall"" chase scene was also filmed at the 007 Stage in Pinewood.
The precise location inside the gallery is in Room 34.
It's a gorgeously ornate room with teal walls displaying British paintings from 1740 to 1800. The seats are different now from when Skyfall was filmed, but I still sat on a bench in the same location as Bond and Q did.
The National Gallery also has free entry, making it a perfect spot to visit.
Founded in 1798, it's the oldest restaurant in London and serves traditional British meals, making it a fitting spot for the movie.
It's on the pricier side, so I haven't eaten there, but one day I'll be sure to dine — just to feel like I'm in a Bond movie.
While it might look like any old building from the outside, the interior of Freemasons' Hall is very ornate, with Art Deco design.
104 Pall Mall was a government office in ""Quantum of Solace."" Ian Fleming, who wrote the books, used to dine at a members' club previously located at 104 Pall Mall.
The road the British Academy is located on is near Pall Mall. The area is known for its exclusive and traditional gentlemen's clubs.
Walking around in my jeans and trainers, I felt a little underdressed.
The actual building is on the left of this photo and overlooks Trafalgar Square. In the same movie, the road pictured is where Swann and Bond have their final farewell.
The luxury Corinthia Hotel is visible in the background. Other scenes in ""No Time to Die"" were also filmed in this area, which contains the headquarters of the Metropolitan Police, Scotland Yard.
This is where Bond confronts the supervillain Blofeld.
I don't often cross this bridge because it's always full of tourists taking photos of the London Eye and the Houses of Parliament, which means it takes longer to get through the crowds. But whenever I visit, I always think it's a magnificent sight. And now I'll think of Bond, too.
While you mostly see the interior of his house, you can also see this road in the movie. It's right by Waterloo Station, which is close to lots of cultural, dining, and night-time spots I frequent, so I was surprised by how residential it was.
By this time, rush hour was in full swing in the city. Broadgate Tower, which was designed in the movie to resemble an office building in Shanghai, was where Bond followed the assassin, Patrice.
All the locations I visited are central and easy to reach when visiting the typical sightseeing spots in London.
The best part is that it takes you down some back alleys, like where the members' clubs are, and into some more residential areas, where you can gaze at the beautiful houses. It was fun to become a tourist in the city I live in.
It also takes you to some of my favorite underrated spots in London, like the Barbican, which I highly recommend.
The directors clearly had good taste, and I'm excited to see if I can spot any of the London filming locations during the Robert Pattinson era.

Jensen Huang's Fall Hits 'Open' Models In Boost to Anthropic and OpenAI
Voyager 1 Reaches One Light-Day From Earth
Old programmers never die, they just become managers."
25,2026-11-18,"
For You
Settings
 news Alerts
There are no new alerts at this time
Leading AI lab Anthropic is expected to make the largest-ever public market debut when it starts trading on the Nasdaq stock exchange Thursday.
Founded in 2021 by brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei, both former employees of rival OpenAI, Anthropic and its main product, Claude, are seen as the gold standard of AI models, despite lacking the household-name cache of ChatGPT. 
With a target market value of $1.5 trillion — larger than SpaceX’s record setting IPO just a few months ago — Anthropic’s public arrival will serve as more than just a measure of what Wall Street thinks the company is worth. It will be a test of current sentiment around AI itself at a pivotal moment for the industry. 
For much of the past year, the AI investment wave has powered the stock market to new heights and sustained U.S. economic growth. But there are signs the wave may be starting to break.
“Investors have over recent weeks become increasingly concerned around the sustainability of the investment boom,” James McCann, senior economist for investment strategy at Edward Jones, said in a client note Wednesday ahead of Anthropic’s debut. 
The market gains notched this spring and summer thanks to the AI boom have now largely been wiped out. 
Fears of over-spending on infrastructure, rising debt loads, ballooning costs and uncertain demand have grown. At the same time, public opinion has turned against data centers and AI-skeptical Democrats won control of Congress earlier this month.
Then, the Jensen Huang scandal broke, creating a cloud of uncertainty over Nvidia, a company at the heart of the AI economy. The tech heavy Nasdaq was up 19% for the year recently. As of Wednesday, it’s higher by less than 5%.  
“The leadership change at Nvidia meanwhile adds to short-term uncertainty around the sector, particularly given the important role this giant company has played in financially supporting a range of smaller technology firms in recent years,” said McCann.
“With the selloff in technology stocks continuing this week investors will be watching to see how well the Anthropic IPO is received.”
The offering priced Anthropic at $83 a share, above the company’s intended range, the company said late Wednesday. 
There is little debate over how uniquely successful Anthropic has been. The latest edition of Claude, dubbed Fable, ranks No. 1 on Artificial Analysis’ leaderboard of top-performing large language models as it is able to handle complex computer engineering tasks. 
And its Claude Code application is now used throughout the tech industry and by some accounts became the fastest-growing product in enterprise software history when it was released in Feb. 2025, hitting $1 billion in revenues within six months of launching. 
Thursday’s IPO is also expected to mint a new crop of billionaires and multi-millionaires as Anthropic’s co-founders and early employees see their interest in the company turned into shares with a defined cash value.
Part of that will be fueled by a massive appetite for investing in the shares among mom-and-pop investors. According to Vanda Research, a markets consultancy, Anthropic could see approximately $250 million worth of buying among non-institutional or “retail” investors when it starts trading Thursday. That would be double the magnitude of interest following SpaceX’s IPO. 
Other major winners will include Amazon, Google-parent Alphabet, Microsoft, and Nvidia, which will each own billions-worth of Anthropic shares.  
Some observers see Anthropic’s debut as more of a major milestone than a make-or-break moment. In their view, there are plenty of other investments backstopping the AI boom.
“A strong IPO would help resurrect the AI trade, but it does not mean a weaker-than-expected IPO would bury that trade,” said Renée Friedman, global head of research at EXANTE, a Europe-based finance and technology group.
“There are significant commitments into the AI cycle by some of the world’s largest companies over a multi-year period. These are not going to just disappear,” he said.
In the first half of 2026, Anthropic saw total revenues of more than $16 billion. That compares with $41 million over the same period just three years ago. While Anthropic was technically operating at a loss to start the year, investors expect it to be profitable soon.   
Those heady figures capture a tension at the heart of Anthropic’s IPO. In addition to being the leading AI lab, current and former Anthropic employees have also been some of the most vocal proponents of AI safety, giving voice to fears that, should the technology become too advanced, it could start to run amok. 
“I think that without international coordination we will incur a risk of causing human extinction,” former Anthropic employee Jacob Coxon wrote of his concerns about AI shortly before resigning. 
Yet some critics have said those concerns have proven no match for the seemingly unstoppable momentum of creating a company publicly valued at more than $1 trillion. 
Dario Amodei has responded, arguing building safe AI and creating the world’s most advanced — and valuable — AI models are not necessarily mutually exclusive.
When a user criticized Anthropic for seeming to plunge ahead with its attempt to create “superhuman” AI despite the risks to humanity doing so, Amodei replied in an X post that “a list of things that Anthropic did in the past year that were pro-safety and commercially costly to us” would be much longer than a list of risks it posed to mankind.
Rob Wile is a Pulitzer Prize-winning journalist covering breaking business stories for NBCNews.com.
© 2026 NBCUniversal Media, LLC"
