Grand Theft Auto 6 
With Grand Theft Auto 6âs world being as big as it is and current consoles being six years old, thereâs naturally a bit of worry about how the game performs on PS5 and Xbox Series X/S. Our reviewer played the game on a PS5 Pro, considered to be the most powerful console on the market right now, and said that while it runs at 30 frames-per-second most of the time, there were still some noticeable frame drops in crowded areas.
But if you havenât bought a PS5 Pro, what can you expect from GTA 6âs performance? Well, the folks over at Digital Foundry have done what they do best and created some lengthy technical breakdowns of the game, including stress testing it in explosive situations.
According to their video, the PS5 Pro tends to hold a pretty solid 30fps, unless you go around making a mess by blowing up a lot of cars at once; in those moments Digital Foundry saw the game dip to around 24fps at its lowest. The base PS5, however, seems to struggle to maintain 30fps in the best of cases, and if you decide to cause a lot of on-screen destruction, those dips are actually sustained until you leave an area.Â 
On the Xbox side, the Xbox Series X seems to share the same relative stability as the PS5 Pro, with the same frame drops during car chases, crowded scenes, or firefights when things get busier on screen. The big question is how the Xbox Series S, which was originally released in 2020 as a cheaper, lower-powered alternative to the bougie, more expensive consoles of the year, handles GTA 6. According to Digital Foundry, there are some details that are less prevalent or rendered with lower-res textures, and the resolution is lower, coming in at 720p compared to the 1440p of Series X. 
The DF video has a number of comparison shots demonstrating how a lot of small details have been downgraded on Series S, but the real difference is that the framerate is, unsurprisingly, struggling the most on the less powerful Xbox. Digital Foundry reports that while 30fps is the target, even the most “typical” play can affect framerate, with standard combat encounters and car chases dropping it to around 25 and the more demanding carnage bringing it down to 20. There’s even a few seconds in the video where it gets down to 18 and 19fps during an explosive shootout.
Ultimately, if youâre hoping for the smoothest possible experience, the PS5 Pro and Xbox Series X are the unsurprising winners here. A PC version of the game is rumored to launch sometime next year, and I imagine thatâs where folks with high-end builds are going to be able to get smoother, 60fps framerates as Rockstar apparently has no plans to add a Performance Mode to GTA6 on consoles. If youâre running a Series S, maybe try not to cause too much mayhem along the way for best results.Â 
Though the Xbox Series S provided a more affordable way for people to jump into the next generation of gaming consoles in 2020, as time has gone on and games have gotten more demanding on the hardware, developers have expressed frustration with having to make their games compatible with the device. Tekken director Katsuhiro Harada described it as akin to âhaving to do extra work for a slightly weaker child.â

32,2026-11-18,SEC filings reveal the diplomat and investor is giving new meaning to the term hedge fund.
33,2026-11-18,"Author of the First Trade newsletter
The housing market has spent much of 2026 in a deep freeze. Mortgage rates remain painfully high, affordability is still a mess, and homeowners sitting on cheap pandemic-era loans have little reason to move.
So naturally, Berkshire Hathaway is loading up on homebuilders.
Wait, what?
Why would a firm known for the everyday practicality of its investments buy into a sector under so much pressure? Finding value in the market is one thing. But eventually those discount investments have to go back up. Otherwise, they're just value traps.
Over the past few quarters, Berkshire has been staking billions on an eventual housing rebound. The firm nearly doubled its Lennar stake in the third quarter, bringing its ownership to about 26.6 million combined A and B shares, or 12.5% of the company. The position is worth roughly $2.2 billion.
Berkshire also owns shares in D.R. Horton and NVR, and new CEO Greg Abel acquired fellow homebuilder Taylor Morrison in a $6.8 billion deal earlier this year.
Put simply, Berkshire has been buying deeper into the housing freeze. And while the filing provides a slightly outdated snapshot of the firm's holdings (as of September 30), the overall message is that Berkshire is sticking its neck out on a contrarian bet.
A gridlocked housing market is not necessarily the same thing as a doomed homebuilder market.
When existing homeowners stay put — as they are now — buyers who need to relocate have fewer choices. That can make new construction one of the only real paths to homeownership, particularly in fast-growing markets where inventory is tight.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Big builders like Lennar also have a significant advantage over individual sellers. They can offer temporary mortgage-rate buydowns — often reducing buyers' monthly payments for the first few years — along with other incentives. They also have scale, existing financing relationships, and strong balance sheets that position them well to absorb a long downturn.
It's possible that Berkshire is drawn specifically to builders because they can keep taking market share even while the broader housing market remains broken. A similar dynamic has played out across the AI trade as jittery investors place a premium on companies with rock-solid finances.
To be clear, Berkshire is not necessarily making a bet on a near-term rebound. It's more of a wager that whenever a rebound does materialize, these companies will be at the forefront.
The beauty of Berkshire's growing homebuilder investment is that it doesn't need mortgage rates to collapse tomorrow to work. But it does need a few specific things to go right over the longer term:
Like many Berkshire investments before it, the homebuilder wager is a patient, somewhat unglamorous one. In an AI-obsessed market, Berkshire is gonna Berkshire.
But that doesn't mean the firm is sitting out the AI boom. Berkshire kept its sizable Alphabet stake unchanged in the third quarter, preserving one of the biggest positions in its portfolio.
In other words, the firm is not making an either-or call between artificial intelligence and housing. It's leaning into both. That may be the most Berkshire thing of all.
Your guide to what's moving markets

Cerebras launches disaggregated inference >>
Nov 17 2026
We’ve deployed Cerebras Wafer Scale Engines with GPUs to deliver lightning-fast tokens at scale
The world is hungry for ultrafast tokens. In 2026, Cerebras saw an insatiable demand for fast inference, both on our inference cloud and via our partners. To meet demand, we began developing disaggregated inference: GPUs handle prompt processing (prefill) and Cerebras systems handle output generation (decode).
This solution is now in production and delivers five times more tokens per megawatt at the same ultrafast Cerebras speeds.
We can now serve more ultrafast tokens via Cerebras Inference Cloud to meet market demand. Infrastructure partners can also leverage the Cerebras disaggregated solution to achieve more ultrafast tokens in their own datacenters.
Alongside our recently announced CS-4, our dramatically scaled manufacturing, and our planned 700MW of data center capacity, disaggregated inference will enable Cerebras and partners to serve many more ultrafast tokens in 2027 and beyond.
In this blog we cover:
Agentic applications like Muse, Copilot, and Codex are scaling rapidly and require highly interactive inference to keep users engaged. To meet the growing demand of these applications in a power-constrained environment, our industry must deliver significantly more inference capacity per megawatt, at response speeds that make these applications useful.
Sources: Goldman Sachs Research, “AI Agents Forecast to Boost Tech Cash Flow as Usage Soars,” May 20, 2026; Goldman Sachs data center capacity estimates, as reported by Investing.com, November 6, 2026. 
That is why Cerebras invested in disaggregation. Pairing a prefill accelerator for prompt processing with the Cerebras System for response generation increases token throughput per megawatt while preserving Cerebras’ ultrafast generation speeds. For inference providers, that means more capacity to serve customers and greater revenue potential from the same power budget.
An inference request has two main stages. Prompt processing, known as prefill, processes the input and produces intermediate model state. Response generation, known as decode, uses that state to generate output tokens. There are many ways to split the inference workload, including draft–target and attention–FFN among others, but separating prefill from decode is uniquely advantageous when the goal is highly interactive inference.
Decode is autoregressive. That means the model generates one token at a time, using each generated token as an input to generate the next. Each step means reading model data from memory again, so memory bandwidth matters a lot. In the ultrafast inference domain, SRAM-centric chips have an advantage because they can keep that data on-chip and read it much more quickly. Cerebras takes this to wafer scale, with tens of gigabytes of SRAM and tens of petabytes per second of memory bandwidth on a single chip—350× the SRAM capacity and 5,400× the memory bandwidth of a typical GB300 GPU. This allows the Cerebras System to fit more concurrent requests and model layers directly on the chip, enabling the CS-3, CS-4, and CS-5 to achieve ultrafast speeds at an attractive throughput.
Pairing Cerebras systems, which are optimized for ultrafast decode, with accelerators well-suited to the compute needs of prefill, such as AMD Instinct GPUs, allows the delivery of an immense amount of ultrafast inference at scale. In a production deployment on the Cerebras Inference Cloud, AMD Instinct GPUs process prompts, and Cerebras CS-3 systems generate responses. Moving prompt processing to GPUs allows Cerebras systems to devote their serving capacity to response generation. In our measured production deployment, this combination delivered 5X the throughput per megawatt of a CS-3-only configuration, with no loss in token generation speed.
Source: November 14th shared GPT-OSS-120B endpoint via the Cerebras Inference Cloud throughput per megawatt before and after enabling disaggregated inference, normalized to the aggregated inference baseline (1×). 
We plan to deploy disaggregated inference broadly across hundreds of megawatts in the Cerebras Inference Cloud and in partner deployments by the end of 2027. Our aim is to increase the ultrafast inference capacity available for our customers.
The Cerebras disaggregated inference solution was also built for our partners to adopt. Infrastructure partners can deploy disaggregated inference clusters based on Cerebras-provided reference architecture, co-locating their chosen prefill accelerators and Cerebras systems within a unified network. For software integration, partners can either integrate with the Cerebras end-to-end disaggregated software stack or a decode-only SDK.
The end-to-end disaggregated software stack is best suited for partners optimizing time-to-market. It consists of:
The decode-only SDK is best suited for partners optimizing for control. With the decode-only SDK, partners can bring their own inference framework and prefill engine to the Cerebras decode engine and KV Portal software. In addition, the decode-only SDK provides development aids such as a mock Cerebras decode replica and a reference integration to an open-source disaggregated inference framework and prefill engine.
Early-access partners, including Amazon Web Services, Fireworks AI, Gimlet Labs, and General Compute, already have access to the Cerebras disaggregation solution for their deployments. We are now expanding access to additional partners looking to bring ultrafast inference to their customers at scale.
To discuss the results, solutions, or deployment opportunities in more detail, contact us here.

37,2026-11-18,"Throughout Summer 2019, our family — two kids, a rescue dog, my husband, and me — lived in Austin. 
I was a mom with a few contract jobs while my husband worked full time. That pivotal year, I got what Vito Corleone would call ""an offer I could not refuse,""  and so did my husband. 
By Fall 2019, my husband and I were living in two different cities, each with one kid, while still married. 
My husband was a young professional working for a tech startup. Pay was reasonable. Culture was great. He felt valued and satisfied with his work. After working in downtown Austin, he'd come home to our elementary-aged son, toddler daughter, dog (Chester), and me.
I had an extensive background in fitness. I had served in campus recreation at a few Texas universities in the early '00s when glamorous rec centers were a competitive recruiting tool for colleges. From 2005 to 2010, I played an integral role in the construction of the Dedman Center for Recreational Sports at Southern Methodist University.
By the mid-2000s, lavish recreation facilities weren't just value-enhancers for schools. They became highly desirable in the multifamily market. My work with SMU's fitness center was noticed by a nearby apartment community, The Village Dallas.
Mention The Village Dallas to anyone who lived in Dallas in the 1970s, and they'll get a grin like a Cheshire cat, recalling the iconic property. The Village is a community of apartment complexes that share central amenities. It attracted young singles. The Village had aged. Lincoln Property Company was ready to restore The Village Dallas to its old glory days.
Village Main Street would be a $350M hospitality addition to The Village Dallas. It would include a boutique hotel, restaurants, bars, a country club, a pool that would rival Las Vegas resort pools, sports fields, and a recreation center. I was invited to serve as a consultant on the rec center.
From 2015 to 2019, I worked from Austin as the project's fitness consultant. I marked up renderings, allocated dedicated space for programming, selected equipment, tendered bids, and procured CAD drawings. I would meet with stakeholders occasionally in Dallas but handled most of my obligations remotely.

In 2019, I received an offer to transition into a full-time role as Executive Director of Wellness. The Village presented me with a six-figure salary, bonus structure, and housing allowance. Acceptance would mean relocation to Dallas. With my husband's support, I took the job.
My husband notified his employer that he would need to tender his resignation to move to Dallas, citing my job offer. He had worked for the tech company for seven years by then, and it was no longer a startup. These were high times for Austin tech businesses, and my husband was valued in his role as senior manager. His supervisor asked that he hold off on giving his letter. The next day, he was presented with a sizable salary increase to stay put.
We had options. I could revoke my acceptance, and we could maintain the status quo in Austin. He could decline the offer and move to Dallas for my job. Or we could lean into both.
We chose the latter.
On weekends, the family would come together. Either the boys would drive up to Dallas or the girls down to Austin. We kept our 6-year-old son in his established school in Austin. Our 2-year-old daughter and I moved into a Village apartment in Dallas. We connected over FaceTime nightly and in person on Saturday and Sunday. Our plan was to give it a year, then revisit our situation.
Then the pandemic changed everything. 
My daughter and I drove home to Austin for what we thought would be a week. It ended up being three months. The pandemic was a tragic time for the world. Conversely, for our family, it bound us back together under the same roof. We felt immense gratitude.
We were glad that we took agency and tried something difficult. Our sacrifices translated to financial security. My husband and I modeled for our son and daughter that our family supports ambitious women.
We agreed that togetherness was our top priority. We had to choose between Austin and Dallas. I resigned from my job in June 2020, although my husband was willing to be the one to do so. He loved his work, and my squirrely heart preferred outdoor adventures over business meetings. 
Now, the kids and I go on hikes, jump off cliffs, and ride a Vespa scooter. I became a wellness writer. I've authored three books, created content, and emboldened other women to get outside and be active. I still model ambition. It just looks different now.

Home > Electric Vehicles
Ford's BlueOval Battery Park Michigan is shipping its first lithium iron phosphate cells to Louisville for Fathom prototypes, a key milestone for the automaker's next-generation EV.
Senior Writer, Electrified Cars
Senior Writer, Electrified Cars

				Edited by Jon Skillings 
November 18, 2026, 5:31 pm ET
This week, Ford began shipments of the first lithium iron phosphate electric vehicle battery cells from its new BlueOval Battery Park in Marshall, Michigan, to its Louisville Assembly Plant in Kentucky. There, the prismatic cells will power early Ford Fathom electric pickup truck prototypes and validation vehicles. The automaker invited me into the plant to meet the people who build the batteries and to see how the so-called “jelly roll” is made.
In 2025, consumers lost access to the clean vehicle tax rebates of the Inflation Reduction Act, but the program and its incentives remain in the law for automakers and suppliers. So bringing this US-based, domestically owned battery plant online is kind of a big deal for Ford. It’s an important milestone for the automaker’s plan to make its new Universal Electric Vehicle, or UEV, platform a reality and to bring the Fathom — its upcoming electric pickup truck — to market in 2027 at a price that EV-eager customers can stomach. 
Ford expects to sell the upcoming Fathom at an estimated price of $28,350 (well, $29,945, including the $1,595 destination and delivery fee), with the first preorder expected to open early next year.
More from CNET
BlueOval Battery Park Michigan is a $3 billion, 2 million-square-foot facility with a targeted annual production capacity of 20 gigawatt-hours. That’s a whole lot of batteries. Ford is expected to employ 1,700 people at the site across two 12-hour shifts, running around the clock to reach that capacity.
The plant is a clean facility, so getting in meant donning a clean suit, mask, hairnet, gloves and two layers of shoe booties. I then crossed special magnetic and sticky floor panels designed to pull any metal particles off the bottoms of those booties, before passing through multiple air-blower airlocks that blasted my body clean of loose dust.
From the outside, the building is huge and difficult to take in completely. On the inside, the scale truly comes into focus as I stare down a massive hallway just under a mile long end to end. Raw materials — rolls of copper and aluminum sheet, plus the chemical powders that coat them — enter the plant at the eastern end, move in a straight line through the facility, and completed battery packs exit at the western end. The first 0.7 mile of the fab belongs to cell production, split between isolated cathode and anode lines that join halfway through for final cell assembly.
Ford engineers, battery experts and executives tell me that the automaker chose lithium iron phosphate, or LFP, chemistry in a prismatic (rectangular) format for its affordability and structural benefits. LFP uses no nickel or cobalt, which keeps costs down, and Ford found the chemistry well suited to bidirectional charging.
To keep costs low enough to deliver a reasonably priced car, Ford couldn’t use a Chinese supplier and still qualify for IRA manufacturing credits. So it licensed the technology from one, working with a company called CATL, which one Ford exec described as “the world’s leading battery manufacturer,” whose batteries power 30 million vehicles worldwide.
Despite working with a Chinese company, Ford says the plant is completely US-owned, staffed and operated and is totally IRA-compliant, with no foreign entity of concern, or FEOC, involvement beyond the licensing agreement.
Charles Poon, who leads vehicle hardware engineering, said Ford didn’t simply copy a catalog part. 
“We designed a unique cell that does not exist in the CATL catalog, specifically for the UEV,” Poon said, adding that the taller and simpler cell delivers “the energy density, the affordability, the durability that [Ford is] looking for” and allows the automaker to more completely integrate the cells and battery pack into the vehicle’s design.
Ford also had to build the supply chain, Poon said, sourcing an IRA-compliant stream for a battery chemistry that is only just entering the mainstream in North America, and working with approved global partners and North American suppliers.
On the cathode and anode assembly lines, meter-wide rolls of copper and aluminum are first coated with a chemical slurry that is baked on. The large rolls are then split into 10 smaller rolls, with tabs notched into their exposed edges at set intervals. As the continuous rolls pass through multiple machines like gigantic cassette players, AI-powered vision systems watch the foil for coating defects, scratches and wrinkles, marking problem sections so they can be automatically removed.
Next, the cathode and anode foils are layered and rolled into a cylinder, then pressed flat into what Ford calls a jelly roll. Imagine a roll of toilet paper mashed in a hydraulic press, and you’ll have a good idea of what it looks like: a flat rectangle with rounded ends.
The jelly rolls are then gathered in pairs, and the previously exposed tabs are folded together and ultrasonically welded to more solid terminals, which are in turn laser-welded to a cap. The jelly roll couple is then laser-sealed into an aluminum box, about the size and weight of a masonry brick, to form a nearly complete prismatic cell. Engineers vacuum out the air, check for leaks by pressurizing the cell with helium, and fill it with liquid electrolyte. Bob’s your uncle: you have a finished LFP prismatic cell.
Ford says the assembly lines can deliver 40 cells every minute, which plant manager Tracy Church says makes BlueOval Battery Park “among the fastest LFP prismatic lines outside China.”
From the cell line, completed prismatic cells would normally pass through a door and straight into pack manufacturing, where full battery packs with integrated “e-box” electronics, integrated cooling and wiring are built. That part of the plant is still undergoing validation, so for now Ford is shipping just the cells to Louisville, not complete battery packs. The company plans to have pack manufacturing up and running in Marshall ahead of Fathom’s launch, with full pack deliveries to Louisville planned in 2027. Church told me that the plant aims to produce 50 battery packs per hour.
Ford hasn’t officially specified the energy capacity of the cells or the completed battery packs and wouldn’t comment when asked about expected range for the Fathom. The automaker wouldn’t even confirm how many cells go into the EV’s pack, but I estimate between 90 and 108 cells based on a cutaway of the finalized unit I examined (but wasn’t permitted to photograph) during my tour. I expect Ford is holding off on finalizing details until closer to the EV’s 2027 launch window.
Battery Park Michigan isn’t just sending cells to Kentucky; it’s also sending expertise. Ford’s Glendale Kentucky plant — which used to build batteries for the F-150 Lightning full-size electric pickup — is currently being retooled to build LFP cells for Ford Energy for large-scale energy storage applications, and the lessons Ford learns in Michigan are already being shared with the manufacturing teams there.
Ford has been in the EV business for over 15 years. In 2011, it revealed the Focus Electric at CES. 2019 brought the debut of the Mustang Mach-E — which Ford recently found is holding up well after years on the road with minimal battery degradation — followed by the arrival of the F-150 Lightning in 2021. This isn’t the brand’s first electric rodeo, but executives tell me that the automaker has never had this much control and ownership over its battery production, and it’s hoping that will make the difference as it prepares the next-gen UEV vehicles.

43,2026-11-18,"The shape of the bullwhip effect in the beer game. Demand changes once. Everyone upstream of it goes nuts.
Four people sit at a table running a beer supply chain. One is the retailer, one is the wholesaler, one is the distributor, and one is the factory. Each week, each player makes one decision - how much beer to order from the player upstream (the factory decides how much to brew). Orders and shipments take weeks to arrive, and nobody is allowed to talk.
In week five, customer demand goes from four cases a week to eight. Then it stays at eight for the rest of the game.
That’s the only change, and the supply chain still goes haywire. The retailer sees the jump and orders more. Nothing shows up for weeks, the backlog grows, and the retailer orders more again. Each player upstream sees a bigger, noisier signal than the one below. Then the beer arrives in a flood, everyone is buried in inventory, and orders crash to zero. The factory, three steps from the customer, gets the wildest swings of all.
This is the MIT Beer Game. Jay Forrester built its ancestor at MIT in the late 1950s, and John Sterman turned it into a landmark experiment in 1989. His diagnosis is simple:
“Subjects failed to account for control actions which had been initiated but not yet had their effect.”
People stare at the empty shelf and forget about the beer already on the truck.
The AI data center business is playing the same game but with more than four different actors. People and businesses buy AI products. Those products run on models from labs like OpenAI and Anthropic. The labs rent compute from the hyperscalers and from the neoclouds - CoreWeave, Nebius, and a bunch of others whose whole business is renting out GPUs. The clouds buy chips from Nvidia, which buys wafers, memory, and packaging from its suppliers. Each actor sees orders from the actor below it, but none of them see actual end demand. Even if they think they do, they are only guessing at some future state. And, in this game, the delays aren’t measured in weeks. Land, power, permits, and construction take years. Elections and geopolitics change regulatory dynamics, which introduce other delays. This week, the Commerce Department closed a loophole that let Chinese companies rent Nvidia computing power located in other countries. The cost of capital moves with the economy, the risk, and, more importantly, how people perceive future risk.
Nvidia is the factory. Its results this week were spectacular: $115.8 billion in quarterly revenue, up 103%, and $65.3 billion in net income, up 105%, on a 74.8% gross margin. It generated $43.8 billion of free cash flow in the quarter, almost double a year ago. It’s also ordering ahead, with $390 billion in purchase commitments to its own suppliers.
But Nvidia hasn’t only been the factory. It has also been the bank. Nvidia has become known as the “central bank of AI” because it took equity stakes in the labs, backstopped the neoclouds, and ended the quarter with $118 billion in guarantees, $105 billion of which is credit support for the land, power, and buildings at a single Ohio campus that SoftBank Energy is developing and OpenAI will lease for 20 years. In beer game terms, the brewery has been lending the distributor money to order more beer. Some of those bets are already going bad. This quarter, Nvidia took a $1.8 billion impairment on its stakes in private companies.
Then Jensen Huang resigned as Nvidia’s CEO last week in a scandal, and the board says he isn’t coming back.
That landed on a credit market that was already cooling. Reuters reported that banks lining up to fund AI deals a few weeks earlier were now reluctant to do more, and some data center bond deals got shelved. Meanwhile, CoreWeave’s 9.75% bonds due 2031 were yielding 15.66%. Apollo’s Marc Rowan said that if Nvidia’s “guarantor function” gets taken away, “access to capital for a large number of neoclouds is going to disappear.”
On the earnings call, Colette Kress, Nvidia’s interim CEO, explained that the guarantee starts once the builder has raised its equity and debt and is ready to go:
“If all of that begins and unfortunately they are delayed or they have a challenge in terms of building that, our guarantee doesn’t exist anymore.”
Hmmm. The delays are already showing up. Microsoft trimmed its capital spending forecast last month because “some third party capacity deliveries we expected in Q1 were delayed.”
On the same call, Kress said returning capital to shareholders is a “super top priority for us, first and foremost.” Nvidia spent $22 billion buying back its own stock last quarter and $61 billion so far this fiscal year, and it has $227 billion of authorization left after the board added $150 billion in September. It also raised about $25 billion of new debt this year, and the money its customers owe it doubled to $81 billion. With $43.8 billion of free cash flow last quarter, Nvidia can afford this today. But if I were sitting in the factory seat with $518 billion of commitments, $118 billion of guarantees, $32 billion of neocloud backstops, about $110 billion of stock in other companies (many of them my own customers), and customer IOUs piling up, I’d be hoarding cash right now. Instead, the factory is tightening the terms on what it backs for the distributors while buying its own stock. It’s hard for me to read that as anything other than an effort to support a stock that was down 24% for the month going into earnings.
Nvidia ended the quarter with $53.5 billion of cash and short-term investments against $32.4 billion of debt, which leaves about $21 billion of net cash. That’s down from $41 billion at the start of the fiscal year. It also holds $44.2 billion of stock in public companies, which brings its net liquidity to about $65 billion. As long as things are fine, none of this matters. Nvidia is generating over $40 billion of free cash flow a quarter, but it’s spending all of it, and then some, on buybacks, dividends, and stakes in other companies. But if orders slow, customers stretch out paying those $81 billion of IOUs, the neoclouds it backstops start missing payments, and the stocks it holds fall along with its own, $21 billion of net cash against $518 billion of commitments isn’t much. That could get ugly fast.
None of this is new. During the Railway Mania, a third of the track Parliament authorized between 1844 and 1847 was never built, and the boom ended in the Panic of 1847. In 2001, one analyst estimated that only 7% to 10% of the telecom boom’s fiber was lit, and Global Crossing, 360networks, and WorldCom ended up in bankruptcy. Cheap debt funded the shale boom until OPEC refused to cut production in 2014, and oil fell from over $100 a barrel to about $30, taking more than a hundred producers with it. In 2021, everyone hoarded chips, and by 2023 the memory makers were buried in inventory.
In every one of those cycles, the demand was real. The railways that got built got used, the dark fiber eventually got lit and carried the streaming internet, and shale came back, regardless of how gloomy everyone was. The infrastructure survived, but the capital structure that paid for it got wiped out, and different people owned the assets on the other side.
I’m not a cheerleader or a doomsayer about any of this. I think delays turn a small change in demand into big swings, and something comes along that breaks the cycle. In 1847 it was a financial panic. In 2014 it was an OPEC meeting. This month, it might be Huang’s resignation landing on a credit market that was already cooling. Rowan, who is in the business of lending, pointed out that this is how GMAC and Ford Motor Credit came to exist, and I expect some new financing structure will emerge from this one, too.
If you want to feel this in your gut, play the game. MIT runs the official version, transentis has a free one with no account, and the GenAI Beer Game puts LLMs in every seat so you can watch them play on Hugging Face. You need three friends, a rule that nobody talks, and at least 36 rounds. Before the debrief, have everyone draw what they think customer demand looked like. The upstream players usually draw big swings. But, in the classic game (which I played in 1987 during my first week of business school), it only moved once.
Now, imagine if it moved around, a lot, all the time. Yeah, I know the argument “It’s different this time.” In 2000, I was saying that to the older people in the room. Most nodded along, believing my enthusiastic, visionary bullshit. Sound familiar?
Subscribe via RSS or visit the subscribe page."

45,2026-11-18,"Many people these days worry about AI taking their jobs; not everybody thinks that it is going to replace them as individuals.
It is no secret that the modern workplace has undergone some pretty radical changes this decade, from the ongoing switch to more flexible working to the artificial intelligence boom completely changing the workflow of almost everybody in a corporate role (and sometimes erasing that role in the process). While some would argue these developments have brought about some positive things, it is undeniable that there are a lot of employers out there who see no issue with mistreating those who work with them in the name of advancement. If it manages to save them money, even better.
One of the main things that AI seems to be celebrated for is that it can do a load of workplace tasks that people used to do. The World Economic Forum predicts that by 2030, current trends in the job market, including artificial intelligence, promise to displace up to 92 million jobs worldwide. People in all kinds of industries have been left wondering if the career that they spent years building and preparing for will survive the next few years. If AI makes our lives easier with the boring stuff, that's great, but we still want to get paid.
The fact is that the employee who is happily (or begrudgingly) using an AI agent to help with their workday today could find their company justifying their layoff with that technology tomorrow. There is no counting on it.
An employee has a remote meeting with an AI agent on her laptop. Picture does not depict real parties.
 
A lot of employers seem to like going a step further than a chatbot when it comes to their shiny, new AI features, with many choosing to give their tools a human face to make it really feel like you're interacting with a person. As the trend of people trolling AI interviewers with ridiculous answers has shown, it's not something that everybody enjoys interacting with, and the technology definitely is not as sophisticated as some make it out to be. Still, it is not uncommon to come across it in the modern workplace, and they are more uncanny than even the weirdest human coworker could ever try to be.
This is something that one employee learned the hard way after she lost her job to artificial intelligence that she had inadvertently trained on her likeness. She had been in charge of onboarding new hires, but now her company had decided that there needed to be cuts to the training department.
They offered her six weeks of severance, but only if she signed and agreed to let them use her AI clone in their onboarding process. She wasn't sure if she should take the money that she needed or make a protest against what they were trying to do.
A group of employees in an onboarding meeting. Representative only.
 
Want More? Follow Us and Add Us as a Preferred Source on Google."

By Gareth Hutchens
 Now we have officially entered a 'Sahm Recession' where the 3-month average unemployment rate is 0.5 percentage points higher than the lowest unemployment rate over the past 12 months. This likely indicates that the Australian economy is experiencing the first months of a recession, and with retailers closing up and more companies announcing layoffs, it looks like it'll only get worse from here....
- Charles
Hi Charles,
We haven't entered a Sahm Recession in Australia (yet).
When the RBA played around with the concept a few years ago, its researchers concluded that the Sahm rule, when applied to Australian conditions, would be based on an increase in unemployment of 0.75 percentage points, not 0.5.
So, if a 3-month moving average of the unemployment rate increases by 0.75 percentage points since its lowest 3-month moving average in the last 12 months, then the Sahm rule will signal we're in recession.
By my calculations, we're not there yet. A 3-month moving average of the unemployment rate has increased by 0.6 percentage points since its lowest point.

By Gareth Hutchens
 The ABS has ‘updated’ its September unemployment figure from 4.6% to 4.7%. As the RBA board was clearly split on this month’s rise , I wonder what effect the correct figure would have had ?
- Phillip
Hi Phillip,
The RBA Board would be well aware of the rubbery nature of the labour force statistics, so they would have taken that into consideration (one assumes).
And this is something that's worth highlighting, because it's one of the things that the media doesn't communicate very well, about the unemployment figures: the ABS conducts its labour force survey every month, but every month when it gets its new survey data it will also run the ruler over the data from the last few months and tweak some of that historical data a little bit to align the data with updated population estimates and survey methods.
And that leads to a situation where historical news stories don't always match the data that the ABS has most recently published.
For example, in September I wrote this piece, which said the unemployment rate lifted to 4.6% in August.
But the next month in October, I had to write that the unemployment rate ""held steady at 4.7%"" in September, ""after the jobless rate was revised upwards.""
So, the ABS was telling us, with that September data release, that it had upwardly revised its August unemployment rate from 4.6% to 4.7%, and that meant that my piece from September now looks wrong.
That's one of the hazards of reporting on data that is based on a sample of the population.
Remember, the labour force survey has a sample size of approximately 24,000 dwellings resulting in a sample of approximately 50,000 people, and the ABS extrapolates from that sample to estimate what the national unemployment rate probably is. So, to keep its sample aligned with its estimates of population growth, it regularly has to make little adjustments to the last few months of data as each new instalment in the survey gets published.
The RBA knows all about this issue with the labour force survey.
But anyway, for readers, the best piece of advice I can give is, if you want to know what the unemployment rate is, look at the most recent news story, or the most recent ABS release, because those numbers will be the ""most correct"" numbers at that point in time.

By David Taylor
Several leading economists have now reviewed the latest economic data and warn Australia is either already in a recession or risks slipping into one.
AMP's chief economist is the latest and has told the ABC, ""Data is going in the direction of recession.""
But there's a caveat.
He says the economic data looked equally as bad in 2001 and 2008 and, on both occasions, Australia avoided a technical recession.
A recession is generally defined as two consecutive quarters of negative economic growth.
Technically a recession cannot be called until the middle of next year.
That's because gross domestic product or GDP would need to be negative for the December and March quarters, and that would not be made public until towards the middle of next year.
Earlier today Betashares chief economist, David Bassanese, warned, ""consecutive large employment declines might suggest the economy already has one foot in recession.""
""Given weak productivity growth, negative employment growth suggests GDP growth has already turned negative.""
And moments after that, HSBC's chief economist Paul Bloxham wrote, ""There is an increasing likelihood that the economy will tip into a recession.""
Key Event
By David Taylor
HSBC is the latest financial institution to flag the risk of Australia slipping into recession.
A recession is regarded as two consecutive quarters of negative economic growth.
HSBC's chief economist, Paul Bloxham, said ""Today's jobs figures were much weaker than the market had been expecting. A part of the story with the monthly labour market figures is always that they are very volatile month-to-month (and therefore hard to predict).""
But, he says, ""... taken together with the collection of other timely indicators, the economy looks to be weakening quickly, and seemingly more quickly than the RBA had been forecasting in the set of forecasts it published just over two weeks ago.""
He adds that, ""Other timely indicators are also suggesting that the economy is tipping into a downturn, with surveyed business conditions tracking negative in the past three months, business and consumer confidence falling recently to low levels and forward orders weakening sharply too.""
Which leads Mr Bloxham to conclude that ""There is an increasing likelihood that the economy will tip into a recession.""

Here are the key takeaways from the Commonwealth Bank's latest note on the unemployment rate and interest rates:
■
The October 2026   labour force survey was universally a weak report.
■
Employment fell by   43.5k in contrast to expectations of a gain.
■
The unemployment rate   lifted to 4.9% despite a fall in the participation rate.
■
Today’s data suggests   the labour market is now at least back into balance but the risks have risen   that we could see the labour market loosen further and faster than expected.
■
Monthly labour force   data is volatile and has become more volatile in recent years. Another month   of data will help determine how quick and how much the labour market is   loosening.
■
The risks of a larger   loosening ahead have increased but upside inflation risks remain. This    make the tradeoffs between inflation and employment worse for the RBA.
■
We continue to see the   RBA on hold from here at 4.85%. There are many moving parts in the Australian   economy now; household spending is slowing, the housing market is correcting   and the labour market is now turning. But inflation has been the priority and   will take time to moderate. We see the RBA remaining on the sidelines in   December as they interpret recent moves in the economic data.

The weakness is concentrated at the margins. Part-time jobs accounted for almost all of the fall in employment, and youth unemployment is at its highest since 2021. Unemployment also rose in every state except New South Wales, with Victoria and Tasmania above 5%.
For the RBA, this lands on a board already split over whether policy has gone far enough. Elevated underlying inflation and wage growth rule out a pivot to easing, but this release all but closes the door on a December hike and shifts the debate to how long rates stay at their peak.

Here's what the ABS has had to say about the jump in Australia's unemployment rate:
The seasonally adjusted unemployment rate rose to 4.9 per cent in October, according to the latest data from the Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS).
This is the highest unemployment rate since October 2021.
''In October, there were 44,000 less employed people and 18,000 more unemployed people,"" ABS head of labour statistics Sean Crick said.
''Similar to last month, we had a higher proportion of people remaining unemployed than we would usually see in October in the post-COVID era.
''The youth unemployment rate – that is, for people aged between 15 and 24 – increased to 11.3% in October from 11.1% in September. This is the highest it’s been since October 2021. In the post-COVID period (from May 2022 onwards), the youth unemployment rate has averaged 9.2%.""

By Adelaide Miller
A new study of migrant working conditions in Australia has exposed large-scale impunity for exploitation throughout Australia.
The Migrant Justice Institute (MJI) study, the largest of its kind to date, found that employers responsible for the worst cases of wage theft are the least likely to face consequences.
Researchers analysed almost 10,000 migrant responses from those who worked on a temporary visa, including international students, sponsored workers and backpackers.
Six out of seven underpaid migrants never tried to get the wages they were owed. 
The report found a fear of losing their job or visa keeps the overwhelming majority silent, with many viewing legal processes as too complex and unlikely to deliver results.
""For international students and other migrants, underpayment is now the expected price of a job in Australia,"" says co-author Bassina Farbenblum, Associate Professor of UNSW Law & Justice and Co-Executive Director of MJI.
""When your employer controls next week’s shifts and speaking up could jeopardise your job and your visa, putting up with wage theft in silence is often the rational choice.""
Co-author Laurie Berg, Associate Professor of University of Technology Sydney Law and Co-Executive Director of MJI says ""government enforcement has been limited"", meaning ""dishonest employers have no realistic expectation of getting caught"".
""Wage theft from migrants remains cost-free for the businesses that profit from it across many industries.""
Migrants who were underpaid the most per hour were least likely to take action.
Despite three in five underpaid migrant workers knowing about the Fair Work Ombudsman, only 3% approached the regulator.
After two years of operation, only one business was listed on the Prohibited Employer Register.  
""Government enforcement must be substantially ramped up. The Fair Work Ombudsman needs to enforce the law at scale where dishonest employers expect to be caught. Wage recovery processes must be accessible and reliably deliver results,"" says Berg.
""Government must urgently create a system that makes it safe and worthwhile for migrants to speak up. The research has sounded the alarm on widespread exploitation of international students. It’s critical that universities and government now partner to get students the help and protections they need,"" says Farbenblum.
Key Event
By Adelaide Miller
On the topic of housing, new laws in Victoria that require home sellers to publish the price they want to sell at appear to be working.
The laws aim to reduce underquoting and help buyers know what a property will sell for by requiring a disclosed ""reserve"" price.
Property platform Homer has compiled the data for the first month since the practice became law in Victoria, for sales from October 16.
""The data does suggest transparency is working,"" Homer chief executive Henry Pedersen says.
He says properties that sold at auction went for a median of 0.9% above the published reserve price.
""So buyers and sellers are landing very close to what was advertised,"" he says.

47,2026-11-18,"I recently wrote an article that discussed why Windows Terminal was still my home for PowerShell, WSL and SSH sessions, despite there being plenty of alternatives out there. It's one of the better default apps in Windows, but being good enough for a default is different than being the best option out there. I tried four different options beyond the one that came with the OS, and one rose above the rest pretty convincingly.
Dual-boot, VMs, and WSL2 walk into a Windows PC...
WezTerm looks pretty unassuming on the surface, but it's incredibly capable under the hood. Its best feature is the fact that it's a multiplexer, which means splits, tabs, and any other kind of named workspace are built in and able to be used concurrently. Windows doesn't have a tmux equivalent, and this is the first terminal I've found that's able to properly replicate what it can do.
SSH functionality is there and also has a killer feature of its own: when you connect to a remote host and open new tabs, those tabs do not need to be authenticated individually again. This is something I run into all the time running a home lab, and it's one of the things that made this terminal a mainstay on my main workstation. Keeping a persistent SSH workspace between windows on Windows is worth trying WezTerm for alone.
Configuration happens all within one Lua file, which for some people will certainly be a pro, but I'm edging towards con. Writing some code to change a font isn't exactly my first choice, but the power of being able to carry my configuration file between Windows, macOS and Linux is a great feature.

It's a must-have tool for terminal-heavy tasks
Warp is far from your standard terminal. Most of the other terminals on this list stick pretty closely to what we think acts as a terminal, but instead of being a continuous scroll of text, Warp groups each command and its output into a block. These blocks can be copied, searched, shared, and manipulated as a unit. Its input line is a lot more like a code editor than a prompt, and it gets a lot of its feel from being attuned more towards being an ""agentic development environment"" where an AI agent can run commands and edit code for you. If that's your forte, then Warp is for you. It's not something I would personally replace my stock terminal client with though.

Microsoft have made a terminal that doesn't feel like it needs to be replaced
Tabby is another cross-platform terminal option that's built on Electron, and its biggest strength by far is remote connections. The saving of SSH profiles and SFTP panel is an excellent feature for a home lab, but one of its more underrated features is serial support. If you manage network gear over a console cable or regularly flash microcontrollers, the serial support alone could justify installing it. The trade-off is the one that comes with every Electron app.
With just one tab open in Tabby, it was using over 300MB of my system memory. When you compare that to the ~40MB that both WezTerm and Windows Terminal use, it seems a bit ridiculous. This more or less disqualifies it for my use personally, but it doesn't make it a bad terminal. If you use a serial connection often and an SFTP panel is a must, I can see how you can look past its heaviness.

They're just that good!
If Tabby is heavier for the sake of more features, Alacritty is minimalist for the sake of being fast. It's stripped of pretty much everything that isn't used to make the window and text drawing quicker. It also has no tabs or splits built-in, which makes it bad for PowerShell use but it lacks those features because it expects you to use a multiplexer or window manager on your own. This works if you're working within WSL where tmux or Zellij fill those voids, but if you're doing anything else in a Windows environment, it's not worth it over the stock option.
I've set up these terminal utilities on every Linux system in my arsenal
Despite the fact that they have some great features, after trying each of these terminals, going back to the stock Windows Terminal didn't feel like the total downgrade I thought it would. Besides WezTerm, I'd still reach for it over the other alternatives because it nails the basics, though the third-party options are great for more specific use cases. It's installed by default, open-source, detects your shells automatically, supports profiles, works flawlessly with WSL, and is still being updated to this day.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

48,2026-11-18,"Nvidia stock tokens reached $401.9 million in combined value as Jensen Huang stepped down and the chipmaker posted record quarterly revenue.
The combined market value of tokenized stocks tied to Nvidia shares rose about 159% from Nov. 8 to Tuesday, while the chipmaker’s Nasdaq-listed shares fell nearly 17% from their last close before co-founder Jensen Huang publicly admitted to an extramarital affair.
The value of Nvidia (NVDA) tokens tracked by Token Terminal rose from $155 million on Nov. 8 to $401.9 million by Tuesday.
Nvidia shed about $750 billion in market value, falling from $4.5 trillion at the Nov. 6 close to $3.75 trillion at Tuesday’s close, according to Yahoo Finance data.
Huang acknowledged the affair in a message to Nvidia employees on Nov. 8, nearly two weeks after The New York Times reported that US intelligence officials suspected Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang of spying for Beijing. China denied the allegations.
Related: ARK sells $85M in Nvidia shares ahead of chipmaker’s Q3 earnings results
Nvidia supplies chips that cloud providers such as Google Cloud and Microsoft Azure use to train and run AI models, making its earnings a gauge of global spending on AI infrastructure.
Tokenized stocks are blockchain tokens designed to track the price of a share. In one common setup, an issuer creates tokens backed by shares held by a custodian.
Binance, for example, lists an Nvidia token that can trade when Nasdaq is closed. It gives investors exposure to Nvidia’s share price without shareholder rights. The market value can rise as more tokens enter circulation, even if Nvidia’s share price falls.
Huang resigned as president and CEO of Nvidia and stepped down from the company’s board Thursday. Nvidia named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.
On Tuesday, Nvidia’s board said it “does not intend to reinstate” Huang, shortly after the chipmaker reported record third-quarter revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year earlier and above Wall Street estimates. Data center sales more than doubled to $107.6 billion, accounting for about 93% of total revenue. Nvidia forecast fourth-quarter sales of about $130 billion.
Nvidia’s shares rose 2.82% to $159.80 in Wednesday premarket trading as of 12:50 pm UTC (07:50 am ET), according to Yahoo Finance.

Nvidia's board have indicated that they aren't about to do a big u-turn and reappoint recently resigned CEO Jensen Huang, following his departure after multiple alleged instances of extramarital cavorting were reported by the press. Since his exit last week, Huang has indicated he'd be open to making such a return, were it deemed best for both the company and AMERICA.
""On November 12th, our board of directors announced that after disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards, and cultural norms, Jensen Huang voluntarily resigned his positions as president, CEO, and board member,"" Nvidia chairman Harvey Jones said during an investors call on November 17th (thanks, Percieving Calls Gamer). ""The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.""
That last bit seems likely to have been said with the intention of quieting down some of the speculation around a Huang return that's been bubbling away since he removed his bum from the black and green throne and that the exec embraced in a statement put out yesterday. ""I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at Nvidia, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life,"" he wrote. ""I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. If the board of directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honoured to answer these calls and return as CEO.""

One of Huang's alleged affairs was with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy. The Chinese government have denied the accusations of espionage.

55,2026-11-18,"Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit
Brand spending on creator marketing is set to grow twice as fast as spending on advertising as a whole over the next five years, according to advertising research firm Madison & Wall.
The consultancy estimated that creator spending will rise 12% each year until 2030, versus a 6% increase in ad spending each year between 2025 and 2030. In 2026, the researcher estimates that marketers will spend $116 billion on creator activity.
Driving that growth is ad spend intended to boost or promote creator content to a larger audience — which accounted for 44%, or $52 billion, of global creator marketing spending in 2026 — and brands paying creators to produce advertising assets for use in CTV, display and paid social contexts. 
The latter accounted for 12% of global creator spending in 2026, but will rise 16% each year until the end of the decade, making it the fastest-growing segment of the creator economy. Traditional sponsored posts or ambassador relationships accounted for 28% of creator spending.
Brands attempting to reach large audiences on social platforms have begun to look to creators not just as a distribution point, but as a source of creative production that can meet the need for a diverse set of creative assets at a lower price point than traditional agency methods.
Unilever CEO Fernando Fernandez, for example, recently said that the CPG giant’s social content had to be constantly refreshed to prevent it from going stale. “The lifespan of a video today is four days,” he said during the firm’s recent Capital Markets Day. “Content has to be renovated because after four days you don’t have diminishing levels of return, you have negative marginal return.”
“The reason we see brands engaging with this is just because it’s cheaper,” said Luke Stillman, managing director at Madison & Wall. “Everybody is trying to squeeze down all those non-working expenses as much as possible.”
As creator rosters get larger — Unilever now works with around 500,000 — the need to manage such programs has driven spending on specialist agencies, platforms and talent representation. Spending on creator intermediaries (fees for specialist agencies, platforms, or talent representation) accounted for 8% of global creator spending.
Stillman said he expected to see spending on that slice grow further in coming years. “Identifying the right creators, contracting with all those creators, managing non-delivery and legal issues and payment issues are really complex, and so there’s plenty of space for ancillary services there,” he said.
“We’re seeing an increase on budgets year-on-year from our clients,” said Andy Ryan, co-founder at social and influencer agency Komodo. Ryan estimated client spending on creator marketing had increased 20%-30% in 2026.
Olivia Wedderburn, head of influence at Born Social, saw an even bigger jump, estimating that client spending on creator activity had risen 50% compared with last year. “I would anticipate that will only continue to grow quite heavily,” she said. 
The U.S. market accounted for 43% of creator spending last year, per Madison & Wall estimates, while the U.K. and Germany are the next-largest, at 5% and 4% respectively. The U.S. will see spending grow 15% this year. India was the fastest growing market, at 20%.
Madison & Wall’s estimate for asset-led spending covered several related practices: whitelisting, fees for user-generated content, content licensing, and fees for producing creative assets.
However brands are commissioning it, creator-led production is on the rise. “More of our brand partners are bringing creators in as the production engine for their paid media, not just their organic social,” said Nicholas Spiro, chief commercial officer at Viral Nation.
While cost is a major factor, as Stillman noted, agency marketers also believe creator-made work can engage audiences more effectively than an ad made for the same environment by a creative director.
“Creator-made assets simply connect best with potential customers on a human level,” said Spiro. “They tend to drive performance better than traditional brand creative, and they can be more measurable for certain objectives than organic creator-only content.”
While creator marketing was once dominated by apparel and beauty brands, healthcare, automotive and finance advertisers now spend on creator activity too.
“Where some sectors like fashion and beauty have been on this for a number of years, and therefore they’re already doing a fantastic job at it… some sectors [were] behind and they’re catching up and are now throwing more at it,” said Ryan.
“An ad can make a claim. A parent can prove it,” said Carmen Graham, CMO at Genexa, a youth-targeted healthcare brand. 
“Parent creators can also do what we can’t: show the product in real use, in their home. The messier and less art-directed the medicine cabinet, the more we see ourselves and our customer in the content,” she said.
“Our bet is that this commitment to being parent-first will drive the trust this category requires — especially if you’re switching to a new product to support you in the most dire moments of parenthood.”
Mediaplus and Publicis clients, including Banco Santander, have paused TikTok spending in wake of investigations into abuse images circulating on the video platform.
A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?
In Q3 earnings, The Trade Desk’s leadership reveals it is searching for answers.

Follow @Digiday for the latest news, insider access to events and more."

58,2026-11-18,"Before 2019, the last place you'd see Mark Jessup was on a cruise ship.
As the long-standing stereotype goes, ""I thought it was just for old people,"" the 47-year-old skincare company owner recalled thinking. The fear of seasickness certainly didn't entice him either.
Actually, nothing did — until his coworkers dragged him, then an unwilling 39-year-old post-office worker, on his first vacation at sea in late 2019 aboard Carnival Sunshine.
""The rest was history,"" Jessup, who's now a travel content creator known on social media as Mark J, told Business Insider. Since then (and even following an industry-wide pause amid the COVID-19 pandemic), the cynic-turned-fan has been on 56 floating resorts.
Jessup cruises every month, usually on four- to seven-day voyages, and even relocated from Massachusetts to Florida three years ago, both for the weather and to be closer to cruise ports. He enjoys the convenience of the onboard gym and food, as well as the ability to unplug from his phone.
""I like traveling, and I realized it was just so much easier for me to go on a cruise,"" Jessup said. ""My sons were getting older, and I was wanting to do something more for me. Cruising was my escape to figure out who I was and what I wanted.""
Meanwhile, the vacation-at-sea industry seems to be facing a perpetual public-relations crisis.
Earlier this month, the ""never-ending cruise"" Villa Vie's Odyssey abruptly suspended operations, leaving more than 200 passengers stranded in Cambodia. A few months before that, there was a deadly multi-country hantavirus outbreak linked to a cruise ship.
Just last year, the 2025 Netflix documentary ""Trainwreck: Poop Cruise"" dove into what happened aboard the Carnival Triumph after an engine fire left about 4,000 people stranded in 2013. The title speaks for itself. In a statement, Carnival called the incident a ""teachable moment for the entire cruise industry"" and said it spent millions upgrading its fleet after investigating the issues that led to the disaster.
Then, of course, there are the seemingly annual nightmarish ""man overboard"" headlines and reports about norovirus outbreaks on cruises.
Yet, the cruise industry sustains. To understand the cruise boom amid its challenges, you have to understand how social media, product innovation, and affordability have built a travel-industry fortress so formidable, even a federally mandated, industry-wide shutdown during a pandemic couldn't shutter it for good.
It's no secret that I, a seasoned cruise reporter, have had my apprehensions about cruising, for reasons quite similar to Jessup's. Yet, throughout my career, I've become especially partial to the behemoth ships of companies like Royal Caribbean and Norwegian, topped with extras like splashy waterparks, exciting Cirque du Soleil-like productions, and wine bars with a cool downtown panache — all bait for younger cruisers that might've thought we were ""above"" cruising.
""People would say that cruising was for old people, but the innovation that's come out … I think is why it's capturing the attention of the people,"" Austin Maxwell, a 31-year-old cruise content creator who's been on 43 unique ships across 16 cruise lines, told Business Insider.
Unsurprisingly, these flashy amenities have become irresistible to travelers, and it's part of what's keeping the industry afloat despite the negative headlines.
The cruise industry is seeing a historic boom in popularity.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
AAA estimates that a record 22.4 million Americans will cruise in 2027, a 3.6% increase from the year prior — and the fifth year in a row AAA has projected a record increase. By comparison, 14.2 million Americans cruised in 2019.
Much of this growth can be attributed to newcomers: According to a 2025 Cruise Lines International Association report, 31% of cruisers globally over the prior two years were first-timers — up from 24% in 2019. For cruise giants like Royal Caribbean Group, this surge means about 60% of its travelers are now either new to cruising or to the brand, Naftali Holtz, the company's CFO, told investors in mid-2025.
""I have a lot of people that have always done land trips, and they're resistant to the idea of going on a cruise,"" Bitsy Clayton, a 63-year-old luxury travel agent of almost 24 years, told Business Insider. ""Once people decide to do it, I think they're surprised at how much they like it.""
Clayton's clients, most of whom are between the ages of 50 and 70, gravitate toward the likes of Silversea, Regent Seven Seas, Crystal, and Seabourn — smaller ultra-luxury ships with high-touch service. The vast majority of her clients are repeat cruisers, including some who've spent $250,000 on a single at-sea vacation.
Are cruise lovers just ignoring the risks and potential horrors at sea? Maybe — but headlines only tell a small part of the story.
Clayton said it's ""pretty rare"" for her clients to bring up these types of nightmare incidents. ""It can happen on any vessel, but I think on the smaller ships, you've got more staff, a lot more attention to detail, and more things that could curtail something escalating,"" she said.
""Cruising could be like 10% bad and 90% good, but everybody's only going to focus on the 10%,"" Jessup said, explaining why the negative headlines don't affect his decision to keep cruising.
Instead, he focuses on what he can control at sea, like decreasing his chances of getting sick aboard by avoiding touching stairwell railings or the tongs at the buffet. He also has an extensive cabin-cleaning routine, which includes bed-bug checks, sanitizing all high-touch surfaces with cleaning wipes, and bringing his own bed sheets and pillowcases.
Cruises have protective measures in place to prevent sickness, too. Hand-sanitizer bottles have become a fixture on floating resorts, luxury or not. Go on a giant Royal Caribbean cruise, and you'll likely see crew in fast food-shaped costumes singing ""Washy washy!"" on repeat by the buffet entrance.
The World Health Organization also has global standards around health screenings and sanitation requirements that cruise lines are expected to follow to further help control disease and other hazards at sea.
Of course, safety and health concerns will remain. Likely no amount of public relations work can make the world forget its norovirus- and COVID-19-era ""floating petri dish"" reputation.
But remember: Thousands of cruise voyages happen every year, and the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported nine at-sea norovirus outbreaks this year.
It's hard not to be enticed by floating resorts when you see the (relatively) low ticket prices.
According to personal finance publication Motley Fool Money, a two-person, weeklong domestic trip costs an average of $3,225 (or about $1,613 per person), including airfare, food, and lodging. According to AAA, a weeklong cruise with a mass-market company like Royal Caribbean or Carnival starts at an average of $400 per person. With these prices, a cruise may seem like a no-brainer for many travelers facing soaring airfare and gas prices.
Along with new onboard ice-skating rinks, drone shows, and virtual reality arcades, the ethos of cruising has remained the same: Your fare includes a place to sleep, as much food as your heart desires, a list of amenities, and the chance to see several destinations without unpacking and repacking.
Makes planning a traditional land-based trip sound like a logistical (and financial) nightmare, doesn't it?
This convenience is what ultimately drew Carly Caramanna, a 40-year-old travel writer, to life at sea. Despite growing up in a cruising family, she fell out of love with cruises in her 20s.
""I would go to Europe for a week and change cities and hotels four times,"" she said of that period, recalling when she used to feel ""disenchanted"" by cruises. However, time, age, and a desire for a less ""exhausting"" travel style have since healed her relationship with cruises: She said she now goes on six to seven cruises a year.
""I can have amazing food, be treated like royalty by these amazing team members, and I don't have to hotel change every other day,"" said Caramanna. ""You can literally pay for your cruise, go on it, and be done, and I think there's an appeal to that style of travel.""
And for what it's worth: I've been cruising for work since 2021, and not once have I caught norovirus, hantavirus, or COVID-19 — nor have I fallen overboard or been left stranded in a foreign country.
The biggest ""nightmare"" situation I've experienced thus far is going home with a gnarly sunburn and one too many souvenirs.
Jump to"

60,2026-11-18,"Lawsuit Seeks Class Certification and Unspecified Damages The complaint was filed in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of California in San Jose, according to court records cited in the lawsuit. The case, identified as 5:26-cv-13758, seeks to represent those who purchased Intel common stock between July 23 and October 29, 2026, inclusive [2]. Reuters reported that a Michigan pension fund filed the proposed class action against Intel, CEO Lip-Bu Tan and CFO David Zinsner on November 13 in federal court in San Jose [3]. The defendants have not admitted wrongdoing.Plaintiffs Allege Omissions on PC Slump, Component Prices and Substrate Constraints The complaint alleges that positive impacts to Intel's business and financial results resulting from data center construction and other aspects of the AI boom were more than offset by the PC sales slump [4]. Investment advisors caution that when the media are full of stories about how well things are going for a company, people tend to assume that it is a guaranteed success, and typically that is when investors get hurt [5]. A fast, cost-effective way to find out what a company does and whether an investor would like to invest in it is to review the annual report (10-K), which discloses whether the company is making money, how it has done over the past five years, and whether sales are growing [6].Earnings Report Preceded Record Single-Day Stock Decline Intel issued weak fourth-quarter guidance on the 29th, and the stock plummeted 28.6% the following day, erasing roughly $140 billion in market value [7]. The next day, Intel shares fell 28.6%, closing at $65.76 after ending the previous session at $92.07 [8]. The complaint describes the move as the largest single-day decline in Intel common stock in more than 50 years [8]. Intel shares declined approximately 5% in premarket trading following the shareholder class-action lawsuit [9]. The market reaction reflected the impact of a deepening PC slump exposed by a weaker-than-expected sales forecast [10].Case Seeks Damages as Intel Has Not Publicly Responded The lawsuit seeks class-action status for shareholders who purchased Intel common stock between July 23 and October 29, 2026 [2]. Some legal observers note that many stock investments can become instruments for filing lawsuits, with some plaintiffs buying stocks knowing that a law firm may eventually sue the companies [11]. A veteran investment advisor has written that after a long period of bad news, when more bad news no longer lowers the price, that could be an opportunity for buyers, while warning that coming down from very high to high feels like a big drop but may not be enough to constitute a bargain [12].Conclusion: Class Action Highlights Disclosure Risks in a Volatile Semiconductor Market The Intel lawsuit is part of a broader pattern of shareholder litigation against major technology companies following significant stock price declines. The complaint alleges that positive impacts to Intel's business and financial results resulting from data center construction and other aspects of the AI boom were more than offset by the PC sales slump [4]. Investment advisors caution that when the media are full of stories about how well things are going for a company, people tend to assume that it is a guaranteed success, and typically that is when investors get hurt [5]. A fast, cost-effective way to find out what a company does and whether an investor would like to invest in it is to review the annual report (10-K), which discloses whether the company is making money, how it has done over the past five years, and whether sales are growing [6].Earnings Report Preceded Record Single-Day Stock Decline Intel issued weak fourth-quarter guidance on the 29th, and the stock plummeted 28.6% the following day, erasing roughly $140 billion in market value [7]. The next day, Intel shares fell 28.6%, closing at $65.76 after ending the previous session at $92.07 [8]. The complaint describes the move as the largest single-day decline in Intel common stock in more than 50 years [8]. The market reaction reflected the impact of a deepening PC slump exposed by a weaker-than-expected sales forecast [10].Case Seeks Damages as Intel Has Not Publicly Responded The lawsuit seeks class-action status for shareholders who purchased Intel common stock between July 23 and October 29, 2026 [2]. Some legal observers note that many stock investments can become instruments for filing lawsuits, with some plaintiffs buying stocks knowing that a law firm may eventually sue the companies [11].
