61,2026-11-18,"Feeling that chill in the air, the rising number of Oscars-hopeful movie releases in theaters, and the unmistakable threat promise of Mariah Carey's ""All I Want for Christmas is You"" playing in every department store you enter for the foreseeable future? Yep, you know what that means: The holiday season is officially upon us and we're positively giddy with Yuletide cheer. Of course, we're even more excited about finally having an excuse to pop on our favorite films and celebrate the festivities properly.
For those of us who practically grew up on one particular classic, that involves ringing in the holidays with ""A Christmas Story,"" arguably the most quintessential Christmas movie of them all. There are approximately a hundred different reasons why this of all movies captures the essence of the season the most — or, more accurately, the childlike wonder and innocence we all used to have come November of every single year. The seemingly endless wait for school to end and the fun to begin. The breathless anticipation every time our parents left the house for some clandestine gift shopping. The snowball fights and snow days and warm evenings by the fire, for those of us fortunate enough to live in more wintry climates.
""A Christmas Story"" takes all those evocative feelings and wraps them inside a snow globe of nostalgia ... with a little commentary on crass commercialism, nuclear family dynamics, and some good ol' fashioned childhood bullying thrown in for good measure. Forty three years later, it's a pop-culture staple that endures. And one particular quote about a certain Red Ryder BB gun goes a long way towards explaining why.
""You'll shoot your eye out."" — Ralph's mother.
Ah, the life-and-death stakes of being a child during the holidays. Every single item on our list is a must-have present or the world will end forever. Each day is fraught with the most impossible homework assignments we need to finish before we can have fun with friends. And every impulsive misstep or moment of misbehavior comes with the possibility of turning Santa Claus himself against us. For Ralphie (Peter Billingsley), the precocious 9-year-old boy living in 1940s Indiana retelling the events of ""A Christmas Story"" to us as an adult (narrated by Jean Shepherd), it all comes down to those five crushing words that destroy all his holiday hopes in one fell swoop.
""You'll shoot your eye out,"" of course, refers to his mother's (Melinda Dillon) appalled reaction to the #1 item on his Christmas list: an Official Red Ryder Carbine-Action 200-Shot Range Model Air Rifle with a compass in the stock and a sundial, to put it plainly. It's his white whale, the forbidden fruit that torments and dazzles poor Ralphie for the entirety of the film's runtime. And when he finally works up the nerve to ask his mom for her blessing, well, she sensibly points out exactly what could go wrong with such a violent ""toy.""
It's a perfect encapsulation of the beating heart of ""A Christmas Story"" and why multiple generations have gravitated towards this movie. It brings us right back to that indescribable feeling of being eight or nine or 10, with an entire world of possibility ahead ... if only the adults would let us free. After countless re-watches, well, the hilarious (and predictable) punchline proves kids don't always know best, after all."
62,2026-11-18,"Phew, praise his playable Popeliness
Funky feudal family frolic Crusader Kings 3 got its second major expansion of the year this week. It's called Silk and Silver (read my impressions), is all about merchant republics, and shipped alongside a beefy free update to the base game which - while largely focused on overhauling economics - also has some funny patch notes and features worth being aware of.
The main features of the free 'Florin' update are outlined by Paradox in the video above, and unless you're really into the nitty gritty details of realm and wealth management, there's one bit of it which stands out. The option to play as a landless adventurer has finally made its way into the base game, as Paradox promised earlier this year. 
This means you can kick off a playthrough as a lowborn leader of a travelling band or a wandering noble with claims on realms ruled by others. Originally locked behind the Roads to Power DLC, the option to start off this way or end up living if you end up deposed from your throne of choice is a great addition to the story arc of any CK3 run. No longer are you glued to the throne from day one to the day you quit for good - power's a privilege which can be earned and revoked depending on what you get up to. Though, it's worth noting that only three ways of operating as a landless adventurer are being made free to access with this update. You can be an aimless wanderers, sword-toting mercenaries, or scholars. If you want your band to earn their living as explorers, free booters, or legitimists, you'll still need Roads to Power.
As for the rest of the free additions, community manager Jacob Blakey summed them up thusly:
Because this is Crusader Kings, the bug fixes and other tweaks which fill out the update's full patch notes aren't short on lines worthy of a giggle or raised eyebrow. The following have caught my eye on a quick scan through:
As ever, I'm a bit sad that those are gone, even if they had to go.

64,2026-11-18,"
					By Matthew Carey 
Documentary Editor, Awards

	Winners of the IDFA Forum prizes were announced at an awards ceremony in Amsterdam Wednesday, honoring projects presented in the festival’s co-production and co-financing market.

How Love Moves by Pallavi Paul won the Al Jazeera Documentary Co-Production Award, which comes with a $25,000 prize. The award recognizes projects “addressing an urgent issue of our time, presented in the Pitch or Roundtable categories.” How Love Moves is no stranger to the festival: it received IDFA Bertha Fund support through the IBF Classic scheme, in the Development category in 2024 and the Production and post-production category in 2025.

	











	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	Documentary
	



	
		
					Cinephil Boards Alexandru Solomon's Romanian Election Doc 'Small Expectations' - IDFA		
			















	Documentary
	



	
		
					IDFA Artistic Director Isabel Arrate Fernandez On Festival's Competition Lineups, ""Huge"" Interest In Elizabeth Holmes Doc, And AI In Documentaries		
			







	Jurors Mohamed Elmongy and Brigid O’Shea wrote of How Love Moves, “As the selectors of the Al Jazeera Co-Production award we took carefully into consideration the urgency and relevance of each selected project for IDFA Forum as well as the aesthetic, artistic, and creative qualities they hold. We unanimously agreed to give this award to a politically and creatively sophisticated project, exploring the vulnerability of communities deemed dangerous and wrong through state-inflicted violence. In doing so, we believe audiences around the world will have access to a place and the people living there who otherwise we may never encounter, and correct or add a new layer of complexity beyond the mainstream narrative. And more importantly, it is an exciting opportunity to support a woman-director’s voice, we hope at a critical junction in the life of her project.”




			Watch on Deadline		






	The ARTE European Talent Development Award, recognizing a project in development or early production presented in the Forum’s Pitch or Roundtable categories, went to Stitch by Stitch by Alina Baitokova. That award comes with a €10,000 prize.

	Jurors Olga Chernykh, Anne-Laure Négrin, and Hanne Phlypo said of Stitch by Stitch, “We were honored to be invited on the jury and listen to so many powerful stories from around the world. We had a long jury deliberation, and many projects were highly appreciated. We recognized the incredible courage of the filmmakers bringing us these stories. If we could, we would have awarded more, and we hope these projects will find their way to be supported, and maybe already found support here at the international market of IDFA.”

	










	The jurors continued, “However, as we needed to pick only one project, we decided to award a project from a country whose stories are rarely told, with high need in support right now, and to give a director tools to move the project ahead and tell the story that shows people struggling for their life in a rarely seen part of the globalization. It’s a film brought by a promising team, a delicate emerging voice. Therefore, the jury decided to award a film that takes us to Kyrgyzstan, Stitch by Stitch directed by Alina Baitokova, produced by Adilet Karzhoev and Marianna Kaat.”


	         The award for Best Pitch Project comes with a €1,500 prize.

	         The award for Best Roundtable Project is accompanied by a €1,500 prize.

	In other IDFA Forum prizes, the award for Best Rough Cut Project went to Resisting Residents by Nikos Pilos and Arsinoi Pilou.

	“Resisting Residents is a powerful reminder of how cinema can champion what we believe in,” wrote said jurors Chalida Uabumrungjit and Cleo Veger. “This brave documentary captures a community standing firm for their homes, their dignity, and their right to live together, proving that film can be an essential voice for justice and human solidarity. We were moved by the courage of the residents, who take in newcomers, face their own conflicts and fight eviction together, and by a film team honest enough to show all of it. Shot by a two-person crew living inside the community, it’s close, trusting and alive, and we can’t wait to see it finished.”

	The award comes with a €1,500 prize.

Resisting Residents also won the InVision Subtitling Award, presented by Walter Groenewegen, which provides closed captioning and subtitles for a project presented in the Rough Cut category.

	The IDFA DocLab Forum Award for Best Project was claimed by This Pleasant Lady by Victoria Mapplebeck. “The jury unanimously agrees that this project is inspirational and addresses serious subjects in an irreverent but impactful way that will engage with audiences no matter of their cultural background or ages. The spirit of the project and the artist fits the vibe of IDFA DocLab. We’re also now encouraged to create our own fuck you list!” said jury members Min-Wei Kuo and David Massey.

	The award is accompanied by a €1,500 prize. 

	Jurors additionally recognized Time Hunter XR: Colonial Protocols by Mark Mushiva and Daniel Chein. “The jury want to give a Special Mention to a project that really thinks carefully about how the experience will take audiences on a journey that explores themes of time travel, sci-fi, and embodied archive. The project creates an imaginative and engaging experience that brings forward the perspectives of people whose voices deserve to be heard, and histories that deserve to be told.”

65,2026-11-18,"Bitcoin ETF outflows returned after a one-day pause as CryptoQuant warned that Bitcoin had returned to a bearish market phase.
US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) resumed logging net outflows on Tuesday after a brief pause in the bleeding to start the week.
The funds recorded around $240 million in net outflows on Tuesday, with BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) accounting for $210 million, according to Farside Investors data.
IBIT had $76.5 million in net inflows on Monday, ending a $2.35 billion net outflow streak that spanned 11-straight sessions.
US spot Ether (ETH) ETFs also recorded net outflows of $29 million on Tuesday, adding to $8.3 million in withdrawals Monday. The ETH funds have seen net outflows in eight of the last nine trading sessions.
The renewed outflows in crypto ETFs came amid ongoing weakness in Bitcoin (BTC), which briefly dipped below $63,000 on Tuesday, according to CoinGecko.
Bitcoin has returned to a bearish phase amid weakening demand and mounting investor losses, CryptoQuant said in a Tuesday report shared with Cointelegraph. Its Bitcoin Analyst Consensus Index showed sentiment at its most bearish level since November 2025.
Source: CryptoQuant
“Bitcoin needs demand growth in order to recover and rally sustainably,” CryptoQuant said, noting that total Bitcoin demand growth was contracting at its fastest pace since July 1.
Despite the bearish signals, CryptoQuant identified potential price support between $62,000 and $65,000 but cautioned that the indicators did not yet confirm a market bottom.

67,2026-11-18,"
  By Steve Adams |
  at November 18, 2026 2:18pm CDT  
As clubs around the league scour the market for rotation help, the Reds have been receiving trade interest in left-hander Nick Lodolo, per Ken Rosenthal of The Athletic. There’s no indication any kind of deal is close at the moment.
Lodolo, 29 in February, is down to one final year of club control. He’s eligible for arbitration this winter and projected by MLBTR contributor Matt Swartz to earn $5.7MM for the upcoming 2027 campaign.
If the Reds were to trade Lodolo, they’d be selling low. The former No. 7 overall pick and top prospect broke out with a terrific 2025 season that saw him pitch 156 2/3 innings with a 3.33 ERA, 24.3% strikeout rate, 4.8% walk rate and 42.6% ground-ball rate. All of his numbers trended the wrong direction during an injury-marred 2026 campaign, however.
Recurring blister issues have been a problem for Lodolo over the years, and that was again the case in ’26, when he started just 21 games and pitched 108 2/3 frames. Lodolo managed only a 4.97 earned run average in that time. His strikeout rate plummeted to 17.1%, while his walk rate nearly doubled to 9.5%. Lodolo posted a strong 12.4% swinging-strike rate and 32.7% opponents’ chase rate in ’25 but saw those numbers drop to 9% and 28.4%, respectively. Both are below average.
To this point, Lodolo has had an up-and-down career with more low points than highs. When looking at his last three seasons in their entirety, however, he sports a respectable 4.23 ERA with a 22.3% strikeout rate and 7% walk rate. He’s not the clear No. 2-3 arm many scouting reports forecast him to be during his prospect days, but Lodolo has flashed that upside at his best and has been serviceable at his worst. He’d also surely benefit by escaping from the homer-happy confines of Cincinnati’s Great American Ball Park; Lodolo has a career 4.60 ERA and 1.51 HR/9 mark at home, compared to a 3.85 ERA and 1.17 HR/9 on the road.
Trading Lodolo would probably net the Reds some mid-level prospect capital but would also exacerbate an existing need for starting pitching. Cincinnati will be without Hunter Greene for most or all of the 2027 season after he underwent Tommy John surgery in July. Brady Singer became a free agent at season’s end. Chase Burns, Andrew Abbott and Lodolo are their most-established starters.
The fourth and fifth spots right now are in line to be filled by a combination of Rhett Lowder, Brandon Williamson, Chase Petty and Jose Franco. Lowder, like Lodolo, is a former No. 7 overall pick but hasn’t broken out in the big leagues yet. Williamson and Petty were also high draft picks and prospects of note, but the former has repeatedly been beset by injuries while the latter struggled as a starter and was moved to the ‘pen in Triple-A this season.
The Reds probably already need to bring in multiple rotation arms — certainly at least one. Lodolo’s stock isn’t high enough right now to bring back a controllable, MLB-ready replacement of much note, and his salary isn’t going to be steep enough that it’d free the Reds to reinvest it in a notable free agent. The looming uncertainty surrounding the upcoming lockout is another factor to consider; interested clubs may well want to take the Reds’ temperature when it comes to Lodolo’s availability, but most teams figure to hold off on surrendering young talent for players who’ll be free agents post-2027 until they know for certain whether a full season will be played next year.
All of that could combine to make a Lodolo trade tricky at present, but he’s a name worth filing away — particularly if the league and the MLBPA can hammer out a new agreement that avoids lost games next year.

68,2026-11-18,"South Africa seeks billions in new investment to revive weak growth and tackle unemployment crisis.
Africa’s most industrialised economy faces an unemployment crisis.
And last year, more foreign investment left South Africa than came in.
Years of power shortages, crumbling infrastructure and corruption have shaken confidence.
One estimate says South Africa needs an additional $75bn a year to revive growth.
President Cyril Ramaphosa is courting US tech giants to help make South Africa an AI hub.
But Washington has hit South Africa with steep tariffs and is cutting its duty-free access to the US market.
Pretoria is increasingly looking to China.
So, can South Africa win back investors and turn promises into jobs?

69,2026-11-18,"Cal-Maine reports avian influenza at Florida site, with 2.7 percent of its total flock affected.
Save
Share
Cal-Maine Foods, the largest egg producer in the United States, has said it would cull 1.8 million birds after avian influenza was detected at one of its facilities in Florida.
On Wednesday, the company said the 1.4 million laying hens and 360,000 young hens being culled account for 2.7 percent of its flock. Production at the facility will be temporarily halted.
Cal-Maine said in a statement that it would coordinate with other production and distribution facilities to minimise disruption across the company.
The risk bird flu poses to human health is low. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), there have been 74 human cases since February 2024, with no evidence of human-to-human transmission.
However, the virus continues to pose a significant challenge to the US poultry and egg industries. Some 21.5 million birds have been affected this year, following a historic surge in outbreaks in late 2024 and early 2025 that sharply reduced the number of egg-laying hens.
Approximately 50.7 million laying hens were lost between mid-October 2024 and early March 2025, when egg prices in the US reached a record high of $6.23 per dozen.
The current average retail price of eggs is $2.32 per dozen, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.
Despite the announcement that production at the Florida facility would be temporarily halted, Cal-Maine’s stock rose 5.3 percent in early trading.

70,2026-11-18,"Trump’s move to exclude South Africa and Rwanda from AGOA shows why Africa must accelerate its own economic integration.
CEO of Alafarika for Studies and Consultancy, Nigeria.
Save
Share
On September 2 , United States President Donald Trump signed legislation extending the African Growth and Opportunity Act (AGOA) through the end of 2028, following overwhelming support in Congress. Yet just two months later, on November 2 , his administration announced plans to terminate the eligibility of South Africa and Rwanda for the programme from January 1, 2027.
The decision lays bare the fundamental weakness of AGOA: African economies depend on trade privileges that Washington can withdraw whenever its political priorities change.




AGOA is a US trade law that currently covers 33 eligible sub-Saharan African countries. It was enacted on May 18, 2000, to allow these countries to export thousands of products to the US market duty-free, and was promoted as a strategic mechanism to foster economic growth, expand trade between the US and the continent and encourage economic and political reforms.
AGOA initially delivered promising results. It is estimated to have supported more than 300,000 direct jobs across Africa and 1.3 million indirect jobs. According to a 2024 US Trade Representative report, two-way goods trade between the US and sub-Saharan Africa reached $47.5bn in 2023, including $9.7bn in imports under AGOA and the Generalized System of Preferences (GSP), of which $5.5bn consisted of non-oil products such as apparel and agricultural goods.
However, recent figures point to growing challenges, with some even suggesting a decline. A Congressional Research Service report shows that US imports receiving AGOA duty-free treatment dropped sharply from $7.9bn in 2024 to $5bn in 2025. Another analysis by the German Institute of Development and Sustainability (IDOS) noted that the share of US imports from sub-Saharan Africa benefiting from AGOA and GSP preferences plummeted from more than 80 percent in 2008 to roughly 24.7 percent in 2021 amid eligibility uncertainty and short renewal windows.
Only a few eligible sub-Saharan African countries capture most of the programme’s benefits. South Africa is its largest beneficiary, with exports to the US worth billions of dollars annually, while most other eligible countries struggle to make full use of its preferences.
Furthermore, because the US administers the programme’s preferences unilaterally, rather than through a bilateral treaty, Washington can exclude or reinstate any eligible country based on its own assessments and criteria, including market reforms, human rights and labour standards.
Since the programme’s inception, some countries suspended from AGOA have criticised the impact of their removal. Ethiopia’s suspension in 2022, for instance, led to the loss of thousands of garment sector jobs.
In practice, Washington’s geopolitical interests often carry more weight in eligibility decisions than AGOA’s stated criteria.
The Trump administration’s decision to terminate South Africa’s and Rwanda’s AGOA eligibility is a clear case in point. Both countries have pursued policies that conflict with Washington’s interests and that the administration regards as a threat. South Africa has strengthened its economic and political ties with US rivals such as China and Russia, while facing accusations from Washington of obstructing American trade and investment, alongside Trump’s allegations of a “white genocide”.
Rwanda’s alleged support for the M23 rebel group in the eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo has also brought it into conflict with US interests in the region’s critical minerals.
Sharp declines in AGOA import values, unstable US trade policies, and short-term extensions leave African exporters highly vulnerable. Losing AGOA access triggers immediate customs setbacks, including tariff increases of 15 to 20 percent on some products. Ultimately, these unpredictable extensions prevent AGOA from building a self-sustaining African industrial base, making accelerated implementation of the African Continental Free Trade Area (AfCFTA) an urgent priority for the continent.
Africa’s economic vision cannot remain hostage to changing administrations’ policies or unilateral decisions made abroad, as the continent needs to build strategic resilience and economic independence. The AfCFTA is a historic trade agreement signed under the auspices of the African Union (AU) on March 21, 2018, in Kigali, Rwanda, which officially entered into force on May 30, 2019, with trading beginning on January 1, 2021.
The agreement aims to bring African Union member states into a unified market of over 1.3 billion people, with a combined gross domestic product (GDP) exceeding $3.4 trillion.
Relying on a vast regional market would mitigate the impact of external shocks, such as the revocation of preferential trade agreements like AGOA or the sudden imposition of steep tariffs. Given that trade between African nations currently accounts for only about 15–18 percent of the continent’s total trade, the AfCFTA aims to progressively eliminate tariffs on 90 percent of tariff lines, thereby stimulating internal commerce and providing a buffer against declines in Western markets caused by tariff fluctuations.
AGOA and many other external agreements have largely kept Africa in the role of a raw material supplier. In contrast, the AfCFTA encourages the development of regional value chains, where one African nation might produce raw materials, another processes them into finished goods, and the final product is sold to consumers within the continent, with fewer tariff barriers.
AfCFTA has made notable progress in developing the instruments needed for continental trade. The number of countries participating in trade initiatives under the agreement has grown to include South Africa, Egypt, Nigeria, Ghana, Kenya, Rwanda and Tunisia, making use of its preferential tariff arrangements.
The Pan-African Payment and Settlement System (PAPSS), launched on January 13, 2022, enables banks and financial institutions to conduct cross-border transactions in African currencies, reducing reliance on foreign currencies such as the US dollar.
The African Union also adopted protocols on competition policy in February 2023 and digital trade in February 2024, aimed at promoting fair competition and facilitating e-commerce. In August 2026, the AfCFTA Secretariat signed an agreement to modernise and digitise customs systems. The AfCFTA Adjustment Fund has also been activated, with an estimated financing requirement of $8bn–$10bn, to support developing countries and small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) as they adapt to the requirements of the single market.
For AfCFTA to serve its purpose and reduce dependence on volatile global trade agreements that prioritise Western interests over those of Africa, the continent still needs to accelerate economic integration by removing tariff and non-tariff barriers and harmonising trade and regulatory policies to facilitate the smooth movement of goods and services.
To improve local markets, African countries must undertake the costly and risky investments involved in building cross-border infrastructure to reduce the cost of moving goods and services between African countries. Digital energy grids will be essential to this infrastructure, alongside roads and railways.
Africa must shift from simply exporting raw materials to building strong regional value chains focused on local manufacturing and innovation. Meeting the needs of the continent’s vast market would strengthen its economic self-sufficiency and reduce its vulnerability to external political decisions and the expiration of unilateral trade agreements.
The views expressed in this article are the author’s own and do not necessarily reflect Al Jazeera’s editorial stance.

					By Jill Goldsmith 
Co-Business Editor

	“If you had told me twenty years ago that someone was going to make a movie about me, that would have struck me as a really weird thing. Now it doesn’t feel like it’s in the top ten weirdest things in my life,” said Sam Altman, asked about the upcoming film Artificial.

	“What is?” asked Andrew Ross Sorking during a Q&A with the OpenAI co-founder and CEO at the New York Times DealBook Summit Wednesday

	“The fact that we are ushering in this technology that we have all of these very real concerns about, and that we find ourselves in a role of such responsibility. That is a crazy situation to find yourself in,” said Altman.

	Neon is releasing the Luca Guadagnino film about OpenAI and Altman, who is played by Andrew Garfield, in December. It’s not complimentary to the executive. Garfield, speaking at Artificial’s world premiere at the New York Film Festival earlier this fall, said the project was meant to question “who we are giving all of our autonomy, power, and data to.”




			Watch on Deadline		






	Altman appeared with the company’s president and co-founder Greg Brockman. With public fear of AI mounting as new models occasionally go rogue and distaste with data centers a flashpoint of the recent midterm elections, they both agreed that the past year has been a key transitional phase in the industry, and that the last few months have been especially difficult.

	“We are very clearly in the new phase, and I think we will have to, as we’ve had to in other new phases, operate differently for this new phase. We’ve been talking a lot about what’s going to happen or what needs to happen,” Altman said.

	“I think if the world and the companies developing AI treated the next four years like they’ve treated the last four years, that would be very bad. We have not been doing that. We have been some of the loudest voices” about the increasingly high stakes of artificial intelligence.

	He believes “the world needs a clear plan from the companies” in the space.

	










	The sector was stressed most recently by the ouster of  its rock star, Nvidia founder and CEO Jensen Huang, who had a relationship with a tech executive alleged to be a Chinese spy. A bizarre series of back-to-back outages across sectors from Microsoft email to aviation to banking over the past week, although not necessarily AI related, has exacerbated fears of global dependence on a handful of big tech players. Meanwhile, investors have become jittery over whether massive capital spending by so-called AI hyper-scalers will result in profits, or a potentially devastating stock market bubble.

OpenAI rival Anthropic is set to go public this week, a major test of investor sentiment on the business. OpenAI itself had plans for an IPO. They have been delayed, although Altman said today he believes it will be ultimately be a publicly traded company.

72,2026-11-18,"Since it first hit the hardwood in 1997, the Nike Air Bakin was a fiery silhouette in every sense of the word. From its name to the controversy surrounding it, it’s a conversation starter. We’re happy to see it back, looking more pared-back than we’re used to, as the Nike Air Bakin Black/White.
Rising to fame on the feet of Miami Heat legend Tim Hardaway and infamous for its turbulent history regarding its original heel branding, the Air Bakin represents late-90s Nike Basketball at its most unapologetic. 
While its first colorways leaned into vibrant primary hues, echoed in its return a couple of months ago in a bold red palette, this latest iteration focuses on texture with an understated Black/White makeup to allowing the unmistakable overlays and climbing-inspired lacing system to shine.
There’s always an appetite for time-tested basketball shoes. This much has been proven already. But something feels particularly timely about the reintroduction of the Nike Air Bakin. The silhouette touches on a number of trends at the same time.
Not least, the booming market of climbing shoes. One of the most defining qualities of this silhouette is its Alpine lacing system that stretches all the way to the toe. This subtle twist makes it a standout in a market of retro ball shoes. 
But while many might have been interested in the silhouette but turned off by its big, bold red colorway, this iteration goes back to basics. Monochrome never loses. Somehow, in black and white, even the Nike Air Bakin, arguably one of the most controversial silhouettes in Swoosh history, feels like a safe bet.

73,2026-11-18,"Today I joined UBC Okanagan's Co-op Sprint Series for a 30-minute session called Unlocking the Hidden Job Market. Morgan Marshall from the co-op office hosted.
One of the themes I talked about is that every major career change I had, there was a personal connection that enabled me to go to the next level.
Here's the conversation, lightly edited.
I graduated high school in '98, went to college for two years from '98 to 2000, and started working for a nonprofit that I'd been volunteering with. I worked with them doing youth work from 2001 to 2008. During that time, I also got my degree in business management.
Every major career change I had, there was a personal connection that enabled me to go to the next level.
While I was working for this nonprofit, there was this guy, Gregg Oldring, who took notice of me. He ran a software company called Mailout, located in downtown Edmonton. He had told my boss, ""If Justin ever wants to leave, let me know.""
When I decided I wanted to leave the nonprofit, I can remember for about a week I was handing out resumes and trying to apply for places. Then Gregg heard that I was looking for work, called me, and said, ""Stop applying. I want to hire you.""
That was good news for me, because I was applying for jobs I was not very excited about. If you've ever applied for jobs, you know how disheartening it is to take your resume around and have nobody really care about you.
I was 28 years old. I applied for the Apple Store. I applied to be a salesperson selling doors and windows, door to door. I would not have liked either of those, but I had bills to pay.
So this offer was just so great. And Gregg took me under his wing. I started in customer support, kind of at the bottom, and just worked my way up. By the time I left in 2014, I was product manager at Mailout Interactive.
The next job I got was with a startup in Portland. The founder there was Joe Stump. Joe was a guy I originally met on Twitter. Then he started listening to this podcast I was doing called Product People, where I was interviewing startup founders and people building software products. Eventually, he said, ""I'd like to hire you."" So that became my next career jump.
I worked with him for a year and a half until they got acquired. By this point, I'd been doing some other things that had built a little bit of an audience: podcasting, blogging, and writing a newsletter. I wrote a book called Marketing for Developers that did well enough that I didn't need a job for the next couple of years.
During that time, one of my podcast guests, Chase Reeves, invited me to a conference in Portland called XOXO. While I was there, I met this guy named Jon Buda. Jon and I stayed in touch. Every year, we would get together. We started building projects together.
At the end of 2017, he was working for Cards Against Humanity, and they were about to launch a podcast. He said, ""I'm thinking about building some software to help them publish the show and distribute it to Apple Podcasts and Spotify."" And I said, ""Jon, we've got to do this together as a company.""
In January 2018, we signed our partnership agreements and started working on Transistor together. This business has just been more than I could have ever dreamed of.
So that's my career so far, and a little insight into how each time there was a person connected to that jump.
I think this is so key. I've been telling this story a lot.
Just as background, Transistor is a small company. We don't hire very often. Every time the idea of doing a co-op came up, it just seemed like too much administrative work for me. It wasn't even on my radar.
But I've been running this meetup in Vernon called Geek Beers. It happens the last Thursday of every month, and I've been running it since 2012 or something like that. We often have students come around, especially lately as comp sci and tech hiring has gotten more challenging for juniors.
What impressed me about Ian, the co-op student we ended up hiring, was that he showed up consistently. He was always very clear about what he was looking for, and clear in a way that was also vulnerable: ""Hey everybody, I'm here because I'm looking for a co-op for this upcoming term. Here's the things I've been working on."" He was clearly engaged, asked really good questions in conversation, and was good at answering questions as well.
But I'd say the important piece is that he kept showing up. Eventually I started to build a rapport with him. I knew who he was. I looked forward to seeing him. We had topics we talked about last time that we could talk about this time.
As he kept sharing his life, I got more invested in who he was as a person, but also just more impressed. Through him coming to this meetup multiple times, I was able to see: ""Oh wow, this guy's a good communicator. Wow, this guy seems to be a really hard worker."" I invited him to our meetup Slack group, and he started participating in discussions there.
All of this built up. And I think that's the important piece: this isn't something you just do once. It's a repeated thing. You just keep showing up, keep showing up, keep showing up, keep building a relationship, which is really what most of life is.
Eventually, Ian posted a YouTube video in our Slack group: ""Hey everybody, just wanted to share. This is a video I made for my last co-op on everything I accomplished, the things I learned."" I watched it. It was the culmination of everything I'd seen about Ian before, and I was like, ""Man, he's just showing so much initiative."" The fact that he was willing to do this and post a video about it, and that he did such a good job of clearly communicating what he had accomplished. I was like, ""I think we should try to hire this guy.""
That's what pushed me to reach out to the UBCO co-op program and say, ""We want to get involved. We want to try to hire Ian for the summer.""
The nice thing about hiring somebody you've known for a couple of years is that it takes so much of the risk out of it. A lot of people look at this from the applicant side. On the employer side, I can tell you, it is so risky to hire anybody new. It's scary, because you're investing a lot. You don't want to have to fire people.
Being able to see how Ian shows up, for him to share what he was working on, his projects, some of his struggles, I just felt like I had a better view of him as a person. It made that hiring decision that much easier.
Then he showed up for the co-op during the summer, and he just killed it. He understood intuitively that he wanted to, A, solve the boss's problem: solve my problem. He was always reaching out, asking how he could do that. B, he was very outcome-driven. And C, he was willing to start in customer support.
He's a comp sci student, but we had him doing customer support. At our company, I'll probably never hire someone again who doesn't start in customer service. It helps you learn the company so quickly. Instantly, as a boss, I can see how you interact with customers, how you take care of them, if you're willing to go the extra mile, if you're a good communicator. It's just a perfect proving ground for new hires.
He demonstrated just how capable he was, and how much he cared. We told him that when he finishes his degree, he should definitely apply for a full-time position.
This has even gotten worse now with AI. On the applicant side, you've got applicants firing out thousands of applications a day. On the employer side, they're using AI to automatically deal with this deluge of applications, and they're ranking them in all sorts of ways.
A big company is going to play that game. If you're applying at Microsoft, that's part of the game. But even at Microsoft, having a warm introduction, a warm connection, helps you jump to the top of the list.
Actually, even in this coworking office, I was just talking to another guy here. There's a company called Kit.com. They do email newsletters. I know the founder. I noticed they have six positions open. I told this fellow he should apply, and I said, ""When you apply, let me know, and I will let the people there know that I can vouch for you.""
It's such a risk to hire a person. If you get stuck with a bad employee, it drags the whole team down. It becomes a headache for you as a boss and for the team. The goal when you hire somebody is that you want the employee to solve your problems. You don't want them to give you more problems.
So warm introductions and existing relationships are going to beat a cold application every time. In fact, if I was applying for roles, I would put 80% of my energy into getting the warm introduction, and only 20% of my energy into just applying cold.
It's funny. People think that 80% of actually developing a relationship seems so unclear and difficult. But the truth is, you'll probably get to your outcome faster, which is getting hired.
I know people who've been blindly sending out applications for years, every single day, and it's debilitating for them. So which is harder? Spending years sending out applications, hoping you're going to win the application lottery? Or doing the harder work up front: going out, building some relationships, seeking out some people, trying to get some coffee meetings?
Maybe you get turned down five times out of ten. Those are still great odds. And if you do get a coffee meeting with somebody, you can say, ""Hey, just to let you know, I'm graduating at this time. I'm trying to figure out how I should be applying. Do you have any advice for me?"" These things all stack on top of each other.
There's an old saying: hard conversations now, easy life. Easy conversations now, hard life in the long term. I would do the hard things now so that you have a better life in the future, as opposed to constantly doing the easy thing. Everybody's doing the easy thing. The way to stand out is to do the hard thing.
I realize this is a struggle, as I've talked to UBC students. The course load can be quite a lot, so sometimes it's hard to think about doing anything extra. But if you do have time, or after you've graduated, or if you have a moment over the holidays, here's one way to stand out.
If I was a student in comp sci or engineering right now, I would be really focusing on security. With AI, there's been an influx of vulnerabilities. If you have some natural interest in that, just geek out about it, and then tell people about it.
It's not enough for you to just geek out about it in your own time. You have to tell people about it. That could be:
Those things can all be very, very helpful. I realize there's a spectrum of people who like to work in public and not work in public. But having some of it is helpful, because it's evidence that you are interested, passionate, and thinking about a topic. The more evidence you can have out in public, the better for you.
In general, I look at people's portfolios and sites, and I'm not seeing enough. I'm seeing too much generic stuff. In tech, it's always, ""Here's some open source projects I worked on at school,"" or ""Here's a website."" But I want to know what you really geek out about. I want to know what you get really excited about. I want to hear that you've done some thinking. I want to see the projects you're building. I want to see the community work you're doing.
For example, one thing I don't think Ian had on his resume, but I would have loved to have seen: he worked at a summer camp for a bunch of summers. There was so much in that position, him lifeguarding at a summer camp: here's what I learned about leadership, here's what I learned about running a team. There were all these lessons he could have talked about.
Depending on who you're applying to, especially if it's a smaller company, I'd be okay with breaking out of the confines of the generic resume. Really show me what you're excited about. Really show me what you genuinely learned. Really show me your experience, not just at school and at work, but other volunteering you've done, other events you've gone to. I want to see all of that. The more evidence there is, the better.
And if you wrote a good paper... everybody has that one professor or class they really like, and you work really hard on those papers. I would be publishing those publicly. Make them a blog post. My favourite class in university ended up being business ethics, and a lot of those papers ended up becoming blog posts that I shared publicly. It just shows that you're fired up about something. That's what you want to see.
Another good example is Kyla Scanlon. She really started getting excited about economics, started posting about economics, and has built a whole career around that. Employers love to see that stuff.
Some basics in a coffee meeting: you eventually need to build the skills of being social. There are some bare minimum things, like your appearance. Be well-groomed, be clean, wear reasonable clothes. Then you have to develop some habits around social interaction: learning to look at somebody when you're speaking to them, learning how to acknowledge them, learning how to get over your nerves, which we all have to do.
That can take time. While you're building those skills, I think it's okay to be honest. If you show up at a coffee meeting and you're nervous, it's okay to say, ""I just want to let you know I've been really nervous about this all day."" That can be disarming in and of itself, because you're speaking it out loud. And the other person can say, ""Oh, don't worry about it. I'm not going to bite. We're just hanging out.""
Sometimes you do need to practice beforehand, until it becomes natural. You might want to practice in a mirror: ""What kind of things might this person ask?"" They're probably going to ask what I'm doing in school. They're probably going to ask what I want to do after. They're probably going to ask what my dream job would be.
If I ask you those questions and you don't have an answer, it can come off as, ""Oh, this person's not really a go-getter, or ambitious, or that serious."" But if you get really excited about something, and I can see the passion in your voice, and I can see you're really interested in it, that is really helpful.
Every community and every industry category has what are called super connectors. They're people who know a lot of people. You probably want to connect with super connectors more than people who aren't. For example, if you came to me and said, ""Man, I'm really into manufacturing or physical product design,"" I could recommend you to a startup in Vernon that makes VO2 masks for athletes and does all their 3D printing here. So connect with super connectors. Learn to identify those people.
And be okay to ask. At the end, just say, ""Hey, is there anyone you can think of that I should connect with, or that you can refer me to?"" It's okay to ask. And it's okay for them to say no.
For practicing this, one thing I've recommended to my own kids is that you can practice with strangers. Bringing up a conversation in a coffee shop with a stranger is one way to practice: ""What are you reading?"" or ""What are you working on?"" or ""Hey, I see you around here a lot. My name's Justin."" If you're sitting at the bar in a pub, it's a universal invitation to speak to the person next to you: ""Hey, you watching the game? What's your favourite team?""
The thing I've said to my kids is that the truth is, most people want connection. Even if the stats are that you'll only get 8 out of 10, those are incredible stats for building up a muscle of learning how to interact with people socially.
So I would be practicing these things all the time: when you're at a coffee shop, when you're out in public. As you practice, you'll become way more confident when you're in person with somebody. You'll be able to tell yourself, ""Oh, I've got evidence that I can do this. I spoke to that person at the coffee shop."" Put it into practice right now, in your daily life.
Social skills, by the way, are something everybody can learn. Going to meetups is another low-stakes way to get into it. If you're looking for a low-stakes meetup, feel free to come out to Geek Beers in Vernon. It's just in a pub, and we try to be as welcoming as we can, especially to younger people.
You can do them, but there needs to be a warm background.
Here's a quick example. I just had someone from Vancouver reply to my email newsletter. He said, ""Hey, I've been reading your blog and your newsletter for a while, listening to your podcast. I'm driving up from Vancouver. Will you have coffee with me?""
Instant yes. Because it's not somebody fake.
I get so many cold emails now, especially using AI, that pull out fake stuff like, ""Oh, I was listening to your podcast,"" or ""I was reading your blog."" You can tell they're fake. But this was a real person who had been reading the newsletter. It was just an easy yes for me.
An example of a LinkedIn cold DM is another person I hired here, Michael Green. He had gone to another one of our local tech events, and I'd seen him a few times. He reached out on LinkedIn, and that got him a meeting. Eventually, that relationship ended up in a hire.
If it's clear they've been following the newsletter or the blog or the podcast. Or if they have a real question, one I can tell they're not just spraying out to everybody. If it's a real, genuine question about something I've written or something they heard me talk about on a podcast, that's a guaranteed reply.
I think it's even better showing up at local events: ""Hey, I met you briefly at this event. I was the student that was struggling with this. Wondering if you have time for coffee?"" Those are just such easy yeses to give.
Students have this free golden ticket, which is: if you are genuine and you approach a business leader... almost everybody I know who's a CEO or a founder loves to have questions from students, or students that want to go for coffee. As long as it's clear that you're genuine and you have a specific question you want to ask, I think you can DM any CEO or founder.
These days, the problem is that so much of it is being automated. If your first email sounds spammy or AI-generated or insincere or just kind of banal, then it doesn't matter how many times you follow up.
It's not that hard to write a genuine email that shows you actually know something about me or my company, or shows genuine interest in what we're doing. What would be an example?
""Hey Justin, I'm a UBC student here in Kelowna, and I've started this podcast about what to do when you're overloaded with stress during exam season. Would love to connect sometime.""
That alone is like, ""Oh wow, okay. They're local. They're in podcasting. I can go listen to it."" Those kinds of things help.
You just can't be any sort of low-hanging fruit, like, ""Oh, I'll just blurt this out. It'll be easy."" No. Put some effort into it. Be strategic with how you're reaching out, and think about it like a relationship.
That's the biggest thing: bosses and people hiring, we're human too. We actually want to connect with other human beings. Just like anyone, I want to connect with interesting, ambitious people.
If you're a student, just own the fact that you're a student. I don't think anybody would expect a student to be overly confident or overly put together. It's okay to be a little nervous.
I would try to get a sense of what people are wearing, so be reasonably presentable. And I would have some answers. People are going to ask:
Having answers to some of those questions makes you more relatable and more interesting. Don't be afraid to show some ambition: ""I would really like to work for this company in this position someday."" People like to see that too.
And get good at asking questions. This is another thing Ian is quite good at. You get kind of a golden ticket. There are no dumb questions. You can even ask questions that an older adult might not be able to ask: ""What's it like being the CEO? How do you think about hiring? What are some things you look for in employees?"" In an open forum, especially a meetup, you can ask all sorts of questions you might not normally be able to ask on LinkedIn or email.
I would put LinkedIn and email down the list, at four and five. There's other things you can do before that. Almost all in-person events would be a better use of your time.
The hidden job market is most of the job market. Almost all of the good jobs, almost all of the good opportunities, are in the hidden job market.
My encouragement to you is that even though it's going to be harder in the short term, it's going to be easier in the long term to just invest in this hidden job market. Really, it's about building relationships, networking, showing up at events, meeting people, building up your social skills, and getting interested in what other people are doing. Subscribe to some email newsletters, follow some interesting people on LinkedIn, follow some interesting podcasts.
One of the easiest warm intros you can do is just: ""Hey, I've been reading your blog,"" or ""I've been reading your posts on LinkedIn, and I've been really resonating with a lot of the things you're saying."" That's an easy in.
I'm convinced that if you work hard enough in this hidden job market sense, you'll be able to find a job even in a hard job market.
The other hidden job market is that there are lots of employers that would hire, but don't have a job posting. A boss always has a list of things in the background: ""Oh man, it'd be great if I had someone to do this."" But we don't get around to doing a job posting. What ends up filling those roles is when we meet somebody who would fit in that slot, and we go, ""Oh my gosh, you'd be perfect.""
So just because there's not a job posting doesn't mean a company isn't hiring. It's worth it to go and speak with those folks.
Another easy one that I did when I was in college: I just reached out to a bunch of companies I wanted to work for and said:
""Listen, I've been following your company. I think you guys are amazing. I would love to work for a company like yours one day. I'm wondering if I can come for a tour, or come and meet you. I would just like to hear more about how you guys hire and what kinds of things I should be doing.""
Anybody can do that. And now all of a sudden, they've got this ambitious self-starter at their doorstep, and they're touring you around. There are lots of creative opportunities like that.
And it's fun. It would be fun to go for a tour at your dream company, right? 80% of those companies didn't hire me or offer me a job, but it was still a great experience to go and see what they were doing and how they hire.
Cheers,
Justin Jackson

					By Ted Johnson 
Political Editor

Newsmax has filed an antitrust lawsuit challenging Fox Corp.‘s $22 billion acquisition of Roku, a transaction that was closed earlier this month.

	In the lawsuit, filed in federal court in San Jose, claimed that the Justice Department “allowed this merger to an effective monopoly to close unchallenged,” while Newsmax’s legal team dismissed a set of commitments that Fox made as “ill-defined” and with “no evident enforcement mechanism.”

	Newsmax contended that Roku’s Roku Channel with Fox’s Tubi have a combined 70% share in the market for free ad supported television streaming channels.

	











	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	News
	



	
		
					David Ellison Asserts Potential Federal Tax Credit Not For Tom Cruise But To Save Below-The-Line Jobs		
			















	News
	



	
		
					David Ellison Says There Might Be ""Some News Sharing In The Future"" Between CBS And CNN		
			







	“DOJ virtually never clears a transaction combining the two leading competitors in a concentrated market; it typically demands divestiture of one or the other to protect competition,” Newsmax’s legal team wrote in their lawsuit.




			Watch on Deadline		






	A Fox Corp. spokesperson said in a statement, “Newsmax’s complaint, which comes after a rigorous review process by regulatory agencies, is wholly without merit. Upon closing, we commenced actively integrating this approved transaction for the benefit of consumers, partners and other stakeholders.”

	Last year, Newsmax filed an antitrust lawsuit against Fox News claiming that it was suppressing competition in the market for right-leaning video content. A federal judge in Florida dismissed the complaint as a “shotgun pleading.” When the case was then filed in Wisconsin, a federal judge there found that it was a form of “forum shopping” and ordered it transferred to Florida. The case is still pending there, with discovery stayed as a judge considers Fox’s motion to dismiss.

	Fox and Roku announced the closing of their transaction on the morning of Nov. 6, less than five months after the deal was proposed last June – well ahead of the initially projected window of the first half of 2027. Later that day, the Justice Department announced in a press release that it had cleared the transaction, outlining the set of commitments that Fox made as it sought the regulatory greenlight. Among them was one promising that Roku would “remain an open and neutral content partner post-merger,” and that Roku would continue to carry non-Fox content on for a period of at least three years.

	










	In their Roku lawsuit, Newsmax’s legal team argued that “when a transaction is likely to harm competition absent conduct remedies of the sort to which Fox supposedly ‘committed,’ DOJ’s practice is to negotiate a consent decree that sets out those commitments.” The consent decree is released by the public and reviewed by the court under the Tunney Act, Newsmax’s lawyers noted.

	“That procedure ensures that the merging parties’ obligations are clear and enforceable – something four bullet points in a press release fail to achieve,” Newsmax’s legal team wrote.

	Newsmax claimed that after the announcement of the merger close, it contacted Fox to ask for a binding contract to make its commitments enforceable. Newsmax sent a term sheet, per the lawsuit, but Fox rejected it and declined to make a counter proposal

	Newsmax’s legal team wrote, “As the press release effectively acknowledges, Fox’s ownership of Roku creates substantial vertical foreclosure concerns: a leading content provider will control its competitors’ access to a critical distribution channel. At a minimum, those concerns must be addressed through enforceable behavioral conditions that prevent Fox from disadvantaging rival content providers.”

	Newsmax claimed that Fox has refused to carry its news channel on Tubi, and that with the acquisition of The Roku Channel “the risk that Fox will further degrade distribution of Newsmax’s content is clear.”

	The lawsuit called for injunctive relief, including the unwinding of the merger unless Fox divests Tubi or The Roku Channel.

76,2026-11-18,"
Russian President Vladimir Putin has named India among the key partners with which Moscow is currently negotiating a Free Trade Agreement (FTA). Russia hopes to expand these trade deals sevenfold to reach roughly 4 billion people, he said in his address at the APEC Summit in Shenzhen, China.
Underscoring the vast potential of preferential trade deals, the Russian leader said that over the past three years the Eurasian Economic Union (EEU), which also includes Armenia, Belarus, Kazakhstan and Kyrgyzstan, was able to expand its market access threefold, from 185 million people to 570 million people.
“Last year such an agreement came into force with Iran, this year with the United Arab Emirates and Mongolia, and early next year an agreement will take effect with one of the largest and most dynamic economies in Asia, and indeed the world: Indonesia. We are conducting similar negotiations with India,” Putin said.
He added that similar talks had been initiated with Tunisia, and prospects for FTAs are being explored with Pakistan, Jordan, Egypt, South Africa and Zimbabwe. Putin noted that Moscow is paying “special attention” to building “comprehensive, multifaceted trade and economic ties” between the EEU and China


🇮🇳🤝🇷🇺 Putin Eyes India FTA As Russia Targets A 4-Billion-Person Free Trade Market

Speaking at the APEC Summit in Shenzhen, he singled out 🇮🇳 as one of the countries Moscow was eager to enter an FTA with.

He also indicated about the impending deal with the ""largest & most dynamic"" Indonesia early in 2027.

He said the combined market covered by Russia's existing trade deals has already tripled to 570mn people in three years.
Last December, Putin and Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi set a bilateral trade target of $100 billion by 2030. Trade turnover between Russia and India exceeded $65 billion last year, and in the first eight months of this year it totaled $55 billion, Russian Trade Commissioner in India Andrey Sobolev said last week.
The two countries have highlighted the need to diversify bilateral trade flows, which are currently dominated by India’s imports of energy, fertilizers, military equipment and related services from Russia.
Russian First Deputy Prime Minister Denis Manturov said earlier this year that the talks over an FTA with India were in their “active stage.” The agreement, once concluded, could be “a new contributing factor to sustainable trade growth,” he noted.
The two countries are also accelerating negotiations on a new Bilateral Investment Treaty (BIT) aimed at giving investors stronger legal certainty and encouraging greater two-way capital flows. Talks in New Delhi in September 2026, involving Indian Commerce and Industry Minister Piyush Goyal and Industry and Trade Minister Anton Alikhanov, placed the treaty high on the agenda alongside broader economic cooperation.

77,2026-11-18,"In stark contrast to the dire straits that the movie theater industry was in just a few years ago, 2026 has been especially encouraging to those of us who adore going to the movies. A big part of why that's changed lies in the industry's embrace of unique Premium Large Formats, with numerous releases this year having been shown in formats like IMAX, 4DX, Screen X, Dolby Cinema, and so on. Along the way, screening on celluloid (70mm in particular) have become a draw unto themselves, and some cities are home to even more specialized formats, among them Las Vegas' immersive Sphere and the Cosm experience. And while the biggest draw to movie theaters will always be good films, it's clear that audiences also enjoy an experience they can't get elsewhere.
As usual, however, the tech industry isn't far behind to try and stoke the competition between theaters and home viewing. While most consumers have been content with things like big screen 4K TV's or even their handheld devices for watching at home or on the go, the latest attempt to leap forward from these is just around the corner, in the form of Meta's VR Glasses. It's important to note that these are not the latest version of the smart glasses that have existed on the market since 2021, as they're explicitly not designed to be used while engaged in other activity. Instead, they're a cutting edge virtual/augmented reality system intended to bring together multiple work and entertainment options while idle. 
I recently had the opportunity to try these glasses for myself, and their potential, especially for home viewing, is amazing. That said, there are some restrictions to them which, unless changed, mean they won't be replacing our existing personal devices anytime soon.
Although virtual reality, or VR, has existed in some form for decades, it's mostly been utilized as a gimmick more than a viable first choice for media. Fortunately, the folks behind Meta VR Glasses seem to understand this. I got to see a clip from the recent concert doc ""Billie Eilish — Hit Me Hard and Soft: The Tour (Live in 3D)"" on the glasses, and the experience was like I was watching it on a movie theater screen. That is to say that the aspect ratio was exactly the same as its theatrical exhibition, and the 3D display was pitch perfect. Turning my head in any direction didn't move the ""screen,"" but kept it fixed in front of me, as if it was a physical object in the space, which is a very smart design.
While the James Cameron documentary acted as a demo of how watching most films and shows on the glasses would go, the Meta employees next showed me what they're calling ""Enhanced Cinema."" Using a clip from ""Avengers: Infinity War"" in which Thor (Chris Hemsworth) constructs Stormbreaker, the presentation was almost exactly akin to the Cosm experience. This meant that the film was playing on a similar fixed ""display"" in front of me, while all around me (literally 360 degrees — I checked!) was the environment of the forge on Nidavellir. It's an effect that's totally immersive but not distracting, nor does it betray the original intent of the film itself. After being told that Meta has an exclusive license with the IMAX corporation to present movies filmed for that format on the glasses, I have no doubt that the device could brilliantly replicate those screens for home viewing, and that's incredibly exciting.
While the Meta VR Glasses are not intended to replace a smartphone or similar device, they do seem to do a lot of what those devices do, only bigger and better. From working on VR video games and multiple computer monitors at once to allowing you to watch sports games as they broadcast while seeing the view from courtside, the glasses seem almost too good to be true as a personal media device. 
This is where the catches come in. For one thing, watching something with other people at the same time can only occur depending on the content. For another, though they're engineered to be used while stationary, the temptation for people to wear them in public while walking (or, god forbid, driving) exists, and it's quite troubling. Then there's the fact that the glasses seem designed to only work with whatever services and content Meta allows. This means that us physical media fans are SOL, and as with any all-digital service, the content available on the glasses could be subject to change (or disappear) at any time. That's a lot to ask for a device priced at $1,299.99 USD, which is about the same as a new iPhone. 
Note: Following publication, a representative for Meta did inform us that the VR glasses will support playback devices that are compatible via a wired DisplayPort-over-USB-C connection, meaning that additional content (like physical media) that the consumer has control over may be viewable on the glasses.
Right now, Meta's killer app beyond the tech itself are its exclusive licenses, such as with IMAX and James Cameron's Lightstorm Entertainment. The only way it'll compete with devices already on the market is if it can consistently deliver the quality experiences I got to have with it while being as user friendly as possible. If the glasses prove they can be used at home and on the go as smoothly as an iPhone can now, then they could be a game changer. Until then, they're just a gimmick, albeit a really nice one."

In the waning hours before GTA VI becomes available on my living room console, I am (for now) posted up at my PC, blasting bright, neon pink directly into my retinas. Just like when I was a kid.
Iâve been playing Barbie Rewind in bits and odd pieces. Released last week, itâs another retro game collection from Digital Eclipse that thankfully makes 16 classics playable again for normal people who donât have giant retro console collections, decades after their original releases. One of those gamesâBarbie: Vacation Adventureânever even got a full release until now, and was previously only known via a handful of magazine previews. Now, you can play it.
I like the idea of Barbie Rewind, and Iâm glad these games are available again. But the presentation of this collection leaves a lot wanting. Itâs buggy, for oneâI had weird audio blips in both the Dreamhouse segments (more on those in a moment) and in several of the games, with sound and music cutting out randomly or going fuzzy like a radio. Barbie Super Sports was, I hate to use the phrase but, literally unplayableâonce I got to the skiing section, I couldnât move my character at all in any direction no matter how many times I tried or what buttons I pushed. At one point, I spent so long navigating back and forth in and out of menus trying to check the controls that my menu got stuck on the controls page, leaving me unable to exit the game without hard shutting it down. Unfortunately, doing so erased all of my progress in both Super Sports and two other games I had tried prior, which in turn set me back on my objectives in the Dreamhouse. I also just found most of the games difficult to parse visually in their original resolution on a modern 1080p monitor; surely something should have been done about that?
For that reason, I find the Dreamhouse bit of Barbie Rewind far more compelling than the games that are the actual purpose of the collection. As you play the retro games, you unlock new rooms in a Dreamhouse and new furniture to fill them with. The decor is all taken from Barbie playsets over the years: couches, tables, lamps, phones, beds, and so forth. While the decorating mechanics are pretty simple, the decorations themselves are bright and colorful neons and pastels in funky and often retro shapes and styles, daring you to try to make them fit an orderly theme. Just as I would want a pile of mixed-up playsets to be.
I imagine that those who played with such things growing up will recognize them, but itâs reminded me that despite being a Barbie fiend, I never had any Barbie playsets growing up. Just dolls and clothes. I would build houses for them out of a couple of empty KâNEX boxes, or use the couch and pillows to make small Barbie forts. Iâm a little jealous of whoever got to mess around with all this plastic, though Iâm sure that, had I had any of this, it would have gotten lost like all the little Barbie shoes that disappeared over the years. Itâs not the same, decorating a virtual Dreamhouse. But itâs a nice enough approximation, given that Iâm not about to go out and buy any of this in reality.
Rachel Simone Weil, a girls video game and toy historian, played a major role in getting the Dreamhouse aspects together. She served as a historical consultant on Barbie Rewind, and especially focused on making sure the Dreamhouse elements were an accurate and celebratory portrayal of Barbie history. Obviously, Barbie Rewind could not include every single playset ever made, so Weil had to be selective as she tapped into different aspects of Barbie history. What were the most important relics of Barbie history?
âObviously, the iconic playsets, the ones that fans remember, those really tactile memories of just the feeling of the plastic or the way the box smelled,â she said. âThere are certain playsets, I think, that are really emblematic of the brand. I think of the Beach Blast pool. That’s pretty iconic. Obviously, the original Barbie Dreamhouse. We also included some deep cuts. There’s Cafe Today, which is pretty rare and hard to come by, especially in good condition, but a beautiful, psychedelic-designed cafe.â
In addition to the mix of iconic sets and deep cuts, Weil also wanted to emphasize technology, given the video game-ness of Barbie Rewind. And Barbie has quite the technological history. Weil tells me about playsets from before I was born making a point to include computers, phones, record players, CD players, and so forth. Barbie was always hip, always on top of the latest trends both in fashion and in tech.
âThe Barbie Dreamhouse from 1979 has this nightstand with this little tiny computer in it,â Weil says. Iâve seen it, I have it in my Barbie Rewind Dreamhouse currently, and it looks wonderfully retro and ridiculous. âAnd in â79, personal computers were just starting to come out. So, you’re starting to see people imagining what computers might be, right? And then you see Day to Night Barbie in the â80s, you start to see the IBM PC style playset computers.
âOh, another one I really like is the travel agent playset from, I think, 1986. The travel industry was famously one of the first industries to adopt computer technology. And right as it’s happening, Barbie is there with that travel agent playset and she’s got a computer. And the home office playsets from the â80s and â90s. Barbie’s got a home computer. She goes to work and she does computers.â
Barbie was a massive, massive part of my childhood. For a solid eight, nine, ten years, Barbie was one of my predominant interests. Dressing up those dolls, taking them to friendsâ houses, and playing make-believe was formative for me, and two of the video games in this collection (Barbie Super Sports and Barbie Race and Ride) represent my earliest memories of playing video games at all. I was playing Barbie long before I had my hands on Mario or Zelda.
And yet, Barbie is rarely if ever considered to be on equal footing with those classics. In some ways, I get itâmainstream gaming is bent far more toward the kinds of games that Mario and Zelda evolved to become. And yet, Infinity Nikki is massive. So is The Sims. The Style Savvy series on Nintendo handhelds was super popular, as were animal-focused games like Nintendogs. Heck, we just heard everyone praising Dressmaker. These games have always been with us, and Barbie pioneered a lot of what they would become. Itâs unfortunately not too hard to work out why Barbie doesnât get the respect she deserves.
âBarbie Rewind is the first retro game compilation that centers predominantly girl-led experiences in console gaming, right, of the 20th century,â Weil says. âThese games were a big deal when they came out in terms of popularity, sales, and it’s not just about the market impact. These games had a big, like, left a big impression on the people that played them, right?
âGirls who played these games in the â80s, â90s, â00s are adults now, and they’re looking back on these games with fondness and nostalgia and stories about, you know, negotiating with their siblings about who got the controller and the hours poured into them. There’s really not beenâI’d say there’s been a lack of an outlet for that fondness or way to revisit these games or even an acknowledgement from the world that these Barbie games, they weren’t just a fever dream, not only were they real, but we can play them again on modern platforms. So, I think for Digital Eclipse and Atari to put out this collection in collaboration with Mattel, I think it’s really gratifying, a gratifying acknowledgement that women’s retro gaming experiences are valid, important, and they’re part of the broader retro gaming culture at large, and they deserve to be included.â
Itâs not just girls, either. David Lowey, director of marketing at Atari, is here to champion girl games for everyone. âIt’s amazing how many people I’ve spoken to, particularly guys I’ve spoken to, who have played a significant amount of these games. And the answer is always the same. It was like, âYeah, it was on the PC in the kitchen. It was my sister’s game, but I played the hell out of it.â I heard that so much.â
Recently, an interview with the developers of Dressmaker sparked some social media discussion around women as gamers, and whether they are being adequately served by games that reflect their interests. Itâs a tricky discussion. On one hand, itâs obviously narrow-minded to assume that women are only going to be interested in games about fashion and vacation and pet rescue and horses. But on the other, it is true that many women do find those things interesting, and that because those things are typically associated with women, games like the ones in Barbie Rewind are less common and often not as well-funded or supported as more âmasculineâ games.
Weil had not seen the Dressmaker discourse when I spoke to her and thus didnât want to comment on the interview specifically, but I asked her to tell me more broadly: what are we talking about when we talk about Girl Games? What does it mean for a game to be a Girl Game?
âFirst of all, it’s changed a lot in the last 15 years,â she says. âI think 15 years ago it was very difficult to have that conversation. I remember being in grad school and having professors say, âyou can’t study Barbie video games.â I remember having an exhibit vandalized. People were upset about the idea that we would talk about girlsâ video games, or that acknowledging that they existed and celebrating those memories was the same as saying, âgirls must do this, and girls must love pink and whatever.â And so it’s a difficult conversation.â
Weil goes on to say that she intentionally uses the phrase âgirl gameâ to provoke that difficult conversation: to get people talking about the dichotomy between what girls must be, what they can be, and what theyâre allowed to be.
âOne thing that I think about as a historian is that this scrutiny of girl games, [where people ask], âIs this set modeling the right behaviors for girls? Is this setting up the right expectations for what femininity means and all this stuff?â We’re not asking those same questions about boy games. We’re not asking, is Mega Man a good role model for boys? Is Mega Man going to teach boys to, you know, be this or that? Or, if we’re asking those questions, it’s not preventing Mega Man from being part of retro gaming canon, right?
âFor me as a historian, I’m not saying we shouldn’t ask those questions, or we shouldn’t talk about those social issues. But I think we have a tendency to let the pendulum swing too far and say, âthere are social issues around this. And so let’s put all the Barbie games in the trash.â We can’t do that. I think the history of girlsâ video games has been forgotten and rediscovered like three or four times because this behavior of saying, âThis is a delicate subject, this touches on social topics or discrimination or stereotypes or things like that. And I feel nervous about that. And so I’m just going to pretend that these games didn’t exist.â We can’t do that.â
