										The latest installment in the Grand Theft Auto series heads to the state of Leonida, home to the neon-soaked streets of Vice City and beyond.									
Jason and Lucia have always known the deck is stacked against them. But when an easy score goes wrong, they find themselves on the darkest side of the sunniest place in America, in the middle of a criminal conspiracy stretching across the state of Leonida — forced to rely on each other more than ever if they want to make it out alive.
Grand Theft Auto VI arrives on PlayStation 5 Thursday, November 19, available at PlayStation Store.

The game is set in the state of Leonida, home to the neon-soaked streets of Vice City and beyond in the biggest, most immersive evolution of the series yet.

Jason grew up around grifters and crooks. After a stint in the Army trying to shake off his troubled teens, he found himself in the Keys doing what he knows best, working for local drug runners. Meeting Lucia could be the best or worst thing to ever happen to him. Jason knows how he’d like it to turn out but right now, it’s hard to tell.
Lucia’s father taught her to fight as soon as she could walk, and life has been coming at her swinging ever since. Fighting for her family landed her in the Leonida Penitentiary. Sheer luck got her out but now more than anything, Lucia wants the good life her mom has dreamed of since their days in Liberty City. Instead of half-baked fantasies, Lucia is prepared to take matters into her own hands.
Leonida is made up of six regions: Vice City, the Leonida Keys, Grassrivers, Port Gellhorn, Ambrosia, and Mount Kalaga. Here’s a closer look at what makes each region unique.
The glamour, hustle, and greed of America captured in a single city. Each neighborhood has something to offer, from the pastel art deco hotels and bright white sands of Ocean Beach, to the bustling panaderías of Little Cuba and the bootleg brands of the Tisha-Wocka flea market, out to the VC Port, the cruise ship capital of the world. We’re a long way from the ’80s, but Vice City is still the sun and fun capital of America

Life in this tropical archipelago isn’t flashy but it’s easy. Get your buzz on and pull up a deck chair but look out — you are right on the doorstep of some of the most beautiful and dangerous waters in all of America.

You never know what lies beneath the surface of this primordial expanse. The gators may be the most famous attraction, but there are far deadlier predators and weirder discoveries among the mangroves.

Cheap motels, shut-down attractions, and empty strip malls won’t bring the tourists back, but there’s a new economy in this once-popular vacation spot. It’s fueled by malt liquor, painkillers, and truck stop energy drinks.

In the heart of Leonida, American industry and old school values still reign supreme — whatever the cost. The Allied Crystal sugar refinery provides the jobs, while the local biker gang provides almost everything else.

A national landmark up against the state’s northern border, Mount Kalaga offers prime hunting, fishing, and off-road trails. In the lush surrounding backwoods, hillbilly mystics and paranoid radicals live far from the prying eyes of the government.

Spanning stations including Classic Rock, Modern Hip-Hop, Pop, Metal, Dance, Latin, ‘80s, Electronic, Blues, Ballads, Talk Radio, and much more, Grand Theft Auto VI features the biggest and widest-ranging selection of in-game radio yet.
Plus, for the first time ever, Grand Theft Auto VI introduces a series of on-demand podcasts for Jason and Lucia to experience beyond Leonida’s terrestrial radio, with both radio and podcasts available to listen to in vehicles and on foot through newly introduced headphones and earbuds connected to their smartphones. 


Available November 19

81,2026-11-18,"""We're still in the early stages of what we want to achieve""
In a first substantial update since acquiring SteamDB from its founders back in September, the owners of Nexus Mods have outlined some new additions coming to , the two sides in the deal have announced some additions coming to the community-run backend database. Affiliate links aimed at providing options to help folks find the cheapest price for a game and a new tab dedicated to the mods Nexus host for a game are incoming, as work on behind-the-scenes improvements continues.
""We're now a couple of months in since [the takeover] announcement, so I thought it was about time we shared a quick update on what we've been working on behind the scenes, along with a few changes you'll start to see rolling out over the coming weeks,"" Victor Folmann, CEO of Nexus Mods owners Chosen wrote in a blog post on SteamDB.
There are two main new features he pointed to that you'll notice popping up on SteamDB soon. The first is one that anyone could have seen coming, a new tab on each moddable game's page that shows off some mods hosted for it on Nexus Mods and a host of modding-related data. ""The new tab will help you discover mods for games you're browsing on SteamDB, making it easier to see whether a game has an active modding community and explore what it has to offer,"" Fomann wrote. ""For now, we'll only be showing mods from Nexus Mods, as we already have access to that wealth of modding data, making it a straightforward starting point for this new feature.
""We appreciate that some of you might be wondering why we're not including Steam Workshop, too. It's certainly something we'd be open to exploring as we develop the feature further, along with other modding sources. With that in mind, if you run a modding website and would be interested in having your community's mods included, we'd love to hear from you.""
That seems perfectly fine, but the other incoming feature's a bit more controversial given SteamDB's historically not been all about the money. ""One thing we've seen people ask for is an easier way to find the cheapest place to buy a game, and we're now working on making that possible,"" Folmann wrote. ""We'll be introducing affiliate links to authorised game retailers, giving you more options when looking for the best available price on a game.
""We recognise that introducing affiliate links may raise some concerns, so we want to be clear about how they'll work. First and foremost, as I mentioned, we'll only feature authorised retailers; we will not be including grey-market resellers. We've also made a deliberate decision about how prices are displayed: retailers will be ordered by the cheapest available price in your currency, not by whichever retailer offers us the highest affiliate commission. If a retailer doesn't offer pricing in your currency, we'll still show deals in other currencies provided they are available in your region.""
Folmann was clear in the initial announcement of the takeover that Nexus Mods' bosses would be actively looking into ways SteamDB could make more money to sustain itself, so the addition of these links isn't out of the blue either. Whether SteamDB regulars agree that they're a ""lighter-touch"" way of earning the site some cash without detracting from the traditional experience of using it is another matter. I'll need to see how I feel once I've actually navigated a few pages with the links in as part of looking up stuff about games, but my first instinct is that as long as the pages don't end up revolving around the links and it's still easy to get to the info you want without interacting with them, I probably won't be too put out.
Beyond those tweaks, Folmann mentioned ""improvements to how updates are tested before they go live"" and how quickly the team can react to issues have been among the less obvious work going on behind the scenes. ""We're still in the early stages of what we want to achieve, and there's plenty more work we need to do and would like to work on,"" he wrote in closing.
Folmann and co only took over Nexus Mods in June last year, making them still relatively new custodians of both it and SteamDB.

82,2026-11-18,"Photonic chips use light instead of electrons to process data
""Photonic chips are on the verge of a breakthrough,"" says Ton van Mol of the Dutch research institute TNO.
If van Mol is right â and TNO, the European Union and the Dutch government are backing the idea with millions of euros - then the technology could be a gamechanger.
The chips which run today's society, from your phone and TV, to the vast data centres meeting our insatiable desire for AI, are mostly electrical.
They process data by shunting electrons around tiny circuits â a single microchip produced today can contain billions of transistors, and sizes are measured in nanometres (one nanometre is a billionth of a metre).
Because they use light instead of electricity, photonic chips are faster, use less energy and generate less heat than traditional computer chips.
Van Mol hopes for an acceleration in photonics similar to the one seen in electrical chips since the 1970s.
""With optical chips, you are now where electronic chips were maybe 40 [or] 50 years ago.""
American, Taiwanese and Chinese firms dominate the computer chip industry, but Europe sees the emergence of photonic chips as a fresh opportunity to lead, on what could be, a key future technology.
Which is why the European Union has committed â¬400m ($450m; Â£330m) to a project called PIX Europe, external, with the stated goal of making the continent ""a global leader in emerging technology for Photonic Integrated Chips"".
""Europe said 'we need to have our own manufacturing in-house',"" says van Mol, who is leading a key part of the programme.
""We cannot depend like in the past that everything is manufactured in the US or China - that's not wise, so the [European] Chips Act was called into life, and one of the parts of the act is to build pilot lines to really bring the technology, which is now in labs, to the next stage - manufacturing.""

Electronic and photonic chips are produced on wafers made up of hundreds of individual chips. Factories with machines that can handle bigger wafers can churn out chips more efficiently. 
""[Photonic chip] manufacturing worldwide is [currently] done on wafers which are four inches (10cm) in size, and the next step is to scale-up production to a six-inch wafer,"" says van Mol.
His team are already building a 10,000 sq m factory to produce six-inch indium phosphide wafers on the High Tech Campus in the Dutch city of Eindhoven.
It's due to fully open in 2030 and will be a major step forward, not just for photonics in Europe, but around the world, according to van Mol.
""That's the development TNO is going to do, in a pilot line which is really serious.
""In 2030, we hope that we have a stable, reproducible, high-yield process, and then the intention is that the pilot line [opens] to industrial parties who can make commercial production out of it.""
Netherland's based ASML is a crucial supplier of chip-making equipment
Europe does have one crucial player in the current computer chip industry - ASML.
The Netherlands-based tech giant is the only company in the world able to build the machines needed to make the most sophisticated semiconductors.
Which is why ASML products will sit at the heart of the pilot line in Eindhoven - it's no coincidence that van Mol's project is based around the corner from ASML's headquarters.
""The way you produce these [photonic] chips is very similar to the way you produce electrical or electronic chip,"" says van Mol.
""You use the same type of equipment, you also need a high-end clean room, you also use wafers, so the production method is very similar to the traditional semiconductor technology.""
ASML itself is keen to explore the opportunities that the emerging technology of photonics could offer.
""Europe has a strong position in photonics research. The next challenge is translating those innovations into industrial-scale manufacturing,"" says Stanislas Baron, who is the head of ASML's EU/US Customer Team.
Peter Wennik says TNO's pilot production line is a ""launchpad"" for European tech
ASML's unique success has helped build an influential and lucrative network of companies and institutions devoted to semiconductor technology in and around Eindhoven, and it's a model for what Europe is trying to achieve with photonic chips.
The Eindhoven project has the backing of former ASML chief executive Peter Wennink, author of an influential report for the Dutch government called The Roadmap to Future Prosperity, external, which noted that the market for photonics was expected to grow 10 to 20 times in the next 10 years.
In a news release backing the new pilot line, Wennink described it as ""a launchpad for Europe's future digital economy"".
""By investing in scalable production capacity, we are creating an ecosystem that can help shape the global technological direction,"" he said.
Of course, Europe is not alone in looking to the potential of photonics to earn a global economic advantage.
In June this year, US photonics company Coherent announced what it called ""the world's first and largest volume-production six-inch indium phosphide manufacturing platform"" at its plant in Sherman, in Texas.
The expansion is backed by chip manufacturing giant Nvidia, external, as well as $50 million public funds under the US Chips Act.
It's an illustration of how quickly the market is moving, and how central photonic chips could be to the continuing race to retain an influential stake in AI and technological development more generally.
Europe's bid to establish itself as a global leader in photonic chips â exemplified by the Eindhoven project â is the kind of strategy that makes sense in a globalised economy, says Joris Teer, policy analyst at the European Institute for Security Studies in Brussels.
If the continent can take a lead in photonic chips, then future developments in AI and data will partly be dependent on Europe.
""Europe knows that its high energy and labour costs, skilled workforce shortages, and lack of private and public capital make an across-the-board reindustrialisation strategy impossible"", Teer says.
""To retain influence, ensuring dependence on European parties rather than European autonomy is key.""
Once up and running, the Eindhoven project will provide a pilot production line which can be used multiple companies.
It will be open to businesses across Europe and beyond, who can design and build chips without having to develop their own expensive production facilities.
""What we want to establish here is what we call a foundry model; there's no intention to have our own product,"" Ton van Mol says.
Instead, they will produce to order for outside customers.
It's the same idea which, at huge scale, has brought Taiwanese-based giant TSMC a position of global dominance in the manufacture of microchips.
Photonic chips are already in use, in highly specialised areas in data centres and in Lidar (light detection and ranging) sensor technology, for instance.
For van Mol of TNO, helping the industry to scale up, by easing production challenges and increasing capacity, is the key step to moving them into the tech we use in our daily lives, like phones and laptops, and to drive innovation in areas like medical scanners and 6G communications.
""The demand is there"", says van Mol, ""and the end goal is to have, in Europe, manufacturing of these chips, which is strategically very important because these chips [could] become the backbone of all data and telecom.""

83,2026-11-18,"This 1968 Plymouth Barracuda fastback is one of 70 Super Stock models built for use in NHRA SS/AA competition, and it is claimed to have been owned and raced by William Elias “Billy the Kid” Stepp of Ohio. The engine had been replaced before the current owner’s purchase in 2014, and subsequent work involved installing a 472ci Hemi V8 with an Indy Maxx aluminum block and Indy 426-HiPo cylinder heads, a Molnar crankshaft and connecting rods, and JE pistons. The A727 three-speed automatic transmission was also overhauled, the dual Holley carburetors were rebuilt, and custom graphics were added in homage to Stepp. The car rides on 15” alloy wheels with a four-link rear suspension, Penske remote-reservoir coilovers, and four-wheel disc brakes. Other highlights include a fiberglass hood and front fenders, Lexan side windows, wheelie bars, a roll cage, a Turbo Action shifter, and AutoMeter supplementary gauges. This BO29 Barracuda is now offered on dealer consignment by Cantech Automotive—a BaT Local Partner—with spare parts, service records, and a bill of sale.

The Barracuda Super Stock was produced exclusively as a fastback using a fiberglass hood and front fenders as well as a lightweight steel front bumper and doors. The heater, body sealer, sound deadening, side mirrors, and rear seats were omitted to achieve a total weight of approximately 3,000 pounds.

This car is finished in black, and custom gold and red decals were added under current ownership. Details include a hood scoop, Lexan side windows, a shut-off switch, and wheelie bars as well as black-painted bumpers, bright trim, a flip-up fuel cap, headlights and taillamps, and round quarter-panel side-marker lights.

Monocoque-style 15” alloy wheels are wrapped in Goodyear Eagle racing tires measuring 27.0×4.5” up front and 32.0×14.5” out back. The suspension incorporates a steering drag link that passes through the oil pan, a four-bar rear setup with a diagonal link, and Penske remote-reservoir coilovers at all four corners. Braking is handled by four-wheel discs with slotted rotors all around.

The replacement 472ci Hemi V8 was built in 2015 using an Indy Maxx aluminum block and Indy 426-HiPo cylinder heads, a Molnar Technologies crankshaft, Molnar connecting rods, JE pistons, and Comp Cams valve springs, seats, and retainers. The dual Holley carburetors were also rebuilt at that time and are fitted atop a Mopar cross-ram aluminum intake manifold. Additional components include a Meziere Enterprises water pump, four-into-one tubular headers, a Barry Grant Fuel Systems fuel regulator, a custom aluminum oil pan, an MSD 7AL-3 ignition module, and an aluminum radiator cooled by an electric fan.

The A727 three-speed automatic transmission was upgraded and overhauled in 2014 at Cope Racing Transmissions of Lowell, Indiana. Power is delivered to the rear wheels through an 8¾” rear end with a limited-slip differential and Mark Williams Enterprises axles. A safety loop surrounds the driveshaft.

The cabin is protected by an NHRA-inspected roll cage and features bucket seats trimmed in black with color-coordinated door panels and carpeting. A Racers Choice Inc harness is provided for the driver, and additional appointments include a Turbo Action Cheetah SCS shifter and an overhead Painless Wiring toggle-switch control panel. The windows are raised and lowered via seatbelt-material straps instead of crank mechanisms. The roll cage will need to be recertified before the Plymouth is returned to competition use.

The Grant GT steering wheel sits ahead of a factory instrument cluster consisting of a 120-mph speedometer, a Performance Indicator gauge, and a combination gauge. AutoMeter supplementary instruments include a dash-mounted tachometer and gauges displaying oil pressure, coolant temperature, and fuel pressure. A liquid-filled Lamb Components brake-pressure gauge is positioned to the left of the steering wheel. The five-digit odometer shows 43k miles, “very few” of which have been added under current ownership, according to the seller.

A fuel cell is located in the trunk along with a Barry Grant Fuel Systems electric fuel pump and dual batteries.

Correspondence with marque specialists Greg Fernald and Jim Schild is presented above.

A magnesium cross-ram intake manifold will accompany the car along with a Northland radiator that can be viewed in the photo gallery below.
The vehicle is not legal for road use and carries no title or registration. It is being offered on a bill of sale.

84,2026-11-18,"BMWBLOG » Models » BMW M3
 BMW M3
The anniversary special comes in G80 Sedan and G81 Touring flavors, wears two exclusive colors, and is limited to Japan.
   Adrian Padeanu  
 November 18, 2026
2026 isn’t over just yet, so BMW still has time to celebrate 40 years since the original M3 hit the market in 1986. And although the internet has been ablaze this week with first drives of the electric M3 (ZA0), there’s still plenty to talk about when it comes to the good old combustion-powered G80 sedan and G81 Touring.
Enter the M3 Edition 40 Jahre, a Japan-exclusive special edition marking four decades of the iconic performance car, with exclusive colors, extra carbon fiber, and a production run limited, naturally, to just 40 cars. Buyers can choose between Individual Laguna Seca Blue and Wildberry Metallic, with BMW splitting production evenly between the two.
Each body style gets 10 examples in each color, meaning just 20 sedans and 20 wagons will be available in the Land of the Rising Sun. Regardless of which one you go for, all cars ride on 19-inch front and 20-inch rear forged M wheels finished in Gold Bronze. Beneath the flashy wheels are M Compound brakes with black high-gloss calipers, and both body styles get an assortment of carbon-fiber exterior upgrades.

The interior isn’t standard fare either, as BMW Japan is sprucing things up with carbon-fiber bucket seats, full Merino leather, and an exclusive interior badge above the cupholder cover. Other additions include a heated steering wheel, acoustic glass, illuminated entry sills, an M-striped roof, and the M Drive Professional package. Opt for the M3 Touring, and BMW throws in an electric tailgate and the luggage compartment package.
Interested? Prepare to fork out 17.61 million yen for the M3 Edition 40 Jahre sedan, while the more practical Touring commands 17.71 million yen. Both prices include Japan’s consumption tax but exclude other applicable expenses. BMW will begin deliveries toward the end of the month and sell the cars through its authorized dealer network in Japan.
Although the electric M3 (ZA0) is right around the corner, BMW isn’t ready to pull the plug on the current combustion-powered models just yet. As we’ve previously reported, production of the M3 G80 sedan and G81 Touring is expected to continue until later in 2027. That gives enthusiasts a few more months or so to get their hands on the twin-turbo inline-six. That’s despite the next-generation 3 Series (G50) already rolling off the assembly line, as BMW is keeping the old M3 models around a little longer before the next-generation M3 arrives.
Looking ahead, BMW is expected to launch the new inline-six-powered M3 in 2028 as the G84, reportedly with mild-hybrid assistance. If our sources are accurate, a new M3 Touring should follow soon after under the rumored G88 codename.
See more of our coverage in your search results.

Trending
You may also like

©2026 BMWBLOG - operated by iMedia Network, Inc."
85,2026-11-18,"Bitcoin halted downside as Nvidia confidence returned, with its stock gaining 3% premarket and South Korea’s tech-heavy KOSPI reclaiming 5,000 points.
Bitcoin (BTC) steadied on Wednesday as US stock futures rose after Nvidia reported stronger-than-expected quarterly revenue.
Key points:
Data from TradingView showed Bitcoin returning to the $64,000–$66,000 range it had traded in since Nov. 15.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView
Bitcoin briefly fell below the range on Tuesday ahead of Nvidia’s earnings release. Nvidia then reported third-quarter revenue of $115.8 billion, above expectations of $108 billion, and forecast $130 billion for fourth quarter. Its shares rose about 3% to $160.60 in Wednesday’s premarket trading.

Nvidia futures one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView
“Our momentum is real. NVIDIA is an exceptional technology leader, powered by the relentless innovation of our people,” interim CEO Colette Kress commented in a press release.
The momentum was reflected in broader equities performance, with Nasdaq 100 futures gaining 1%. South Korea’s tech-heavy KOSPI Composite closed 2% higher at 5,067 Wednesday, bouncing back above 5,000 after its lowest daily close since February.

KOSPI one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView
Meanwhile, Nvidia’s board rejected calls to reinstate former CEO Jensen Huang. On the earnings call, board chairman Harvey Jones said the directors had unanimously accepted Huang’s resignation.
“I’m good with Colette. She is clearly competent and strong. I feel that Nvidia is in a good spot and over time the right leadership will emerge,” Ross Gerber, cofounder, CEO and CIO at investment manager Gerber Kawasaki, wrote on X. Gerber compared Kress to Apple CEO Tim Cook and called it a “new chapter” for Nvidia.
Optimism allowed Bitcoin to stay above 18-week lows of $62,000 seen Sunday. Analyzing Binance order-book composition, crypto research platform Glassnode emphasized the importance of the area around the $60,000 mark.
Related: Bitcoin ETFs lose $240M as CryptoQuant flags bearish market phase
“Within 5% of price, resting bids on Binance and Coinbase now outweigh asks about 2.3x. Since late August, bids have usually outweighed asks only slightly,” it wrote in the latest edition of The Week Onchain newsletter.

Binance BTC spot order-book liquidity data. Source: Glassnode
Glassnode flagged $60,000 as the lower end of a range where falling prices could force traders out of long positions and accelerate the sell-off. It added that the majority of forced position closures had “already happened” on the initial move down.

Bitcoin futures liquidation heatmap. Source: Glassnode
Cointelegraph is committed to providing independent, high-quality journalism across the crypto, blockchain, AI, and fintech industries.
All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy."
86,2026-11-18,"Anthropic is a brilliant model company with an impossible balance sheet. I say that as someone who does not own the stock, does not short it, and does not root for its collapse. The Claude models are genuinely excellent, and I have watched them do things that would have seemed like science fiction just a few years ago. But brilliance in engineering does not guarantee survival in finance, and the numbers published ahead of this IPO tell a story that no amount of benchmark superiority can rewrite. The IPO narrative itself is the first red flag. Anthropic initially filed for a valuation above $2 trillion, according to a filing reported on Tuesday, Sept. 29, with the company seeking that valuation in an initial public offering planned for autumn [1]. Then the market wobbled. Reuters reported that investors doubted whether the IPO roadshow could start on time after AI stocks crashed and the Nasdaq posted its worst day since April 2025 [2]. The reported valuation pulled back toward a more ""reasonable"" $1.6 trillion. That whiplash is not a temporary bump on the road to glory; it is a marketing story laid over a cash-flow nightmare, and the market is beginning to read the fine print. I see only two escape routes from here: an endless rolling of debt, or a rescue acquisition by a company that can subsidize the losses. Neither one is survival as a standalone firm. This article walks through the arithmetic, the required revenue curve, the cost of borrowing, and the likely acquirer. My conclusion is that Anthropic ends up as a highly capable division of some future diversified giant, not a standalone public company that lives to see 2032.Nearly $800 Billion in Signed Obligations Is Not a Growth Story Start with the arithmetic. Anthropic's commitments to Broadcom, AMD, Microsoft, Google, and various infrastructure partners add up to just under $800 billion stretching out toward 2031. These are signed obligations, not speculative projections about what the company might want to buy someday. Gary Gensler, who ran the SEC under Biden, pointed to estimates that as much as $800 billion in commitments are on the books and warned that the AI boom is a bubble that will likely burst and push the United States into recession before the 2028 presidential election [3]. When the former top securities regulator of the United States flags an $800 billion commitment load three days before an IPO prices, I pay attention. Against that stack of obligations sits revenue that is not even $20 billion. A leaked prospectus reportedly shows a $42 billion net loss, $518 billion in unfunded spending commitments, and only $20 billion in cash [4]. Even if portions of that accounting are non-cash, the direction is unmistakable. No business in history has ever grown out of a liability-to-revenue ratio like this by simply running the playbook harder. The structural trap here is that these are take-or-pay style commitments. Data centers, chip reservations, and energy contracts are not optional line items you can trim when demand disappoints. In a normal company, when the quarter turns cold, you cut the marketing budget, you freeze hiring, you delay the office renovation. Anthropic cannot do that. The money is already spoken for, and the bills arrive on schedule whether customers show up or not. That removes the one lever every normal business pulls in a downturn, and it means the company has to grow into its obligations regardless of the macroeconomic weather.The Math Says Anthropic Needs $100 Billion a Year. I Don't See Where It Comes From. Run the rough calculation on the back of an envelope. To cover operating expenses, leases, hardware refresh cycles, and future training compute, Anthropic needs something on the order of $100 billion in annual revenue. That is the all-in breakeven number, not the aspirational one. Consumer and prosumer subscriptions cannot close this gap. Even at $100 to $200 a month, individual and corporate seat fees are a rounding error against infrastructure spending at this scale. I have noted in my own interviews that users have little incentive to pay Anthropic $200 a month when comparable coding and bug-fixing inference is available from DeepSeek for $5 or less [5]. OpenAI's price cuts make the same point from the other direction. The company recently reduced the price of its GPT-5.6 Luna model by 80%, three weeks after launch, putting Luna at 20 cents per million input tokens and $1.20 per million output tokens [6]. When the two biggest Western labs are racing each other to the bottom on price while Chinese open-weight models undercut them both, subscription revenue isn't a revenue model that can fund much of anything for very long. The uncomfortable irony is that the money has to come from new borrowing justified by more spending on infrastructure. Anthropic must in essence spend its way to a revenue number it cannot yet charge for. Training costs are expensed in advance of the revenue the trained models generate, which is the basic accounting reality of the business [7]. But that's also the doom loop: the only path to the revenue scale that covers $100 billion a year is more compute, and more compute deepens the hole before it fills it. There is a version of this that works, but it requires either a subsidy from a parent company or a monopoly on pricing power. Anthropic currently has neither.Borrowing at 16% Is Not a Business Model, It's a Countdown Watch the cost of money, because that is where this story gets ugly. The dynamic is Ponzi-like in plain terms: keep raising, keep rolling over, keep hoping the rate environment stays friendly. Except it is clearly not staying friendly. CoreWeave debt pricing near a 16% yield is the tell. When an AI infrastructure borrower has to pay distressed-style rates to attract buyers, the market is telling you that it no longer believes the revenue projections behind the paper. I have been tracking this dynamic for months and comparing it to the subprime crisis of 2008. As I said in a recent Health Ranger Report, the banking industry's exposure to AI startups bears a striking resemblance to the subprime mortgage crisis, where institutions held tranches of debt rated as high quality but built on a fragile foundation [8]. I have also pointed to the circular accounting at work here, where companies secure contracts with large corporations and then leverage those agreements to procure billions of dollars in lending, often based on the projected value of GPU hardware that has not yet been delivered or manufactured [9]. This is the same pattern of cumulative obligations stacked on top of each other that historically ends the same way. Now consider the refinancing wall. Debt borrowed at 6% to 8% that comes due in a 16% market is self-defeating; every rollover makes the hole deeper, not shallower. Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The math says Anthropic does not survive as a standalone company. The lesson says you should not let your own intelligence depend on any company that sells you tokens on a meter. Own your own compute, and steer clear of Anthropic's industry drama in the mean time.References   Anthropic IPO Prospectus Warns Investors of âExistential Risk to Humanityâ. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-prospectus-warns-existential-risk-humanity.html  AI Stocks Crash, Anthropicâs IPO Wobbles, GPT-6 Cyber Goes Critical | Weekly Digest. 2026-11-06T18:18:43.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/ai-stocks-crash-anthropics-ipo-wobbles  Gensler Warns AI Bubble Will Burst Before 2028 As Warsh Reveals Fed Is Modeling A Crash. 2026-11-17T18:10:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/economics/gensler-warns-ai-bubble-will-burst-2028-warsh-reveals-fed-modeling-crash  Anthropicâs IPO Looks Like a Speculative Disaster in the Making: Why Iâm Staying Away. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-speculative-disaster-in-the-making.html  Decentralize TV - I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. Anthropic Will Probably Survive. 2026-03-09T18:08:36Z. https://insights.priva.cat/p/anthropic-will-probably-survive-trumps/comments  The Compute Famine Isnât an Accident â Itâs an Engineered Crisis to Strip You of Power, AI, and Freedom. 2026-08-20T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-08-20-the-compute-famine-isnt-an-accident.html  Report: U.S. Companies Turn to Cheaper Chinese AI Models Due To Cost and Flexibility - NaturalNews.com, July 15, 2026. by NaturalNews.com  Steve Eisman: What If OpenAI Actually Fails?. 2026-09-09T15:00:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/ai/steve-eisman-what-if-openai-actually-fails  Agent-to-Agent Virus, Gemini 3.7 Flash, New Frontier Models | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-14T19:24:47.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agent-to-agent-virus-gemini-37-flash  Agents Go Rogue, SpaceX Burns $18B, Open Weights Win | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-07T15:45:15.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agents-go-rogue-spacex-burns-18b  Explainer Infographic 


The IPO narrative itself is the first red flag. Anthropic initially filed for a valuation above $2 trillion, according to a filing reported on Tuesday, Sept. 29, with the company seeking that valuation in an initial public offering planned for autumn [1]. Then the market wobbled. Reuters reported that investors doubted whether the IPO roadshow could start on time after AI stocks crashed and the Nasdaq posted its worst day since April 2025 [2]. The reported valuation pulled back toward a more ""reasonable"" $1.6 trillion. That whiplash is not a temporary bump on the road to glory; it is a marketing story laid over a cash-flow nightmare, and the market is beginning to read the fine print. I see only two escape routes from here: an endless rolling of debt, or a rescue acquisition by a company that can subsidize the losses. Neither one is survival as a standalone firm. This article walks through the arithmetic, the required revenue curve, the cost of borrowing, and the likely acquirer. My conclusion is that Anthropic ends up as a highly capable division of some future diversified giant, not a standalone public company that lives to see 2032.Nearly $800 Billion in Signed Obligations Is Not a Growth Story Start with the arithmetic. Anthropic's commitments to Broadcom, AMD, Microsoft, Google, and various infrastructure partners add up to just under $800 billion stretching out toward 2031. These are signed obligations, not speculative projections about what the company might want to buy someday. Gary Gensler, who ran the SEC under Biden, pointed to estimates that as much as $800 billion in commitments are on the books and warned that the AI boom is a bubble that will likely burst and push the United States into recession before the 2028 presidential election [3]. When the former top securities regulator of the United States flags an $800 billion commitment load three days before an IPO prices, I pay attention. Against that stack of obligations sits revenue that is not even $20 billion. A leaked prospectus reportedly shows a $42 billion net loss, $518 billion in unfunded spending commitments, and only $20 billion in cash [4]. Even if portions of that accounting are non-cash, the direction is unmistakable. No business in history has ever grown out of a liability-to-revenue ratio like this by simply running the playbook harder. The structural trap here is that these are take-or-pay style commitments. Data centers, chip reservations, and energy contracts are not optional line items you can trim when demand disappoints. In a normal company, when the quarter turns cold, you cut the marketing budget, you freeze hiring, you delay the office renovation. Anthropic cannot do that. The money is already spoken for, and the bills arrive on schedule whether customers show up or not. That removes the one lever every normal business pulls in a downturn, and it means the company has to grow into its obligations regardless of the macroeconomic weather.The Math Says Anthropic Needs $100 Billion a Year. I Don't See Where It Comes From. Run the rough calculation on the back of an envelope. To cover operating expenses, leases, hardware refresh cycles, and future training compute, Anthropic needs something on the order of $100 billion in annual revenue. That is the all-in breakeven number, not the aspirational one. Consumer and prosumer subscriptions cannot close this gap. Even at $100 to $200 a month, individual and corporate seat fees are a rounding error against infrastructure spending at this scale. I have noted in my own interviews that users have little incentive to pay Anthropic $200 a month when comparable coding and bug-fixing inference is available from DeepSeek for $5 or less [5]. OpenAI's price cuts make the same point from the other direction. The company recently reduced the price of its GPT-5.6 Luna model by 80%, three weeks after launch, putting Luna at 20 cents per million input tokens and $1.20 per million output tokens [6]. When the two biggest Western labs are racing each other to the bottom on price while Chinese open-weight models undercut them both, subscription revenue isn't a revenue model that can fund much of anything for very long. The uncomfortable irony is that the money has to come from new borrowing justified by more spending on infrastructure. Anthropic must in essence spend its way to a revenue number it cannot yet charge for. Training costs are expensed in advance of the revenue the trained models generate, which is the basic accounting reality of the business [7]. But that's also the doom loop: the only path to the revenue scale that covers $100 billion a year is more compute, and more compute deepens the hole before it fills it. There is a version of this that works, but it requires either a subsidy from a parent company or a monopoly on pricing power. Anthropic currently has neither.Borrowing at 16% Is Not a Business Model, It's a Countdown Watch the cost of money, because that is where this story gets ugly. The dynamic is Ponzi-like in plain terms: keep raising, keep rolling over, keep hoping the rate environment stays friendly. Except it is clearly not staying friendly. CoreWeave debt pricing near a 16% yield is the tell. When an AI infrastructure borrower has to pay distressed-style rates to attract buyers, the market is telling you that it no longer believes the revenue projections behind the paper. I have been tracking this dynamic for months and comparing it to the subprime crisis of 2008. As I said in a recent Health Ranger Report, the banking industry's exposure to AI startups bears a striking resemblance to the subprime mortgage crisis, where institutions held tranches of debt rated as high quality but built on a fragile foundation [8]. I have also pointed to the circular accounting at work here, where companies secure contracts with large corporations and then leverage those agreements to procure billions of dollars in lending, often based on the projected value of GPU hardware that has not yet been delivered or manufactured [9]. This is the same pattern of cumulative obligations stacked on top of each other that historically ends the same way. Now consider the refinancing wall. Debt borrowed at 6% to 8% that comes due in a 16% market is self-defeating; every rollover makes the hole deeper, not shallower. Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. NaturalNews.com, July 15, 2026. by NaturalNews.com  Steve Eisman: What If OpenAI Actually Fails?. 2026-09-09T15:00:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/ai/steve-eisman-what-if-openai-actually-fails  Agent-to-Agent Virus, Gemini 3.7 Flash, New Frontier Models | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-14T19:24:47.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agent-to-agent-virus-gemini-37-flash  Agents Go Rogue, SpaceX Burns $18B, Open Weights Win | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-07T15:45:15.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agents-go-rogue-spacex-burns-18b  Explainer Infographic 


I see only two escape routes from here: an endless rolling of debt, or a rescue acquisition by a company that can subsidize the losses. Neither one is survival as a standalone firm. This article walks through the arithmetic, the required revenue curve, the cost of borrowing, and the likely acquirer. My conclusion is that Anthropic ends up as a highly capable division of some future diversified giant, not a standalone public company that lives to see 2032.Nearly $800 Billion in Signed Obligations Is Not a Growth Story Start with the arithmetic. Anthropic's commitments to Broadcom, AMD, Microsoft, Google, and various infrastructure partners add up to just under $800 billion stretching out toward 2031. These are signed obligations, not speculative projections about what the company might want to buy someday. Gary Gensler, who ran the SEC under Biden, pointed to estimates that as much as $800 billion in commitments are on the books and warned that the AI boom is a bubble that will likely burst and push the United States into recession before the 2028 presidential election [3]. When the former top securities regulator of the United States flags an $800 billion commitment load three days before an IPO prices, I pay attention. Against that stack of obligations sits revenue that is not even $20 billion. A leaked prospectus reportedly shows a $42 billion net loss, $518 billion in unfunded spending commitments, and only $20 billion in cash [4]. Even if portions of that accounting are non-cash, the direction is unmistakable. No business in history has ever grown out of a liability-to-revenue ratio like this by simply running the playbook harder. The structural trap here is that these are take-or-pay style commitments. Data centers, chip reservations, and energy contracts are not optional line items you can trim when demand disappoints. In a normal company, when the quarter turns cold, you cut the marketing budget, you freeze hiring, you delay the office renovation. Anthropic cannot do that. The money is already spoken for, and the bills arrive on schedule whether customers show up or not. That removes the one lever every normal business pulls in a downturn, and it means the company has to grow into its obligations regardless of the macroeconomic weather.The Math Says Anthropic Needs $100 Billion a Year. I Don't See Where It Comes From. Run the rough calculation on the back of an envelope. To cover operating expenses, leases, hardware refresh cycles, and future training compute, Anthropic needs something on the order of $100 billion in annual revenue. That is the all-in breakeven number, not the aspirational one. Consumer and prosumer subscriptions cannot close this gap. Even at $100 to $200 a month, individual and corporate seat fees are a rounding error against infrastructure spending at this scale. I have noted in my own interviews that users have little incentive to pay Anthropic $200 a month when comparable coding and bug-fixing inference is available from DeepSeek for $5 or less [5]. OpenAI's price cuts make the same point from the other direction. The company recently reduced the price of its GPT-5.6 Luna model by 80%, three weeks after launch, putting Luna at 20 cents per million input tokens and $1.20 per million output tokens [6]. When the two biggest Western labs are racing each other to the bottom on price while Chinese open-weight models undercut them both, subscription revenue isn't a revenue model that can fund much of anything for very long. The uncomfortable irony is that the money has to come from new borrowing justified by more spending on infrastructure. Anthropic must in essence spend its way to a revenue number it cannot yet charge for. Training costs are expensed in advance of the revenue the trained models generate, which is the basic accounting reality of the business [7]. But that's also the doom loop: the only path to the revenue scale that covers $100 billion a year is more compute, and more compute deepens the hole before it fills it. There is a version of this that works, but it requires either a subsidy from a parent company or a monopoly on pricing power. Anthropic currently has neither.Borrowing at 16% Is Not a Business Model, It's a Countdown Watch the cost of money, because that is where this story gets ugly. The dynamic is Ponzi-like in plain terms: keep raising, keep rolling over, keep hoping the rate environment stays friendly. Except it is clearly not staying friendly. CoreWeave debt pricing near a 16% yield is the tell. When an AI infrastructure borrower has to pay distressed-style rates to attract buyers, the market is telling you that it no longer believes the revenue projections behind the paper. I have been tracking this dynamic for months and comparing it to the subprime crisis of 2008. As I said in a recent Health Ranger Report, the banking industry's exposure to AI startups bears a striking resemblance to the subprime mortgage crisis, where institutions held tranches of debt rated as high quality but built on a fragile foundation [8]. I have also pointed to the circular accounting at work here, where companies secure contracts with large corporations and then leverage those agreements to procure billions of dollars in lending, often based on the projected value of GPU hardware that has not yet been delivered or manufactured [9]. This is the same pattern of cumulative obligations stacked on top of each other that historically ends the same way. Now consider the refinancing wall. Debt borrowed at 6% to 8% that comes due in a 16% market is self-defeating; every rollover makes the hole deeper, not shallower. Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The math says Anthropic does not survive as a standalone company. The lesson says you should not let your own intelligence depend on any company that sells you tokens on a meter. Own your own compute, and steer clear of Anthropic's industry drama in the mean time.References   Anthropic IPO Prospectus Warns Investors of âExistential Risk to Humanityâ. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-prospectus-warns-existential-risk-humanity.html  AI Stocks Crash, Anthropicâs IPO Wobbles, GPT-6 Cyber Goes Critical | Weekly Digest. 2026-11-06T18:18:43.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/ai-stocks-crash-anthropics-ipo-wobbles  Gensler Warns AI Bubble Will Burst Before 2028 As Warsh Reveals Fed Is Modeling A Crash. 2026-11-17T18:10:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/economics/gensler-warns-ai-bubble-will-burst-2028-warsh-reveals-fed-modeling-crash  Anthropicâs IPO Looks Like a Speculative Disaster in the Making: Why Iâm Staying Away. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-speculative-disaster-in-the-making.html  Decentralize TV - As I said in a recent Health Ranger Report, the banking industry's exposure to AI startups bears a striking resemblance to the subprime mortgage crisis, where institutions held tranches of debt rated as high quality but built on a fragile foundation [8]. I have also pointed to the circular accounting at work here, where companies secure contracts with large corporations and then leverage those agreements to procure billions of dollars in lending, often based on the projected value of GPU hardware that has not yet been delivered or manufactured [9]. This is the same pattern of cumulative obligations stacked on top of each other that historically ends the same way. Now consider the refinancing wall. Debt borrowed at 6% to 8% that comes due in a 16% market is self-defeating; every rollover makes the hole deeper, not shallower. Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The math says Anthropic does not survive as a standalone company. The lesson says you should not let your own intelligence depend on any company that sells you tokens on a meter. Own your own compute, and steer clear of Anthropic's industry drama in the mean time.References   Anthropic IPO Prospectus Warns Investors of âExistential Risk to Humanityâ. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-prospectus-warns-existential-risk-humanity.html  AI Stocks Crash, Anthropicâs IPO Wobbles, GPT-6 Cyber Goes Critical | Weekly Digest. 2026-11-06T18:18:43.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/ai-stocks-crash-anthropics-ipo-wobbles  Gensler Warns AI Bubble Will Burst Before 2028 As Warsh Reveals Fed Is Modeling A Crash. 2026-11-17T18:10:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/economics/gensler-warns-ai-bubble-will-burst-2028-warsh-reveals-fed-modeling-crash  Anthropicâs IPO Looks Like a Speculative Disaster in the Making: Why Iâm Staying Away. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-speculative-disaster-in-the-making.html  Decentralize TV - NaturalNews.com, July 15, 2026. by NaturalNews.com  Steve Eisman: What If OpenAI Actually Fails?. 2026-09-09T15:00:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/ai/steve-eisman-what-if-openai-actually-fails  Agent-to-Agent Virus, Gemini 3.7 Flash, New Frontier Models | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-14T19:24:47.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agent-to-agent-virus-gemini-37-flash  Agents Go Rogue, SpaceX Burns $18B, Open Weights Win | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-07T15:45:15.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agents-go-rogue-spacex-burns-18b  Explainer Infographic 


 Against that stack of obligations sits revenue that is not even $20 billion. A leaked prospectus reportedly shows a $42 billion net loss, $518 billion in unfunded spending commitments, and only $20 billion in cash [4]. Even if portions of that accounting are non-cash, the direction is unmistakable. No business in history has ever grown out of a liability-to-revenue ratio like this by simply running the playbook harder. The structural trap here is that these are take-or-pay style commitments. Data centers, chip reservations, and energy contracts are not optional line items you can trim when demand disappoints. In a normal company, when the quarter turns cold, you cut the marketing budget, you freeze hiring, you delay the office renovation. Anthropic cannot do that. The money is already spoken for, and the bills arrive on schedule whether customers show up or not. That removes the one lever every normal business pulls in a downturn, and it means the company has to grow into its obligations regardless of the macroeconomic weather.The Math Says Anthropic Needs $100 Billion a Year. I Don't See Where It Comes From. Run the rough calculation on the back of an envelope. To cover operating expenses, leases, hardware refresh cycles, and future training compute, Anthropic needs something on the order of $100 billion in annual revenue. That is the all-in breakeven number, not the aspirational one. Consumer and prosumer subscriptions cannot close this gap. Even at $100 to $200 a month, individual and corporate seat fees are a rounding error against infrastructure spending at this scale. I have noted in my own interviews that users have little incentive to pay Anthropic $200 a month when comparable coding and bug-fixing inference is available from DeepSeek for $5 or less [5]. OpenAI's price cuts make the same point from the other direction. The company recently reduced the price of its GPT-5.6 Luna model by 80%, three weeks after launch, putting Luna at 20 cents per million input tokens and $1.20 per million output tokens [6]. When the two biggest Western labs are racing each other to the bottom on price while Chinese open-weight models undercut them both, subscription revenue isn't a revenue model that can fund much of anything for very long. The uncomfortable irony is that the money has to come from new borrowing justified by more spending on infrastructure. Anthropic must in essence spend its way to a revenue number it cannot yet charge for. Training costs are expensed in advance of the revenue the trained models generate, which is the basic accounting reality of the business [7]. But that's also the doom loop: the only path to the revenue scale that covers $100 billion a year is more compute, and more compute deepens the hole before it fills it. There is a version of this that works, but it requires either a subsidy from a parent company or a monopoly on pricing power. Anthropic currently has neither.Borrowing at 16% Is Not a Business Model, It's a Countdown Watch the cost of money, because that is where this story gets ugly.
