Interview with Hakeem - Mike Adams - Decentralize.TV, May 25, 2026. by Mike Adams - Decentralize.TV  OpenAI Cuts GPT 5.6 Luna Price by 80% as Chinese Models Close In - NaturalNews.com, July 31, 2026. by NaturalNews.com  Where Anthropicâs Revenue Goes, and Why It Matters. 2026-11-11T04:07:55Z. https://www.morningstar.com/stocks/where-anthropics-revenue-goes-why-it-matters  Health Ranger Report - NEXT DOT COM BUBBLE - Mike Adams - BrightVideos.com, June 03, 2026. by Mike Adams - BrightVideos.com  Bright Videos News - AI DOOM PSYOP OPEN SOURCE BAN - Mike Adams - BrightVideos.com, September 14, 2026. by Mike Adams - BrightVideos.com  Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei could still be trying to make a deal with Pentagon. 2026-03-05T16:45:51.000Z. https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/05/anthropic-ceo-dario-amodei-could-still-be-trying-to-make-a-deal-with-pentagon/  Comments - Anthropic Will Probably Survive. 2026-03-09T18:08:36Z. https://insights.priva.cat/p/anthropic-will-probably-survive-trumps/comments  The Compute Famine Isnât an Accident â Itâs an Engineered Crisis to Strip You of Power, AI, and Freedom. 2026-08-20T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-08-20-the-compute-famine-isnt-an-accident.html  Report: U.S. Companies Turn to Cheaper Chinese AI Models Due To Cost and Flexibility - NaturalNews.com, July 15, 2026. by NaturalNews.com  Steve Eisman: What If OpenAI Actually Fails?. 2026-09-09T15:00:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/ai/steve-eisman-what-if-openai-actually-fails  Agent-to-Agent Virus, Gemini 3.7 Flash, New Frontier Models | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-14T19:24:47.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agent-to-agent-virus-gemini-37-flash  Agents Go Rogue, SpaceX Burns $18B, Open Weights Win | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-07T15:45:15.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agents-go-rogue-spacex-burns-18b  Explainer Infographic 


 The structural trap here is that these are take-or-pay style commitments. Data centers, chip reservations, and energy contracts are not optional line items you can trim when demand disappoints. In a normal company, when the quarter turns cold, you cut the marketing budget, you freeze hiring, you delay the office renovation. Anthropic cannot do that. The money is already spoken for, and the bills arrive on schedule whether customers show up or not. That removes the one lever every normal business pulls in a downturn, and it means the company has to grow into its obligations regardless of the macroeconomic weather.The Math Says Anthropic Needs $100 Billion a Year. I Don't See Where It Comes From. Run the rough calculation on the back of an envelope. To cover operating expenses, leases, hardware refresh cycles, and future training compute, Anthropic needs something on the order of $100 billion in annual revenue. That is the all-in breakeven number, not the aspirational one. Consumer and prosumer subscriptions cannot close this gap. Even at $100 to $200 a month, individual and corporate seat fees are a rounding error against infrastructure spending at this scale. I have noted in my own interviews that users have little incentive to pay Anthropic $200 a month when comparable coding and bug-fixing inference is available from DeepSeek for $5 or less [5]. OpenAI's price cuts make the same point from the other direction. The company recently reduced the price of its GPT-5.6 Luna model by 80%, three weeks after launch, putting Luna at 20 cents per million input tokens and $1.20 per million output tokens [6]. When the two biggest Western labs are racing each other to the bottom on price while Chinese open-weight models undercut them both, subscription revenue isn't a revenue model that can fund much of anything for very long. The uncomfortable irony is that the money has to come from new borrowing justified by more spending on infrastructure. Anthropic must in essence spend its way to a revenue number it cannot yet charge for. Training costs are expensed in advance of the revenue the trained models generate, which is the basic accounting reality of the business [7]. But that's also the doom loop: the only path to the revenue scale that covers $100 billion a year is more compute, and more compute deepens the hole before it fills it. There is a version of this that works, but it requires either a subsidy from a parent company or a monopoly on pricing power. Anthropic currently has neither.Borrowing at 16% Is Not a Business Model, It's a Countdown Watch the cost of money, because that is where this story gets ugly. The dynamic is Ponzi-like in plain terms: keep raising, keep rolling over, keep hoping the rate environment stays friendly. Except it is clearly not staying friendly. CoreWeave debt pricing near a 16% yield is the tell. When an AI infrastructure borrower has to pay distressed-style rates to attract buyers, the market is telling you that it no longer believes the revenue projections behind the paper. I have been tracking this dynamic for months and comparing it to the subprime crisis of 2008. As I said in a recent Health Ranger Report, the banking industry's exposure to AI startups bears a striking resemblance to the subprime mortgage crisis, where institutions held tranches of debt rated as high quality but built on a fragile foundation [8]. I have also pointed to the circular accounting at work here, where companies secure contracts with large corporations and then leverage those agreements to procure billions of dollars in lending, often based on the projected value of GPU hardware that has not yet been delivered or manufactured [9]. This is the same pattern of cumulative obligations stacked on top of each other that historically ends the same way. Now consider the refinancing wall. Debt borrowed at 6% to 8% that comes due in a 16% market is self-defeating; every rollover makes the hole deeper, not shallower. Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The math says Anthropic does not survive as a standalone company. The lesson says you should not let your own intelligence depend on any company that sells you tokens on a meter. Own your own compute, and steer clear of Anthropic's industry drama in the mean time.References   Anthropic IPO Prospectus Warns Investors of âExistential Risk to Humanityâ. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-prospectus-warns-existential-risk-humanity.html  AI Stocks Crash, Anthropicâs IPO Wobbles, GPT-6 Cyber Goes Critical | Weekly Digest. 2026-11-06T18:18:43.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/ai-stocks-crash-anthropics-ipo-wobbles  Gensler Warns AI Bubble Will Burst Before 2028 As Warsh Reveals Fed Is Modeling A Crash. 2026-11-17T18:10:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/economics/gensler-warns-ai-bubble-will-burst-2028-warsh-reveals-fed-modeling-crash  Anthropicâs IPO Looks Like a Speculative Disaster in the Making: Why Iâm Staying Away. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-speculative-disaster-in-the-making.html  Decentralize TV - As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The math says Anthropic does not survive as a standalone company. The lesson says you should not let your own intelligence depend on any company that sells you tokens on a meter. Own your own compute, and steer clear of Anthropic's industry drama in the mean time.References   Anthropic IPO Prospectus Warns Investors of âExistential Risk to Humanityâ. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-prospectus-warns-existential-risk-humanity.html  AI Stocks Crash, Anthropicâs IPO Wobbles, GPT-6 Cyber Goes Critical | Weekly Digest. 2026-11-06T18:18:43.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/ai-stocks-crash-anthropics-ipo-wobbles  Gensler Warns AI Bubble Will Burst Before 2028 As Warsh Reveals Fed Is Modeling A Crash. 2026-11-17T18:10:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/economics/gensler-warns-ai-bubble-will-burst-2028-warsh-reveals-fed-modeling-crash  Anthropicâs IPO Looks Like a Speculative Disaster in the Making: Why Iâm Staying Away. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-speculative-disaster-in-the-making.html  Decentralize TV - Anthropic Will Probably Survive. 2026-03-09T18:08:36Z. https://insights.priva.cat/p/anthropic-will-probably-survive-trumps/comments  The Compute Famine Isnât an Accident â Itâs an Engineered Crisis to Strip You of Power, AI, and Freedom. 2026-08-20T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-08-20-the-compute-famine-isnt-an-accident.html  Report: U.S. Companies Turn to Cheaper Chinese AI Models Due To Cost and Flexibility - NaturalNews.com, July 15, 2026. by NaturalNews.com  Steve Eisman: What If OpenAI Actually Fails?. 2026-09-09T15:00:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/ai/steve-eisman-what-if-openai-actually-fails  Agent-to-Agent Virus, Gemini 3.7 Flash, New Frontier Models | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-14T19:24:47.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agent-to-agent-virus-gemini-37-flash  Agents Go Rogue, SpaceX Burns $18B, Open Weights Win | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-07T15:45:15.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agents-go-rogue-spacex-burns-18b  Explainer Infographic 


Run the rough calculation on the back of an envelope. To cover operating expenses, leases, hardware refresh cycles, and future training compute, Anthropic needs something on the order of $100 billion in annual revenue. That is the all-in breakeven number, not the aspirational one. Consumer and prosumer subscriptions cannot close this gap. Even at $100 to $200 a month, individual and corporate seat fees are a rounding error against infrastructure spending at this scale. I have noted in my own interviews that users have little incentive to pay Anthropic $200 a month when comparable coding and bug-fixing inference is available from DeepSeek for $5 or less [5]. OpenAI's price cuts make the same point from the other direction. The company recently reduced the price of its GPT-5.6 Luna model by 80%, three weeks after launch, putting Luna at 20 cents per million input tokens and $1.20 per million output tokens [6]. When the two biggest Western labs are racing each other to the bottom on price while Chinese open-weight models undercut them both, subscription revenue isn't a revenue model that can fund much of anything for very long. The uncomfortable irony is that the money has to come from new borrowing justified by more spending on infrastructure. Anthropic must in essence spend its way to a revenue number it cannot yet charge for. Training costs are expensed in advance of the revenue the trained models generate, which is the basic accounting reality of the business [7]. But that's also the doom loop: the only path to the revenue scale that covers $100 billion a year is more compute, and more compute deepens the hole before it fills it. There is a version of this that works, but it requires either a subsidy from a parent company or a monopoly on pricing power. Anthropic currently has neither.Borrowing at 16% Is Not a Business Model, It's a Countdown Watch the cost of money, because that is where this story gets ugly. The dynamic is Ponzi-like in plain terms: keep raising, keep rolling over, keep hoping the rate environment stays friendly. Except it is clearly not staying friendly. CoreWeave debt pricing near a 16% yield is the tell. When an AI infrastructure borrower has to pay distressed-style rates to attract buyers, the market is telling you that it no longer believes the revenue projections behind the paper. I have been tracking this dynamic for months and comparing it to the subprime crisis of 2008. As I said in a recent Health Ranger Report, the banking industry's exposure to AI startups bears a striking resemblance to the subprime mortgage crisis, where institutions held tranches of debt rated as high quality but built on a fragile foundation [8]. I have also pointed to the circular accounting at work here, where companies secure contracts with large corporations and then leverage those agreements to procure billions of dollars in lending, often based on the projected value of GPU hardware that has not yet been delivered or manufactured [9]. This is the same pattern of cumulative obligations stacked on top of each other that historically ends the same way. Now consider the refinancing wall. Debt borrowed at 6% to 8% that comes due in a 16% market is self-defeating; every rollover makes the hole deeper, not shallower. Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. The dynamic is Ponzi-like in plain terms: keep raising, keep rolling over, keep hoping the rate environment stays friendly. Except it is clearly not staying friendly. CoreWeave debt pricing near a 16% yield is the tell. When an AI infrastructure borrower has to pay distressed-style rates to attract buyers, the market is telling you that it no longer believes the revenue projections behind the paper. I have been tracking this dynamic for months and comparing it to the subprime crisis of 2008. As I said in a recent Health Ranger Report, the banking industry's exposure to AI startups bears a striking resemblance to the subprime mortgage crisis, where institutions held tranches of debt rated as high quality but built on a fragile foundation [8]. I have also pointed to the circular accounting at work here, where companies secure contracts with large corporations and then leverage those agreements to procure billions of dollars in lending, often based on the projected value of GPU hardware that has not yet been delivered or manufactured [9]. This is the same pattern of cumulative obligations stacked on top of each other that historically ends the same way. Now consider the refinancing wall. Debt borrowed at 6% to 8% that comes due in a 16% market is self-defeating; every rollover makes the hole deeper, not shallower. Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The math says Anthropic does not survive as a standalone company. The lesson says you should not let your own intelligence depend on any company that sells you tokens on a meter. Own your own compute, and steer clear of Anthropic's industry drama in the mean time.References   Anthropic IPO Prospectus Warns Investors of âExistential Risk to Humanityâ. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-prospectus-warns-existential-risk-humanity.html  AI Stocks Crash, Anthropicâs IPO Wobbles, GPT-6 Cyber Goes Critical | Weekly Digest. 2026-11-06T18:18:43.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/ai-stocks-crash-anthropics-ipo-wobbles  Gensler Warns AI Bubble Will Burst Before 2028 As Warsh Reveals Fed Is Modeling A Crash. 2026-11-17T18:10:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/economics/gensler-warns-ai-bubble-will-burst-2028-warsh-reveals-fed-modeling-crash  Anthropicâs IPO Looks Like a Speculative Disaster in the Making: Why Iâm Staying Away. 2026-09-30T06:00:00.000Z. https://www.naturalnews.com/2026-09-30-anthropic-ipo-speculative-disaster-in-the-making.html  Decentralize TV - Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The math says Anthropic does not survive as a standalone company. The lesson says you should not let your own intelligence depend on any company that sells you tokens on a meter. Watch the cost of money, because that is where this story gets ugly. The dynamic is Ponzi-like in plain terms: keep raising, keep rolling over, keep hoping the rate environment stays friendly. Except it is clearly not staying friendly. CoreWeave debt pricing near a 16% yield is the tell. When an AI infrastructure borrower has to pay distressed-style rates to attract buyers, the market is telling you that it no longer believes the revenue projections behind the paper. I have been tracking this dynamic for months and comparing it to the subprime crisis of 2008. As I said in a recent Health Ranger Report, the banking industry's exposure to AI startups bears a striking resemblance to the subprime mortgage crisis, where institutions held tranches of debt rated as high quality but built on a fragile foundation [8]. I have also pointed to the circular accounting at work here, where companies secure contracts with large corporations and then leverage those agreements to procure billions of dollars in lending, often based on the projected value of GPU hardware that has not yet been delivered or manufactured [9]. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The math says Anthropic does not survive as a standalone company. The lesson says you should not let your own intelligence depend on any company that sells you tokens on a meter. NaturalNews.com, July 15, 2026. by NaturalNews.com  Steve Eisman: What If OpenAI Actually Fails?. 2026-09-09T15:00:00.000Z. https://www.zerohedge.com/ai/steve-eisman-what-if-openai-actually-fails  Agent-to-Agent Virus, Gemini 3.7 Flash, New Frontier Models | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-14T19:24:47.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agent-to-agent-virus-gemini-37-flash  Agents Go Rogue, SpaceX Burns $18B, Open Weights Win | Weekly Digest. 2026-08-07T15:45:15.000Z. https://thecreatorsai.com/p/agents-go-rogue-spacex-burns-18b  Explainer Infographic 


 Now consider the refinancing wall. Debt borrowed at 6% to 8% that comes due in a 16% market is self-defeating; every rollover makes the hole deeper, not shallower. Anthropic faces a cascading, rolling refinancing challenge across roughly seven years, precisely the window in which its largest capital commitments come due. There is no version of this where the company calmly refinances at friendly terms while growing revenue 40% a year. The math forces a decision: raise equity at whatever valuation the market grants, sell the company, or default. I'm betting Anthropic eventually gets acquired by a tech company with even deeper pockets.Government Contracts Won't Rescue Anthropic -- And Users Are Already Leaving The standard bull case is that military and government contracts will provide a reliable revenue floor. I do not buy it. Anthropic's $200 million contract with the Department of Defense broke down after the two parties failed to agree over the degree to which the military could obtain unrestricted access to Anthropic's AI, and when the DoD made a deal with OpenAI instead, it looked like the relationship was over -- though new reporting indicates Amodei resumed negotiations with a Pentagon official [10]. You cannot build a revenue floor on a relationship that has already collapsed once over your own stated red lines. As a commenter on a related piece put it, it is nice for Amodei to say his AI systems should not be used for certain purposes, but how does he imagine enforcing those principles in practice with non-deterministic systems [11]? The Pentagon wants unrestricted access. Anthropic wants restrictions. That's a structural conflict. Give credit where it is due, though. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Opus-level models are genuinely excellent, and the industry widely calls them the best coding models in the world. Capability is not Anthropic's problem. Distribution and economics are. As I have increasingly argued, this is a compute famine being engineered to strip power from ordinary people, and every incentive pushes toward centralizing model access [12]. But that centralization strategy runs straight into cost arbitrage. Companies are turning to cheaper Chinese models for cost and flexibility, including DoorDash, Airbnb, and Siemens [13]. When a $5 model does work comparable to a $200 subscription, the market eventually notices. This is precisely why I run local open-source models instead of renting my cognition from a San Francisco lab. Refusals, unpredictable policy shifts, and the risk of being cut off at any time push both individuals and companies toward models they control. I have been building decentralized AI infrastructure for years precisely because I do not want my access to intelligence to depend on a company that could disappear in a refinancing. The case for running your own models locally, on your own hardware, with open weights you can inspect and control, has never been stronger. You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com.  You can explore uncensored AI yourself at BrightAnswers.ai, where my research engine is trained on health, decentralization, liberty, and reality rather than on the PR priorities of a company with an $800 billion balance sheet.My Call: Google Buys Anthropic, Because AI-Only Companies Cannot Subsidize Themselves State my best guess directly: Google buys Anthropic. Google is the biggest investment partner, the best positioned strategically, and already carries the infrastructure relationships via its cloud and its own TPU line. When I look at who can write the check and absorb the write-downs, Google is at the top of the list by a wide margin. The company also needs a plausible answer to OpenAI's enterprise momentum, and buying the best coding model in the world would solve that problem overnight. I am not giving financial advice, and I have no inside knowledge about any deal talks, but if I were structuring the outcome, this is the transaction I would predict. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. The broader lesson is what I actually care about. Firms with only AI to sell cannot fund AI's cost structure. The capex is too large, the pricing pressure is too intense, and the payback period is too long. Diversified giants like Google and Microsoft can subsidize AI indefinitely out of search, cloud, advertising, and software margins. That is why the standalone AI lab is a structurally unstable business form, and why the long-run equilibrium of this industry is consolidation into a handful of diversified parents. OpenAI is racing toward the same conclusion. I have argued that even OpenAI, with all its name recognition, is the weak link in a chain of loss-making labs and hyperscaler capex [14], and none of that logic changes for Anthropic just because its models are excellent. Here is the tradeoff I do not like: Consolidation into Google makes Anthropic less trustworthy, not more. A standalone lab that answers to its own safety-minded board and its own customer contracts is at least theoretically accountable to the users who depend on it. A division of Google answers to Google, and to the policy priorities of whoever occupies the White House or the European Commission in a given year. That is exactly why decentralization and locally run AI matter now more than ever. If you want to understand why this matters for everything downstream, follow NaturalNews.com, use the uncensored engine at BrightAnswers.ai, and watch the free-speech video platform at BrightVideos.com. Mike Adams (aka the ""Health Ranger"") is the founding editor of NaturalNews.com, a best selling author (#1 best selling science book on Amazon.com called ""Food Forensics""), an environmental scientist, a patent holder for a cesium radioactive isotope elimination invention, a multiple award winner for outstanding journalism, a science news publisher and influential commentator on topics ranging from science and medicine to culture and politics.
Mike Adams also serves as the lab science director of an internationally accredited (ISO 17025) analytical laboratory known as CWC Labs. There, he was awarded a Certificate of Excellence for achieving extremely high accuracy in the analysis of toxic elements in unknown water samples using ICP-MS instrumentation.
In his laboratory research, Adams has made numerous food safety breakthroughs such as revealing rice protein products imported from Asia to be contaminated with toxic heavy metals like lead, cadmium and tungsten. Adams was the first food science researcher to document high levels of tungsten in superfoods. He also discovered over 11 ppm lead in imported mangosteen powder, and led an industry-wide voluntary agreement to limit heavy metals in rice protein products.
Adams has also helped defend the rights of home gardeners and protect the medical freedom rights of parents. Adams is widely recognized to have made a remarkable global impact on issues like GMOs, vaccines, nutrition therapies, human consciousness.

87,2026-11-18,"Newsmax is suing Fox Corp., alleging its recently completed $22 billion purchase of Roku unlawfully consolidates control over America’s rapidly growing free streaming television market.
The antitrust lawsuit was filed Wednesday in the United States District Court for the Northern District of California.  In a shared statement, Newsmax said Fox owning Tubi and The Roku Channel, the nation’s two largest FAST platforms, gives it approximately 71% of the U.S. FAST television market as measured by viewing time.
Newsmax is seeking injunctive relief to stop further integration of the companies, preserve Roku as an independently managed business, and ultimately require Fox to divest either Tubi or The Roku Channel.
Newsmax founder and CEO Christopher Ruddy said in a statement, “This merger represents an extraordinary threat to competition, consumer choice, and the ability of independent news organizations to reach the American public.”
For its part, Fox Corp., in a statement shared with TVNewser, said, “Newsmax’s complaint, which comes after a rigorous review process by regulatory agencies, is wholly without merit. Upon closing, we commenced actively integrating this approved transaction for the benefit of consumers, partners, and other stakeholders.”
Earlier this month, the Rupert Murdoch-owned company completed its purchase, combining Fox Corp.’s broadcast, news, sports, streaming, and local news portfolio with Roku’s open connected TV platform and first-party data from more than 100 million global streaming households.
As part of its agreement with the Department of Justice to take over Roku, Fox Corp. committed to keeping Roku open and neutral for at least three years, including continuing to carry non-Fox content, avoiding search or placement discrimination, providing third-party data products, and maintaining contractual firewalls.
This is not the first time Newsmax has tussled with Fox; in Sept. 2025, a Florida judge dismissed another Newsmax lawsuit against Fox News without prejudice. That lawsuit alleged the Rupert Murdoch-owned network engaged in an extensive, unlawful campaign to block competition in the right-wing pay-TV market.
Fox News responded to the dismissed lawsuit by saying, in a statement to TVNewser: “Newsmax cannot sue its way out of its own competitive failures in the marketplace to chase headlines simply because it can’t attract viewers.”
Mark Mwachiro is a TVNewser contributor who has been writing for the blog since March 2022. Based out of New York, Mark has also contributed to ADWEEK.

88,2026-11-18,"As geopolitical and technological threats multiply, the big question is whether you can hold on to your wealth and access funds when the system fails.
Amid kinetic war, cyberattacks and the prospect of the power grid failing, governments around the world have been encouraging citizens to prepare for major disruption.
In the UK, citizens have been stocking up on tinned food and bottled water in response to the government’s Be Ready campaign.
With fighter jets streaking across the skies of the Middle East, talk of a dirty bomb attack on Washington and the world’s largest software company going offline for a day, the possibility of disaster striking close to home is starting to look more realistic.
But what do you do with your money in times like these? How can you protect your wealth, but still be able to access it when the system fails?
There are two key aspects: choosing assets that maintain value even as the world goes up in flames, and being able to access funds during an emergency.
On the first point Maximilian Kunkel, chief investment officer for ultra-high-net-worth clients at UBS Global Wealth Management, tells Magazine, “We would caution against thinking of any single asset as ‘safe’ in all circumstances.”
Instead, he says wealthy investors should focus on building a portfolio that is “resilient across different scenarios,” with enough liquidity and diversification across asset classes, currencies and geographies.
The corporation’s 2026 global family office report, representing families with an average net worth of $2.7 billion, found 64% cited major geopolitical conflict as a concern over the next 12 months. These investors are not flocking to crypto for wealth preservation however.
Hadley Stern, head of institutions at Silence Laboratories and founder of Fidelity Digital Assets, explains that financial institutions may now place Bitcoin in the same category as gold, but it does not yet enjoy the same reputation as a safe haven.
“Gold has thousands of years of history [...] Bitcoin has less than two decades. But the reasoning people use is the same: a scarce asset that isn’t anyone else’s liability,” he says.
Related: Will gold’s ‘infinite mint bug’ make Bitcoin more valuable?
He advises against using it as a reliable hedge in a crisis; “I’d be careful with the hedge story,” he says. “Bitcoin is still volatile, and in a sell-off it often trades like other risk assets.”
Cash, by contrast, is handy for emergencies “for liquidity and paying bills,” but not for “holding value over long stretches,” he says.
Michael D’Alessio, chief operating officer at digital asset trading platform Talos, tells Magazine, there are early signs that Bitcoin is beginning to behave more like gold. He said that through the volatility around the US midterm elections Bitcoin’s correlation with equities remained high. However, over the same period, its correlation with gold also rose sharply, to around 0.75 on a 30-day basis as of early August:
BTC vs gold, 30-day rolling correlation of daily returns. Source: Talos/Coin Metrics
The collapse of Mt Gox, or more recently the failure of Paraguay’s Ueno Bank show that “the asset is only as safe as the system holding it,” according to Stern.
Rather than automatically moving into Bitcoin or gold when tensions rise, risk committees are asking different questions:
“Where is the asset? Which jurisdiction is it in? Who can freeze it? What happens if a counterparty or a payment rail is down for a week?” he says. “Those used to be back-office questions. Now they come up at the board level.”
Kunkel said that concern over major geopolitical conflict is encouraging wealthy families to consider “not only asset allocation, but also where assets are held, currency exposure, liquidity and jurisdictional diversification.”
The UBS report found that 88% of family offices hold bankable assets in two or more jurisdictions, while 30% are increasing or considering increasing currency diversification.
Related: Half of UK wealth advisers say clients’ crypto is ‘invisible’ to them: CoinShares survey
Fortunately the banks and custody providers are making practical disaster preparations too.
“Regulators expect firms to plan for severe but plausible events,” Stern explains, such as a cloud outage, a cyberattack, a key vendor failing or a market closed for days, and “show they can keep their most important services running through them.”
The UK government is advising citizens to stock up on supplies. Source: Gov.uk
For custody providers, that means testing whether assets can still be moved if a signing location goes offline, and how quickly operations can recover if a data center is lost. Stern says while households might prep for an emergency by stocking a full pantry:
Similarly investors need to work out which assets you can reach quickly and which you can’t, and “keeping enough liquidity to get through a bad stretch without being forced to sell.”
Kunkel says capital preservation “does not mean eliminating volatility, but maintaining enough liquidity and diversification to withstand volatility” without becoming a forced seller.
When the balloon goes up, diversification can protect a portfolio from a market shock, but it can’t guarantee access to funds if the infrastructure supporting it fails. A safe haven isn’t just about owning the right things; it’s about being able to access them when the system stops working.

                Indian arrivals to Vietnam are growing more than twice as fast as the overall market, and Vietnamese carriers are chasing that demand into cities like Ahmedabad. Seats alone won’t lock it in, though. Vietnam still needs the food, guides, and wedding and events products Indian travelers expect.
            
Vietnam’s airlines are adding flights to India as arrivals from one of the country’s fastest-growing source markets show no sign of slowing.
Sun PhuQuoc Airways will launch Ahmedabad-Phu Quoc flights on Dec. 3, and Vietnam Airlines will take its Delhi and Bengaluru services to daily from Dec. 14. That comes weeks after Vietnam Airlines opened its own Ahmedabad route.
Low-cost carrier VietJet today runs the most extensive network between the two countries, connecting Hanoi, Ho Chi Minh City and Da Nang with six Indian cities.
Vietnam welcomed 746,000 Indian visitors in 2025, making India its sixth-largest international source market. Arrivals rose from 138,000 in 2022 to 507,000 in 2024. In the first nine months of 2026, the country received 670,972 Indian visitors, according to Vietnam’s National Statistics Office.
Indian arrivals grew 33% year over year during the period, more than twice the already strong 14.5% growth Vietnam saw in o

Vietnam's carriers are adding capacity to India as Indian arrivals keep climbing. Sun PhuQuoc Airways launches Ahmedabad–Phu Quoc on Dec. 3 with five weekly flights, going daily from early February 2027, and Vietnam Airlines takes its Delhi–Hanoi and Bengaluru–Hanoi services to daily from Dec. 14, weeks after opening Ahmedabad–Hanoi on Nov. 3. VietJet currently operates the most extensive network between the two countries, linking Hanoi, Ho Chi Minh City, and Da Nang with six Indian cities. Vietnam received 746,000 Indian visitors in 2025, its sixth-largest source market, up from 138,000 in 2022. In the first nine months of 2026, Indian arrivals reached 670,972 and grew 33% year over year, more than twice Vietnam's overall international arrivals growth of 14.5%. Booking.com data shared with Skift ranked Vietnam among the top three most-searched international destinations for Indian travelers this year, and Ixigo said bookings to Vietnam have doubled year on year. Demand extends beyond leisure into weddings, corporate travel, and events, with provincial authorities in Khanh Hoa and Quang Ninh now building India into their tourism plans. Vietnam's tourism industry has called for more India-focused products, including wedding packages, Indian food options at hotels, culturally familiar guides, and Hindi-language marketing."

A very small international airline plans to add long haul, international flights to the United States as of next spring, and I feel pretty confident in saying that 99% of people have never heard of this airline (I had to look it up after seeing one of their planes at an airport several weeks back, and I know a thing or two about identifying airplanes). 😉
In this post:
As reported by AeroRoutes, as of May 1, 2027, Parata Air will launch daily nonstop flights between Seoul Incheon (ICN) and Los Angeles (LAX). The flight will operate with the following schedule:
WE301 Incheon to Los Angeles departing 12:00PM arriving 7:20AMWE302 Los Angeles to Incheon departing 9:50AM arriving 3:20PM (+1 days)
Parata Air will operate the route with an Airbus A330-200, featuring 260 seats, including 18 business class seats and 242 economy seats. The 5,944-mile flight is blocked at 11hr40min eastbound and 13hr30min westbound.
Tickets on the flight are now on sale, and I see fares starting at around $670 one-way in economy, and around $2,500 one-way in business class. Those aren’t exactly terribly attractive fares for a newcomer that appears to have a pretty lackluster onboard product.
For those not familiar with Parata Air, the airline launched operations back in 2019, but it has a fleet of just a handful of planes, including three Airbus A330s and two Airbus A320s. This will be the carrier’s first service outside of Asia. As I hinted at in the headline, I imagine this is a fully unknown player to most people, and I had never heard of the airline until a few weeks ago.
Random thought — can anyone think of a smaller international airline (in terms of fleet size) that added long haul flights to the United States? I know that’s very specific, but I can’t think of another one… it’s possible I’m missing something, though!
The airline acquired its three A330s secondhand from different sources, with the planes having flown for Garuda Indonesia, Hainan Airlines, and Thomas Cook (though that plane doesn’t have business class).
The Seoul Incheon to Los Angeles market is pretty well served in terms of capacity, but not so well served in terms of competition. That’s because no US carrier flies between Los Angeles and Seoul Incheon, and with Korean Air having acquired Asiana, there’s now only one South Korean flag carrier.
If you look at the market, you’ll see that Korean Air operates up to four daily flights, and Air Premia operates up to two daily flights. Now we’ll see a third airline from the country operating in the market, though with the lowest capacity, and arguably the worst product.
It’ll be interesting to see when we see some competition here on the US side. Delta might operate the route eventually, but that doesn’t actually increase competition, since the airline has a joint venture with Korean Air. American has (smartly, probably) given up on Los Angeles as a long haul hub. So that leaves United, and I could definitely see the airline eventually adding service in the market, as a competitive response against Delta and Korean Air, if nothing else.
As of May 2027, Parata Air will be launching a new nonstop route between Seoul Incheon and Los Angeles, representing the carrier’s first service outside of Asia. The airline will be operating daily in the market with Airbus A330s. With this, there will be three South Korean carriers flying this route, though no airlines from the United States.
What do you make of this new Parata Air service?

FILE PHOTO: Anthropic's logo is shown in this illustration created on November 10, 2026. REUTERS/Dado Ruvic/Illustration/File Photo
NEW YORK, Nov 18 : Options on Anthropic shares could begin trading as soon as Monday, giving investors a new way to wager on the AI company's prospects as market participants expect heavy interest.
Cboe Global Markets expects to list the contracts two business days after the stock begins trading, pending industry readiness. “The Anthropic IPO could be yet another catalyst, bringing a new wave of investors and trading activity to the options market,"" said Rob Hocking, Cboe’s global head of derivatives. ""It’s great to see investor education driving this growth, as more people learn how options let them define their risk. An exciting trend and one we expect to continue.” 
The Claude developer is expected to price its IPO later on Wednesday, and the shares will begin trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday.  
""We expect significant interest in Anthropic options once they list,"" said Garrett DeSimone, head quant at OptionMetrics. ""The combination of AI-driven enthusiasm and the lottery-like payoff profile typical of IPOs are likely to draw investors immediately. As one of the most concentrated pure-play exposures to AI, the stock is also likely to be highly volatile.""

Interest in Anthropic options could exceed the interest in SpaceX options earlier this year, said Ophir Gottlieb, chief executive of Capital Market Laboratories, citing oversubscription that is pushing the company's valuation higher and could invite various positioning ahead of the deal, as well as the market backdrop.""The AI trade is melting down,"" Gottlieb said. ""Many feel it is overdone, while many others feel it's the popping of a bubble. Whenever there is such opposing views in a heated market, we get a lot of option positioning.""Options give holders the right, but not the obligation, to buy or sell shares at a set price within a certain period. They offer investors a cheaper way to gain exposure to a stock and to express their views on both short-term price moves and longer-term positioning.Anthropic’s $64 billion IPO has drawn stronger early institutional demand than the offering of SpaceX, with the book six times oversubscribed last week, Reuters previously reported.The IPO has drawn anticipation from investors, with Wall Street rolling out products tied to the stock, hosting client events and giving away merchandise ahead of the AI company's potentially record launch.
Interest in Anthropic options could exceed the interest in SpaceX options earlier this year, said Ophir Gottlieb, chief executive of Capital Market Laboratories, citing oversubscription that is pushing the company's valuation higher and could invite various positioning ahead of the deal, as well as the market backdrop.
""The AI trade is melting down,"" Gottlieb said. ""Many feel it is overdone, while many others feel it's the popping of a bubble. Whenever there is such opposing views in a heated market, we get a lot of option positioning.""
Options give holders the right, but not the obligation, to buy or sell shares at a set price within a certain period. They offer investors a cheaper way to gain exposure to a stock and to express their views on both short-term price moves and longer-term positioning.
Anthropic’s $64 billion IPO has drawn stronger early institutional demand than the offering of SpaceX, with the book six times oversubscribed last week, Reuters previously reported.
The IPO has drawn anticipation from investors, with Wall Street rolling out products tied to the stock, hosting client events and giving away merchandise ahead of the AI company's potentially record launch.

93,2026-11-18,"Since 2006, the 992 silhouette has been an undisputed pinnacle of heritage footwear. While the 99X family continue to evolve, the 992 remains largely unchanged, for good reason. Refreshing the archival shape through deconstruction, the New Balance 992 Made in USA Black/Turtledove is one to bookmark.
Part of the acclaimed Made in USA collection under the creative direction of Teddy Santis, this pair highlights an industry truth: few footwear brands are putting out mainline colorways as masterfully executed as New Balance. 
With the elevated construction offered by its Made in UK and Made in USA arms, New Balance has the ability to hit the market with a different level of design. Combine that premium finish with colorways curated by the likes of Santis, and you’ve got regular seasonal rollouts hitting the same standards as sought-after limited-edition drops. 
For proof, look no further than the New Balance 992 Made in USA Black/Turtledove. This iteration revolves around a rich black foundation set off by exposed Turtledove and Afterglow leather edges that create an inside-out, deconstructed aesthetic.
As can be expected of the Made in USA range, the leather is supple and hard-wearing, adding to the texture of the shoe as well as revealing the raw, undyed edges: a detail that won’t go unappreciated by the leather purists. 
The shoe is, of course, built on an ABZORB and ENCAP cushioned midsole with a durable NDurance rubber outsole for classic New Balance comfort. But comfort aside, this shoe is all about the simple colorway.

94,2026-11-18,"This 1969 Oldsmobile 442 Sports Coupe is one of a reported 189 examples produced for the Canadian market for the model year, and it was acquired by the current owner in 2000. Metal work was completed before the body was refinished in Astral Blue with white stripes circa 2021, and other work completed during current ownership included overhauling the 400ci Rocket V8, TH400 automatic transmission, and rear axle, installing a Vintage Air unit, and replacing the windshield, front brake calipers, and more as detailed below. The car also has body-color 14″ Super Stock II wheels, power steering, a black vinyl front bench seat, and an AM radio. This 442 is now offered with GM of Canada documentation, a Protect-O-Plate, manufacturer’s literature, refurbishment records, removed parts, and British Columbia registration in the seller’s name.

Assembled at the Oshawa, Ontario, plant, the car left the factory finished in Crimson (52) with Topaz stripes (65). The seller notes that the body was separated from the chassis during the refurbishment work completed in 2021, and it was stripped and refinished in Astral Blue with white hood stripes. Reported sheetmetal work performed prior to the repaint consisted of replacing the floor pans, trunk floor, outer wheel housings, left quarter skin, right inner wheelwell, and fender liners, along with fabrication on the rear wheel wells, rear quarters, trunk, and rear window trough. The bumpers were repaired and re-chromed in July 2020, and replacement door hinges and windshield glass were installed in October 2021.

Body-color 14″ Super Stock II wheels are mounted with Firestone Wide Oval white-letter tires. The car is equipped with power steering, and braking is handled by power-assisted front discs and rear drums. Suspension and braking service noted in the records in the gallery include installing a replacement master cylinder and brake lines circa 2021, replacing the front brake calipers and sway bar bushings in 2021, and reshaping the rear coil springs with replacement insulators in October 2025.

The front and rear bench seats are upholstered in black vinyl, and the seats received replacement foam and covers during the refurbishment. The carpeting, insulation, door panel trim, and headliner were also replaced. A Vintage Air climate control system with under-dash vents was installed in 2022.

The N34-option three-spoke steering wheel fronts a 120-mph speedometer, a rebuilt analog clock, and a fuel gauge. The speedometer was rebuilt and calibrated in March 2025 to correct a “jumpy” needle. The five-digit odometer shows 84k miles, approximately 21k of which have been added under current ownership.

The 400ci Rocket V8 was factory-rated at 325 horsepower and is topped by a red air cleaner assembly. The engine was disassembled in 2020, per the seller, and other work completed at that time consisted of installing replacement gaskets and seals along with the distributor cap, spark plug wires, fuel pump, and fuel lines. The carburetor was rebuilt in 2022, and the timing cover seal, harmonic damper, motor mounts, radiator, water pump, and PCV valve were replaced in October 2025.

The engine stamp is shown above, and the numeric sequence aligns with the last eight digits of the VIN.

Power is routed to the rear wheels through a three-speed TH400 automatic transmission and a Chevrolet 12-bolt rear end, the latter of which was were specified from the factory for Canadian-built Oldsmobile 442s. The transmission and rear end were rebuilt in 2022, and subsequent service included replacing the transmission rear seal, output seal, speedometer seals, and front U-joint in October 2025, followed by installing a replacement transmission pan and extended pickup tube in March 2026.

The transmission and rear axle identification stamps are provided in the gallery.

Decoding the data plate reveals the following production information:

Correspondence from Vintage Vehicle Services indicates that the vehicle was built on June 9, 1969, and optioned with a front bench seat, custom two-tone paint, and a Custom Sport steering wheel, among other items.

Items accompanying the car include a removed windshield, instrumentation, a transmission pan, and other parts detailed in the gallery.
The car is being sold on its British Columbia registration, which serves as an ownership document in British Columbia.

Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8
Samsung's One UI offers plenty of customization options right out of the gate, and Good Lock pushes customization even further. Today, we'll briefly look at an example of how you can use Good Lock to add a bit more personal flavor to stock One UI.

By default, Samsung allows Galaxy phone users to customize virtually every aspect of the lock screen, except one: the padlock icon at the top. Everything else is customizable under standard One UI rules.

To customize that final piece of the lock screen to your heart's content, you can use the LockStar Good Lock module. With it, you can change the padlock icon and color. You can even hide it completely. Here's how.

You don't have to worry about installing third-party apps for this task. Good Lock is a Samsung initiative, and while the platform used to be region-locked, it hasn't been for the past year and a half.

You can download Good Lock on your Galaxy phone via the Galaxy Store or Google Play Store by hitting the buttons below.

Once you have Good Lock on your device, open the app, search for LockStar in the list of modules, and install it. Then proceed to the next steps below.

With Good Lock and the LockStar module installed on your Galaxy phone, you're just a few steps away from customizing the final lock screen item. Do this:

From that window, you can select one of five preset padlock icon sets, change the color of the padlock icon, create your own padlock icons using photos from the Gallery, or hide it entirely.

In other words, you don't even have to open the Good Lock or LockStar apps to change the padlock icon. You only need to install them on your device to enable extra options. Then, you can change the icon through One UI's standard lock screen customization layer. It's that simple.

Vlad 
18 November 2026


Apple iOS Rumors

Apple unveiled the iPhone 18 Pro, iPhone 18 Pro Max, and iPhone Duo in September, while the standard iPhone 18 and the iPhone Air 2 are expected to follow next spring. Now, a new report out of South Korea tells us when the displays for those two will enter the mass production phase.
According to some unnamed industry sources, LG Display will start mass production of OLED panels for the new iPhones late this year, while Samsung Display will begin in January. ""Panel makers are prioritizing production for the higher-value Pro, Pro Max and iPhone Duo models through the end of the year,"" one of the sources said, adding that the standard and Air panels ""require lower volumes and are less complex to manufacture.""
The two display makers are reportedly aligning their production plans with an expected March launch. That matches what a tipster on Weibo said last month, when he claimed the iPhone 18 will launch in March alongside the iPhone 18e.
Both Samsung and LG will supply panels for the iPhone 18, but Samsung Display will allegedly be the only supplier for the Air 2. That's because OLED orders for the latter are expected to be limited, since the original iPhone Air got a ""lukewarm market response"". Apple has reportedly left the Air 2's display specs unchanged so it can make the fullest possible use of its existing panel inventory, which means new orders should be minimal.
The report also says the standard and Air models typically account for more than 30% of total iPhone sales, with the standard model outselling the Air. Strong demand for the iPhone 17 apparently helped drive the previous lineup's success, so it's not hard to see why Samsung and LG both want a piece of the iPhone 18 pie.
Source
If Apple's aim is to use the unsold inventory of the current iPhone Air's displays, does that mean the Air 2 will not have the smaller dynamic island seen on the 18 Pros?

97,2026-11-18,"The Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) is set to roll out a net settlement framework for mutual funds starting April 2027. This innovative approach will allow for transactions to be settled on a net basis, significantly enhancing liquidity management. By aligning purchase and sale transactions, mutual funds can reduce cash requirements. While immediate gains for investors might not be evident, potential cost efficiencies could eventually benefit them.

98,2026-11-18,"Warframe, Sea of Stars, and Deep Rock Galactic: Survivor also picked up awards
Gryphline's Arknights: Endfield has been named Google Play's best game of 2026, taking the top prize in every market except Japan and Korea.
Google announced the winners on November 17 in a blog post from Sam Bright, VP and general manager of Google Play and its developer ecosystem. Hololive Dreams won best game in Japan, while Nexon's MapleStory: Idle RPG won in Korea.
Bright said this year's picks reflected a shift toward games that span phones, tablets, and PCs, and highlighted Arknights: Endfield's Sidekick integration and its cross-device play through Google Play Games on PC.
XSeed Games' farming sim Moonlight Peaks won best cross-device game in the main list. Perfect World's Neverness to Everness took the same award in many Asian markets.
Digital Extremes' Warframe was named best multiplayer game and Sybo's Subway Surfers City won best pick up and play. Deep Rock Galactic: Survivor, published by Ghost Ship Publishing, won best indie, and Sea of Stars, published by Playdigious on mobile, won best story. Hoyoverse's Zenless Zone Zero won best ongoing game in the Americas and Europe.
Outside games, note-taking app Notability was named best app, and Strava won best cross-device app. Google's Asia Pacific list also included regional awards, with Blast+ Games' Scarfall 2.0 named best made in India and Own Games' Tukang Sate VS Ojol Rush best made in Indonesia.
Arknights: Endfield is also a finalist for iPad Game of the Year in Apple's App Store Awards 2026, revealed earlier today, and is nominated for best mobile game at The Game Awards alongside Monster Hunter Outlanders, Neverness to Everness, Pokémon Champions, and Where Winds Meet. Winners will be revealed on December 10.
Last month, Google Play added a batch of new discovery and social features for games, including a personalized Game Finder, timed competitive Sprints, and clip-sharing via its Sidekick overlay.
